--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This is the title page for an influential alchemical collection featuring the works of "Geber," edited and annotated by the German physician Caspar Horn and published by Georg Horn.</meta>

## OF GEBER THE ARAB <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), a foundational figure in both Islamic and Western alchemy.</note>
# C H E M I S T R Y <note>The Latin "Chimia" at this time (17th century) referred to the unified study of chemistry and alchemy.</note>
->*Or*<-
### The Transmission of the Highest Perfection
### A N D
### The Investigation of the Mastery <note>The "Mastery" (Magisterium) refers to the successful completion of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> corrected in numerous places,
->by<-
## CASPAR HORN <note>Caspar Horn (1583–1653) was a prominent physician and scholar in Nuremberg.</note>
### Physician of the Republic of Nuremberg.
->*To which is added, by the same author*<-
## THE MARROW <note>In this context, "Marrow" (Medulla) signifies the essence or the core principles of a subject.</note>
### of Alchemy
## OF GEBER.
->*All Edited*<-
->by<-
## GEORG HORN.

<summary>This title page introduces a corrected edition of the "Summa Perfectionis," one of the most important texts in the Western alchemical tradition, attributed to the Arabic philosopher Geber. It also includes a supplemental "Marrow of Alchemy" compiled by the physician Caspar Horn.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Jabir ibn Hayyan, Alchemy, Chemistry, Caspar Horn, Nuremberg, Summa Perfectionis, Magisterium, 17th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the dissertation by Georgius Hornius (Georg Horn). Sigla: * 3. Catchword: rimen- (for experimentum).</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical arrangements of leaves, flowers, and scrolled flourishes.</image-desc>

-># GEORGE HORN’S<-
->## Dissertation on<-
-># ALCHEMY<-
->### *And its Truth.*<-

It is an ancient question what one should think of <term>Alchemy</term> <note>A philosophical and protoscientific tradition aimed at the purification and perfection of objects, most famously through the transformation of base metals into gold.</note>, which has been so widely celebrated for many centuries past because of the art of <term>transmuting metals</term> <note>original: "transmutandi metalla"; the chemical process of changing one element into another.</note>. Most people regard it as a vain and deceptive fiction created by certain impostors, or by the simple-minded and the ignorant. I will not now gather together the reasons with which they attack it, nor is it worth the effort to recount the arguments of its defenders, as this has already been done by others long ago and at great length. Instead, I shall casually and briefly collect a few examples from history, as much as is sufficient for the matter.

The most recent experiment of this art—

<summary>This introductory page sets the stage for Georg Horn’s historical inquiry into alchemy, acknowledging the long-standing debate between those who believe in the transmutation of metals and those who dismiss it as a fraud.</summary>
<keywords>Georg Horn, alchemy, transmutation of metals, history of science, philosophical dissertation, gold-making</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page, completing the word "experimentum" (experiment). It discusses a famous 17th-century alchemical event involving Johann Friedrich Helvetius.</meta>

experiment, about which there can be no doubt at all, was performed in this very year of 1667 at The Hague. A certain unknown man, a Dutchman by speech and dress, came to John Frederick Helvetius, Doctor of Medicine, and after much conversation, handed him a small particle of a <term>tincture</term> <note>original: "tincturae"; in alchemy, this refers to a concentrated substance or "medicine" (often a form of the Philosopher's Stone) capable of transforming base metals into noble ones.</note> about the size of a grain of millet, and ordered it to be immersed in lead. Once this was done, a half-ounce of lead was entirely transformed, without any loss of mass, into the purest gold. This gold was examined by all the official tests of the mint-masters; not only did it not fail the trial, but furthermore, during <term>quartation</term> <note>original: "quartatione"; a metallurgical process used to refine gold by alloying it with silver and then separating them with nitric acid.</note>—as they call it—it actually increased in weight by two <term>Anglici</term> <note>original: "Anglicorum"; a historical unit of weight used by goldsmiths and money-changers in the Netherlands, equivalent to about 0.8 grams.</note>. 

This event became known to many trustworthy people throughout all of The Hague. Thus, proof was given to all those who were previously skeptical that the <term>transmutation of metals</term> <note>original: "transmutationem metallorum"</note> is a reality and that it is known even in this, our own time. A narrative of this entire marvelous work has been published to the world. There are also other examples that could be given, but I have chosen to rest my case on this one alone, because it is more familiar to me and

<summary>Georg Horn recounts a famous 1667 alchemical success story in The Hague, where a mysterious stranger provided Johann Friedrich Helvetius with a substance that turned lead into gold. This contemporary account is used as definitive proof that the transmutation of metals is possible.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Friedrich Helvetius, The Hague, transmutation, lead to gold, tincture, quartation, 1667, alchemy evidence</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of documented alchemical transmutations, following the 1667 experiment in The Hague. It connects those events to the earlier testimony of the famous chemist Jan Baptist van Helmont and the legendary wealth of Nicolas Flamel.</meta>

* 4

is more certain than the others and is fresh in the memories of many. By this most recent experiment, credit was given to <term>Helmont</term> <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering Flemish chemist and physician who claimed to have performed transmutation using a small piece of the Philosopher's Stone given to him by a stranger.</note> when he asserted that with a single grain of the <term>tincture</term> <note>original: "tincturae"; in alchemy, a "tincture" is a potent medicinal or transformative substance—essentially a prepared form of the Philosopher's Stone—capable of "dying" or changing the nature of base metals.</note>, he had transmuted a large quantity of Mercury into pure, unadulterated gold. I will say nothing for now of other events that are recorded but are more distant from our own time, and which might therefore be called into doubt by the skeptical.

Yet I do not wish to pass over one particular case. It is a settled opinion among Alchemists that <term>Nicolas Flamel</term> <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe. Long after his death, legends arose claiming he had discovered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone through an ancient book of Jewish wisdom.</note> forged vast wealth for himself through this art and was a true possessor of the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>original: "lapidis Philosophici"; the legendary substance sought by alchemists for its ability to turn lead into gold and grant long life.</note>. I shall report what <term>Naudé</term> <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a prominent French scholar and librarian. He was often skeptical of the supernatural, seeking rational explanations for "magical" reputations.</note> noted about him in his manuscript *Italian Observations*. He writes as follows:

Nicolaus Flamel was a Parisian scribe, who managed the business affairs of the Jews in Paris around the year 1393 and those following. And because he had acquired great riches in a short time, some suspected that he had discovered the Philosopher’s Stone. From this, the Alchemists...

<summary>The author cites Jan Baptist van Helmont’s successful transmutation of mercury into gold as a well-remembered proof of alchemy, then introduces the case of Nicolas Flamel, using the historian Gabriel Naudé to explain Flamel's reputation for wealth.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Baptist van Helmont, Nicolas Flamel, Gabriel Naudé, Alchemy, Transmutation, Philosopher's Stone, Mercury, Gold, Paris, 14th century, 17th century</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the debunking of Nicolas Flamel’s alchemical reputation, transitioning from the previous page’s mention of Gabriel Naudé’s skepticism. It shifts from the legend of the Philosopher’s Stone to a historical, more cynical explanation for Flamel’s wealth involving the expulsion of Jews from France.</meta>

and they count **Flamel** among their Patriarchs, alongside <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the origins of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy are attributed.</note> and <term>Lullius</term> <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher. Although he likely did not practice alchemy, many later alchemical texts were falsely attributed to him.</note>. But this is a mere deception and the unhealthy delusion of overly credulous men—a fiction regarding the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: "lapidis Philosophici"</note> pursued by those with an insane desire for wealth. 

However, the truth regarding Flamel is as follows, which will reveal the source of this entire mad delusion. **Nicolas Flamel** was, as has been said, a scribe who managed the legal and financial affairs of the <term>Jews</term> <note>In 14th-century Paris, Flamel’s historical profession as a manuscript seller and scrivener brought him into contact with various legal documents and financial records.</note>. At that time, the Jews had been banished from all of **France** by the King, and their property was confiscated. 

Flamel secretly negotiated with the Jews' debtors (for he knew exactly how much each person owed them and could have reported these debts to the King, revealing the full extent of what was owed to the Crown). Instead, he offered to either completely suppress their names and debts or significantly reduce the recorded amounts in exchange for a fee. By this "art"—not the Alchemical art, as the foolish <term>seekers of the Stone</term> <note>original: "lapidistæ"; a derogatory term for those obsessed with the Philosopher's Stone.</note> believe, but the art of deception—Flamel amassed vast wealth from the property of the Jews in a very short time. From these riches, he built...

<summary>This page argues that Nicolas Flamel’s legendary alchemical wealth was actually acquired through financial corruption and the exploitation of confiscated Jewish debts following their expulsion from France.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolas Flamel, Hermes Trismegistus, Ramon Llull, Philosopher's Stone, Alchemical history, Jewish expulsion from France, financial corruption, Gabriel Naudé</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 4, which proposed that Nicolas Flamel’s wealth came from financial opportunism during the expulsion of Jews rather than alchemy. It cites historical evidence from Gabriel Naudé and suggests alternative figures who might better fit the legend of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

as the religious custom of those times dictated, he established several churches—such as **St. Genevieve of the Ardents** and the **Cemetery of the Holy Innocents**, where he is buried—which he did for the sake of expiating his guilt <note>original: "culpæ expiandæ ergo"; the author suggests Flamel built these monuments to atone for his questionable financial dealings.</note>. Flamel was certainly a scribe. I saw in Rome, in the library of **Cardinal Bagni**, a copy of the *Romance of the Rose* <note>original: "fabulam de la Rosa"; a famous medieval French poem.</note>—the authors of which are **Jean de Meun** and **Clopinel**—written in Flamel’s own hand. Thus says **Naudé** <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a French scholar and librarian to Cardinal Mazarin, known for his skeptical approach to history and magic.</note>. If these things are indeed true, then Flamel must henceforth be removed from the list of those who possessed the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note>, and another must be substituted in his place.

Such a replacement might be **Yusuf Bulhagix** <note>Yusuf I (1318–1354), the Nasrid Sultan of Granada.</note>, King of **Granada**, who reigned around the year 1346 in **Baetica** <note>The Roman name for the region of Andalusia in southern Spain.</note> and enclosed the **Albaicín** <note>A historic district of Granada known for its Moorish architecture.</note> with a wall. This was done at such immense expense that, according to rumor, he must have transmuted metals through the Alchemical art, as the kingdom's own resources were insufficient; this is recorded by **Mariana** in book 25, chapter 16, of *The History of Spanish Matters* <note>Juan de Mariana (1536–1624), a Jesuit historian.</note>. **Master Johannes of Cologne** writes that since the year 1480, there were no more than three true <term>artists</term> <note>original: "artistas"; in this context, it refers to "adepts" or practitioners who successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note> in **Germany**, all of whom had passed away by the year 1520.

<summary>This page concludes the debunking of Nicolas Flamel by citing Gabriel Naudé's evidence of Flamel’s work as a scribe and suggests King Yusuf I of Granada as a more likely candidate for alchemical wealth. It ends with a note on the rarity of true alchemists in Germany during the late 15th and early 16th centuries.</summary>

<keywords>Nicolas Flamel, Gabriel Naudé, Philosopher's Stone, Jean de Meun, Yusuf I of Granada, Albaicín, Alchemical history, Juan de Mariana, Johannes of Cologne</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of individuals who supposedly possessed the Philosopher's Stone, following the mention of the King of Granada. It provides three specific historical examples of men in Germany who were said to have performed successful transmutations.</meta>

...ded by those who chronicled such wonders.

There was one man at the **University of Erfurt**, Master *Burckhard*, born in Kreuzberg, four miles from Giessen. He was a man highly skilled in the art, and he secretly possessed the true elixir for the red and the white <note>In alchemy, the "white elixir" was used to transmute metals into silver, while the "red elixir" was the final stage used to create gold.</note>. 

The second was named *Georgius Krapitt*, a member of the household of the Bishop of **Trier**. With only a single half-ounce of tincture, he transmuted 300 ounces of Mercury into good silver <note>original: "bonam Lunam"; literally "good Moon," the alchemical symbol and name for silver.</note>. 

The third was *Ludovicus à Neus* of **Silesia**, who lived in **Marburg** with *Johann von Dorrenberg*, the Master of the Court for **Landgrave William**. With a single ounce of his substance, he transmuted 16 ounces of quicksilver <term>quicksilver: a common historical name for liquid mercury</term> into true 24-carat gold, which withstood every test of purity. This man was miserably murdered in prison by that same *Dorrenberg* and was buried in Marburg. 

And this, I believe, is the reason why—although there are many who possess this knowledge—none have thus far revealed themselves to ungrateful, greedy, and cruel tyrants <note>original: "Mezentiis"; a reference to Mezentius, a mythical Etruscan king known for his extreme cruelty and contempt for others.</note>. Instead, they remain mindful of that old saying:

> He who has lived well-hidden, has lived well <note>original: "Bene qui latuit bene vixit"; a famous maxim by the Roman poet Ovid, often used by alchemists to justify their secrecy.</note>.

<summary>The author lists three German figures—Burckhard, Georgius Krapitt, and Ludovicus à Neus—as true possessors of the Philosopher's Stone, citing their successful transmutations. He uses the tragic murder of Ludovicus to explain why alchemists choose to remain in hiding rather than reveal their secrets to powerful rulers.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, transmutation, Philosopher's Stone, silver, gold, mercury, University of Erfurt, Marburg, Master Burckhard, Georgius Krapitt, Ludovicus à Neus, secrecy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the secrecy of true alchemists, contrasting them with the many frauds who prey on the nobility. It transitions from the dangers of revealing the art to the prevalence of "sophists" or fake alchemists in European cities.</meta>

* 6

...conceal their art and even omit it entirely, becoming useful neither to themselves nor to others.

Among those who possess the true knowledge and practice of **Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymiæ"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were not yet distinct disciplines</note>, there wanders a countless multitude of Sophists, impostors, deceivers, and **ash-blowers** <term>ciniflones: literally "hair-curlers," but used here as a derogatory term for fraudulent alchemists who wasted time and fuel at the bellows without results</term>, so much so that **Paris** <note>original: "Lutetia Parisiorum"</note> alone contains many thousands of such characters. Thus, the **Duke of Florence** was swindled by a certain **Daniel of Transylvania** with his *Usisur* <note>likely a reference to a fraudulent alchemical preparation or a specific "secret" name</note>; the **Duke of Württemberg** by **Johann Heinrich Mullenfels** and **Georg Hanauer**; and the **Duke of Bavaria** by **Gabriel Marvisio**; while many others have been circumvented by similar frauds.

Those magnificent promises of the **Philosopher's Stone** have stirred countless individuals to an untiring search for it, because whoever possesses it is able—according to **Palingenius** <note>Marcellus Palingenius Stellatus, author of the "Zodiacus Vitae," a popular didactic poem</note>:

> ...to live decently wherever he wishes,
> *And fears neither the wrath of Fortune nor the hands of thieves.*

Indeed, such is its power—to use the poetic exaggeration <note>original: "hyperbologia"</note> of **Augurellus** <note>Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, an Italian poet known for his work *Chrysopoeia*, which taught the art of making gold through verse</note>—

<meta>catchword: His own (Ipsius)</meta>

<summary>The author warns that for every true alchemist, there are thousands of "ash-blowing" impostors who have famously defrauded European dukes. He cites the poetic promises of the Philosopher's Stone as the primary motivation for this widespread and often dangerous obsession.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, impostors, Paris, Duke of Florence, Duke of Württemberg, Duke of Bavaria, Philosopher's Stone, Palingenius, Augurellus, ciniflones</keywords>

And it is certain that the primary origin and practice of this science <note>referring to chemistry or alchemy</note> is to be sought from **Egypt**, and that **Moses** was learned in it, as **Stephen** <note>referring to the speech of Saint Stephen in the Book of Acts 7:22: "Moses was educated in all the wisdom of the Egyptians."</note> testifies and the matter itself speaks, when he burned the **golden calf** with fire and presented the ashes, soaked in water, to the people of **Israel** to drink. To burn gold with fire belongs to none other than a **Chemist** <note>original: *Chimici*</note>; for gold is indeed incombustible by common fire. There was, therefore, another fire with which Moses burned his gold, and which the Egyptians used in destroying gold and preparing it for a **tincture** <term>tinctura: a potent alchemical substance capable of transmuting metals or healing the body</term>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of materials used in chemical work, transitioning from the list of "absurd things" on the previous page to the core principle that all substances containing the three prime elements are useful, while emphasizing that Chemistry's influence extends far beyond the mere transmutation of metals.</meta>

more remote, can be adapted. For whatever possesses sulfur, mercury, and salt <term>Tria Prima: the three "prime" substances—sulfur (soul/flammability), mercury (spirit/fluidity), and salt (body/solidity)—that alchemists believed made up all matter</term>—whether it be vegetable, animal, or metallic—has its use in Chemistry <term>Chimia: the early modern study of matter, encompassing both practical metallurgy and the spiritual goals of alchemy</term>: although the material for metallic transmutations is sought most closely from the metals themselves. As the seed is, so is the harvest; the fruit reveals the tree. Therefore, he who seeks gold will find no material closer than gold:

->*In gold are the seeds of gold.*<-
<note>original: "In auro semina sunt auri."</note>

And so the matter usually stands with the other metals.

Nor should it be thought that Chemistry is primarily concerned with metals: that is the smallest province of its kingdom; its limits and boundaries extend far and wide throughout all of nature. No art, not even the mechanical arts, can do without it. Those three things that changed the face of the whole world—*the nautical needle or compass, the art of artillery* <note>original: *ars tormentaria*; referring to the invention of gunpowder and cannons</note>, *and Typography* <note>the printing press</note>—flowed from no other source than Chemistry. --- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chemistry’s broad utility, moving from the invention of the compass and printing press to the transformative power of gunpowder and the application of chemical principles in agriculture and lost industrial arts.</meta>

**Gunpowder** <term>pulvis pyrius: literally "fire-powder," the early modern term for gunpowder</term> is that infernal fire and the key by which not only the most solid bodies are opened, but even the most fortified cities and once-impregnable castles are utterly demolished. In the same way, sterile fields are fertilized by the **Chemical art**, as was achieved by **Leo the Philosopher** <note>Leo the Philosopher (also known as Leo the Mathematician, c. 790–869) was a Byzantine polymath and archbishop of Thessalonica, famous for his scientific knowledge and mechanical inventions.</note>, the Bishop of Thessalonica. He converted the territory of Thessalonica—which had been reduced to such extreme sterility that the inhabitants were forced either to die of hunger or to migrate elsewhere—back to productivity and rendered it fertile through his own genius and the help of the sciences in which he was skilled. 

There are also many things once known to Chemists which, over time, have fallen into disuse and oblivion, such as **transparent red glass** <note>original: *vitra rubra pellucida*; a reference to specialized stained glass techniques, often involving copper or gold particles, that were difficult to reproduce</note> made from copper. Remnants of these still exist in the windows of ancient temples, yet the art is unknown today or hidden among a very few, because **gold** and **silver** might be extracted from such metallic glass by fire. The same must be thought of **malleable glass** <term>vitrum malleabile: a legendary lost material said to be as durable as metal; ancient accounts claim a craftsman presented a bowl of this glass to Emperor Tiberius that could be hammered back into shape after being dented</term>, whose au—

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the anecdote from the previous page regarding Emperor Tiberius suppressing the invention of malleable glass. It then transitions into a critique of the secrecy and naming conventions used by contemporary chemists and alchemists to deceive the public.</meta>

the inventor, **Tiberius** did away with him along with his entire workshop <note>original: *sustulit*; this refers to the account where the Emperor executed a craftsman to prevent the value of gold from plummeting due to the invention of durable glass.</note>. There is also no small amount of jealousy among craftsmen, who hide even their common discoveries under new names and sell them as secret mysteries. What tricks have they not played on the world under the phantom of the ***Alcahest***? <term>Alcahest: a hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, believed to be capable of dissolving any substance into its primary matter.</term> This monstrosity was first a child of **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss-German physician who sparked the "iatrochemical" revolution by using minerals in medicine.</note>, then adopted by **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a chemist from the Spanish Netherlands who bridged the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry.</note>, and passed from there to others. At one time it was nitre fixed by **Antimony** <term>antimonium: a semi-metal used in the purification of gold; alchemists often associated it with the "wolf" that devours other metals.</term>; later it was a liquid made from oil of vitriol and the spirit of urine or human blood; while for others it was prepared from **Mercury** by the removal of external sulfur.

And today's **Chemists** push a huge harvest of such "secrets" everywhere in their little books, which truly have nothing secret about them, but are merely common things encrusted with a new name. Hence, chemistry falls into contempt, and the craftsmen themselves—because they cannot fulfill what they promise—are counted among impostors. There appeared recently an anonymous work, ***An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King*** <note>original: *Introitus apertus ad occlusum Regis Palatium*; a highly influential alchemical text first published in 1667, later revealed to be the work of George Starkey under the pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, sent many years ago from **England**

<summary>The author criticizes the deceptive "secrets" of alchemists, specifically debunking the various definitions of the Alcahest and noting how the field's reputation suffers from such obfuscation.</summary>
<keywords>Alcahest, Paracelsus, Jan Baptist van Helmont, antimony, mercury, alchemical deception, Introitus apertus ad occlusum Regis Palatium, George Starkey</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of fraudulent alchemists and "secret" texts, specifically mentioning a deceptive manuscript received from England. It then shifts to a historical account of the discovery and spread of "aqua fortis" (nitric acid), citing legal and historical authorities to date its emergence.</meta>

...English manuscript sent to me, a work plainly deceptive and fabricated. This has been known through the experience of many who tested it precisely, resulting in the loss of both expense and time. And by such little books, countless people are deceived today.

Among all chemical discoveries, there is hardly any more wonderful than ***strong waters*** <term>aqua fortis: nitric acid, a powerful corrosive capable of dissolving silver while leaving gold untouched, essential for the process of "parting" or refining precious metals.</term>, by which metals are corroded and gold is most accurately separated from silver. These were not yet known in the time of **Justinian** <note>Justinian I (r. 527–565 AD), the Byzantine Emperor famous for codifying Roman law.</note>, as is evident from Book 5, paragraph 3 of the *Digest* regarding the recovery of property <note>original: *ff. de rei vindicatione*; "ff" is the traditional symbol for the *Digest* or *Pandects*, a massive compilation of Roman legal jurists' writings.</note>; the invention is so recent that **Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola** <note>An Italian nobleman and philosopher (1469–1533) who wrote *De Auro* (On Gold), a scholarly defense of the possibility of alchemy against skeptics.</note>, in Book III of *On Gold*, chapter 1, doubts whether the use of strong water for separating gold from silver existed in the time of **Arnaldus de Villa Nova** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a famous physician and alchemist; the author here cites a slightly later date of 1330 for his influence.</note>. He lived around the year 1330.

**Michael Maier** <note>A German physician and counselor to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, known for his work in alchemy and Rosicrucianism.</note>, in his *Symbols of the Golden Table*, records that in the time of the monk **Vincent of Beauvais** <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar whose *Speculum Maius* was the most significant encyclopedia of the Middle Ages.</note>, strong water was still a most profound secret and not yet widely known. Its first use was in Venice; from there, around the year 1400, it spread into Germany and throughout the whole world.

...ed. That at almost the same time, the ***mariner’s compass***, ***gunpowder***, ***Printing***, and ***strong waters*** <term>aqua fortis: nitric acid, previously described as essential for refining metals</term> came into human knowledge, by which four most distinguished inventions the nautical, military, book-making, and metallurgical arts took on a new face and reached the highest perfection.

Nothing now remains except the ***universal medium or solvent*** <term>menstruum universale: also known as the "Alkahest," a mythical substance sought by alchemists that was believed to be capable of dissolving every other substance into its primary matter</term>, about which those who never had knowledge of it boast wonderful and incredible things. That such a thing exists is most certain, and of such great power that all nature cannot resist it; it tames and crushes all things no differently than a Lion does a fawn—truly a Stygian water <note>original: *aqua Stygia*; a reference to the River Styx in the Greek underworld, used here to imply a water of unstoppable or "hellish" power</note> and Phlegethon <note>A river of fire in the Greek underworld</note>, the fire of fires and the thunderbolt of Jove. But of these matters, more at another time. His works were the foundation of European alchemy.</note> and drink in the **Chemical** art from the very fountains rather than from turbid puddles, with profit and pleasure. There remains another part of this work which **Levinus Warnerus** <note>Levinus Warner (1615–1665) was a Dutch diplomat and orientalist who collected more than 1,000 Middle Eastern manuscripts, which he bequeathed to the Leiden University Library.</note> sent from the East as an Arabic manuscript to Leiden,

I have in my possession a transcript of this, shared with me by that most eminent man **Jacob Golius** <note>Jacobus Golius (1596–1667) was a celebrated Dutch mathematician and professor of Arabic at the University of Leiden. ### GEBER THE ARAB’S
# CHEMISTRY,
### OR
### The Sum of Perfection of the Mastery,
### Divided into two Books.

->*PREFACE,*<-
->*On the manner of teaching this art, and concerning suitable disciples and the purpose of the book.*<-

### CHAPTER I.

All of our knowledge regarding the <term>transmutation of metals</term><note>The alchemical process of changing "base" metals like lead or tin into "noble" metals like gold or silver.</note>, which we have condensed from the books of the ancient philosophers through various compilations in our own volumes, we have here reduced into a single summary. Those things that remained incomplete in other books written by us, we have compensated for in this tradition of the <term>sum of perfection</term><note>The Latin 'Summa Perfectionis' refers to the ultimate completion of the alchemical Great Work.</note>; we have supplied their defects with a brief discourse. Similarly, that which was hidden by us in one part elsewhere, we have made manifest in that same part within this volume of ours: so that, in this way, the completion of such an excellent and noble branch of philosophy might be made manifest <note>The Latin catchword "pate-" completes to "patefiat" on the following page.</note> to the wise—

<summary>This page serves as the introduction to Geber's influential alchemical treatise, stating that the author has consolidated and clarified his previous scattered writings into one comprehensive summary for the benefit of worthy students.</summary>

### OF THE FIRST BOOK

...be made manifest. Know therefore, Dearest son, that in this volume, the operation of this art is sufficiently contained in general chapters, universally and without any omission. By God, he who shall have worked according to this book will rejoice to have found the true end of this art. But know, dearest [son], that he who is ignorant of the <term>natural principles</term><note>Latin: *principia naturalia*; the fundamental laws and elements of the natural world as understood in medieval Aristotelian science.</note> in philosophy is far removed from this art of ours, since he lacks a true root upon which to ground his intention. 

Indeed, he who has known the natural principles and the <term>causes of minerals</term><note>The geological and chemical theories regarding how metals and ores "grow" within the earth.</note> is near: yet he has not yet **ATTAINED**<note>Latin: *ADEPTUS*; this is the root of the word "Adept," describing someone who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note> the true root or the profitable end of this most hidden art, even though he has an easier access to the principles of this art than he whose mind is clouded by ignorance concerning the method and progress of our work; and know that such a one is only a little removed from the entrance of the art. 

But he who shall have known all the principles of nature and the causes of minerals, and the manner of their generation—which consists in the intention of nature—very little indeed is withheld from such a person regarding the completion of the work; nevertheless, without this [knowledge], <term>our science</term><note>Latin: *scientia nostra*; a standard term used by alchemists to distinguish their practice as a formal discipline.</note> cannot be perfected, because Art cannot follow nature in all its operations, but rather imitates it as far as it is able. ### CHAPTER II.

Secondly, we will argue against the ignorant and the <term>sophists</term><note>Latin: *sophistas*; a derogatory term for those who use deceptive arguments or lack true philosophical understanding, often applied here to "puffers" or fraudulent alchemists.</note>, who, because of their own lack of knowledge and inability to seek the <term>mastery</term><note>Latin: *magisterium*; the ultimate goal and highest skill of the alchemical art, often associated with the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> and profit of this art, try to destroy the art and claim it does not exist. We shall first present all their arguments and persuasions, and afterward, we will most clearly demolish them, so that it may be plain enough to the wise that the arguments of those sophists contain no part of truth.

### CHAPTER III.

There are, therefore, two general categories of impediments that affect this work: namely, natural incapacity, and a lack of the necessary funds, or even the distraction of outside labors. We say, however, that natural incapacity is manifold. It may arise from the <term>organ</term><note>Latin: *organum*; in this context, it refers to the physical body or the senses of the practitioner as the "instrument" of the soul.</note> of the craftsman, or from the craftsman’s soul itself. ### On the impediments to the work on the part of the craftsman’s body.

### CHAPTER IV.

So far, we have defined the impediments of this work for you in a general way. Now, however, my son, we shall address you in this chapter with a more specific discourse, and we shall relate all those impediments to you most fully and in order. We say, therefore, that if the craftsman himself does not have his organs complete and healthy, he will not be able to reach the completion of this work by his own efforts—for example, if he were blind, or if he were missing his limbs, so that he is not aided by those members through which <term>our art</term><note>Latin: *ars nostra*; a common term for alchemy, emphasizing it as a sacred or specialized craft.</note> is necessarily perfected, acting as they do as the servants of nature. If, indeed, the craftsman’s body is weak or diseased, such as those suffering from fevers or lepers whose limbs fall away, or those of a decrepit old age and those struggling at the very end of life, they will never by themselves [reach] the completion of this art...

### CHAPTER V.

We have previously set forth one chapter in which we related, in plain and manifest speech, the impediments of this work depending on the body of the craftsman. It now remains to see briefly the impediments which exist on the part of the craftsman’s <term>soul</term><note>Latin: *anima*; in this context, it refers to the mind, intellect, and spiritual state of the practitioner.</note>, which are the greatest impediments to the completion of this work. We say, therefore, that if the craftsman himself does not possess natural <term>genius</term><note>Latin: *ingenium naturale*; an innate intellectual ability or talent for understanding the world.</note>, and a soul that subtly scrutinizes natural principles, the foundations of nature, and the <term>crafts</term><note>Latin: *artificia*; the methods or practical skills used to manipulate matter.</note> which are able to follow nature in the properties of its actions, he will never find the true root of this most precious art. For there are many who possess a "stiff neck" <note>An idiom for being stubborn, obtuse, or unteachable.</note> and are devoid of genius in every investigation; these people can scarcely understand common speech, and they learn even those works familiar to the common folk only with great difficulty. Apart from these, there are many others who possess a soul that very easily adopts any <term>fancy</term><note>Latin: *phantasia*; the faculty of imagination which, if uncontrolled, leads to delusion or false beliefs.</note> as an opinion, so that they believe they already...

### CHAPTER VI.

We have reduced all the impediments of this <term>mastery</term><note>Latin: *magisterium*; the alchemical process and its culmination in the Philosopher's Stone.</note> to two categories that delay its completion: those which arise from the fundamental principles according to the nature of the practitioner himself. Therefore, it is now useful to recount only the impediments that come from without—from the accidents of fortune and chance—by which this <term>glorious work</term><note>Latin: *opus gloriosum*.</note> is hindered. 

For we see certain ingenious and clever men, who are not ignorant of the works of nature, following her principles in this work wherever possible; and in all her works involving investigation and imagination, in all things regulated by the actions of nature beneath the <term>lunar circle</term><note>The "sublunary sphere" in medieval cosmology—referring to the Earth and everything below the Moon, which was thought to be subject to change, decay, and the four elements.</note>, these men are nevertheless weighed down by extreme poverty and a lack of all funds. Thus, they are forced to set aside this most excellent mastery. 

There are also others, inquisitive men, who are held back by the vanities, cares, and anxieties of this world, occupying themselves entirely with every secular business; from such people our <term>precious science</term><note>Latin: *scientia preciosa*.</note> flees. ### *Epilogue on what kind of craftsman is required.*

### CHAPTER VII.

Therefore, we conclude from the aforementioned things that the <term>craftsman</term><note>Latin: *artifex*; the alchemist or practitioner of the art.</note> of this art must be most excellently learned in the sciences of <term>natural philosophy</term><note>The study of nature and the physical universe that was dominant before the development of modern science.</note>, and must possess a naturally profound intellect, along with a great, fervent desire for this craftsmanship. Because that which is not acquired through natural talent is supplemented by art and instruction. It is necessary, therefore, to be aided by the deepest scrutiny and natural diligence. For no matter how much knowledge he might acquire through instruction, unless he is also assisted by natural diligence, he will not be invited to our most precious banquets <note>A metaphor for the successful completion of the "Great Work" or the attainment of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. 

For he will be able to correct his error in an instant through his own diligence, whereas he would not know how to apply a remedy if he were based on instruction alone; for art is aided by talent, and talent by art. Moreover, it is necessary for the craftsman himself to be of constant will in his operation, lest he presume to attempt now this, now that; for our art does not consist in, nor is it perfected by, a multitude of things. *For there is one stone: one medicine: one decoction: in which the whole <term>mastery</term><note>Latin: *magisterium*; the alchemical process and its completion.</note> consists, to which we add no external thing,* <note>original: "Est enim lapis unus: medicina una: decoctio una: in quo totum magisterium consistit, cui non addimus rem extraneam aliquam." This is a foundational alchemical principle stating that the transformation happens within a single, unified system without outside additives.</note>

It introduces the core alchemical principle of the "Unity of the Work"—one stone, one medicine, and one process.</summary>

Moreover, we shall relate to you, **dearest son**<note>A traditional form of address from an alchemical master to a student, implying a spiritual or intellectual lineage.</note>, in this book all those principles in a complete discourse, one that is sufficiently open and clear to the wise, according to every necessary requirement of the art. It is also beneficial for the craftsman himself to be mild-mannered and slow to anger, lest perhaps by a sudden impulse of rage he scatter and destroy the work he has already started. Similarly, it is wise for him to guard his money, lest he presumptuously squander and waste it in vain; for if he should fail to discover the art, he might be left in misery, poverty, and despair—or perhaps, just as he approaches the end through his own <term>industry</term><note>Latin: *industria*; meaning hard work and careful application.</note> and <term>investigation</term><note>Latin: *indagatio*; the systematic search for natural secrets.</note>, his funds may be exhausted, and the poor wretch might be forced to abandon the true end due to his <term>poverty</term><note>Latin: *paupertas*; a common warning in alchemical texts against "puffers" (unskilled alchemists) who spent all their money on coal and equipment without understanding the science.</note>. Just like those who, at the beginning, while they are still ignorant of the art, wastefully exhaust their entire treasure, and when they are now...

# PART TWO

<sig>A 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: your</meta>

...[if they] have been allied to the art and to the truth, they have nothing further with which to work. For this reason, such men are buried in a double sorrow: namely, because they have uselessly spent and squandered their money, and they have lost this most noble science. Indeed, it is not necessary to consume your goods, because for a low price (if you are not ignorant of the principles of this art, and have rightly understood our words) you will be able to arrive at the true completion of the art without notable expenses. 

If, therefore, you lose your money by not attending to our precepts or the doctrines which we have written for you in this book, do not unjustly despise us, but attribute it to your own ignorance and misfortune. For this science is not suitable for the poor or the needy; rather, it is an enemy to them. <note>The author argues that alchemy requires a "safety net" of capital; the "enemy" of the poor is the risk of total financial ruin if the experiment fails.</note> Nor should you strive to find the sophisti<term>cal</term><note>Latin: *sophisticam*; in alchemy, this refers to "sophistry"—processes that produce "false" gold or silver that only looks real on the surface but lacks the true nature of the metal.</note> goal of the work, but aim for no [other] completion. 

Nevertheless, our art is reserved within the power of God, which He bestows upon or withdraws from whomever He wishes; He who is glorious and sublime, and filled with all justice and goodness. For perhaps, as an act of vengeance for a <note>original: *sophistici operis*</note> sophistical work, He might deny the art to you, and thrust you into the bypass of error, and from error into perpetual unhappiness and misery. Most wretched and unhappy is he to whom God always denies the sight of truth after the end of his work and labor; for he concludes and terminates the span of his life in error, who, being established in labor, perpetu- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "your" suggests the sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

## PART TWO,

->*In which the arguments of those who deny this art are set forth, and then refuted.*<-

### Division of the topics.

### CHAPTER VIII.

Since in the first summary <term>summa</term><note>A comprehensive overview or principal section of a treatise.</note> of this little book of ours we have already set before you all the obstacles of the art, and have sufficiently provided the instruction for adhering to this art: it is now appropriate, according to the intention of our plan, to dispute against the <term>sophists</term><note>original: *sophistas*; in this context, 'sophists' refers to those who use false logic or fraudulent methods to claim they have mastered alchemy, or those who use deceptive arguments to claim the art is impossible.</note> and the ignorant. First, by setting forth their arguments and motives, as we promised to determine at the beginning of this book; and thereafter, by dismantling them all, we shall prove to the wise by a clear demonstration that they contain no truth.

## PART TWO.

### CHAPTER IX.

There are, moreover, various people who deny and seek to destroy the art. Some do so simply, while others reject it based on specific premises and assumptions. Those who simply deny that art strengthen their opinions in this way, saying: <margin>1</margin> That because the <term>species</term><note>Latin: *species*; the essential nature or classification of a thing.</note> of things are distinct and diverse, and similarly the distinct <term>proportions</term><note>Latin: *proportiones*; the specific ratios of the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) that were believed to constitute all matter.</note> of the elements to one another in their mixing are unknown to human ingenuity: Therefore, we are ignorant of the proportions of the things to be mixed, through which the form and perfection of a thing are acquired. For a donkey is different from a human in species because it has a much different proportion of elements in its composition, and so it can be inferred in other diversities of things: therefore also in minerals. Thus, with the proportion of the mixable substances through which the form and perfection of the thing is acquired being unknown: how shall we know how to form both the mixture and the thing to be mixed? But because we are ignorant of the proportion of the Sun, the Moon, or the elements, we must therefore also be ignorant of how to form them. <note>In the language of alchemy, the "Sun" (*Sol*) refers to gold, and the "Moon" (*Luna*) refers to silver.</note> From these things, therefore, they conclude: this art is useless and impossible. Similarly, they also argue in another way, attempting to destroy our <term>mastery</term><note>Latin: *magisterium*; the secret process or the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>: for they say, even if the proportion— <margin>2</margin>

<margin>9</margin> Likewise, nature perfects metals over thousands of years; how then can you, in the art of transmutation, endure for thousands of years, when you are scarcely able to reach the limit of a hundred years of life?

# THE FIRST BOOK

...undoubtedly because this too is impossible, and especially in the case of metals, since they are substances or very subtle vapors which require a tempered <term>decoction</term><note>Latin: *decoctione*. In alchemy, this refers to a process of prolonged, steady heating to "ripen" a substance.</note>, so that their own proper moisture may be thickened within them in a balanced way. This prevents the moisture from fleeing from them and leaving the metals deprived of all that <term>Mercurial moisture</term><note>Latin: *humiditate Mercuriali*. Medieval science held that "mercury" was the principle of liquidity and malleability in all metals; without it, a metal becomes brittle or turns to ash.</note> through which they possess the capacity for being hammered and stretched. 

If, therefore, you wish by your art to shorten the time of nature's heating in minerals and metallic bodies, it would be necessary to use an excess of heat to do this. However, this heat will not create a balance, but rather, by dissolving the moisture, it will dissipate and destroy it within those bodies. For only a moderate heat is capable of thickening moisture and perfecting a mixture; an excessive fire is not. 

Similarly, the very existence and perfection of things are granted by the stars, which act as the primary "perfectors" and movers of the matter of <term>generation and corruption</term><note>A standard philosophical phrase referring to the natural cycle of things being born (generation) and decaying (corruption).</note>, leading to both the non-existence and the existence of specific species. This, however, happens suddenly and in an instant, when one or more stars, through their movements, arrive at a specific position in the <term>firmament</term><note>The sphere of the heavens/stars.</note>. From this position, the "being" of perfection is granted, because every single thing acquires its nature in a moment from a specific alignment of the stars. Furthermore, there is not just one such position, but many, and they differ from one another just as their effects are diverse. We are unable to clearly observe the diversity and distinction between these alignments, since they are unknown to us...

In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the essential nature or identity of a thing, which combines with "matter" to create a specific object.</note> through human skill does not happen in an instant, but is a gradual process; therefore, the form will not be granted to the work if the preparation itself is not instantaneous. <margin>13</margin> Similarly, in the natural world, the rule is that it is easier to destroy things than to build them. ### CHAPTER X.

However, we say that these principles, upon which nature itself bases its intention, are of the strongest composition; they are sulphur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum". Because they are of the strongest and hardest composition, they are the most difficult to break down <note>Latin: "resolutionis". Referring to the chemical dissolution or separation of a substance into its components.</note>. Their thickening together and hardening is of such a nature that there occurs in them a striking and extension by the force of the hammer without breaking; this is only because the viscous moisture, in their mutual mixing, is preserved through successive and long-lasting thickening <term>inspissation</term><note>Latin: *inspissatio*. The process of a fluid substance becoming thick or solid through evaporation or chemical change.</note>, and a most tempered <term>decoction</term><note>Latin: *decoctionem*. A process of "cooking" or ripening materials through steady, natural heat within the earth.</note> within the mine.

But we hand down to you a general rule: that no thickening of any moisture occurs unless there first occurs an exhalation of the subtlest parts from it, and a preservation of the thicker parts. This must happen in such a way that the moisture in the mixture overcomes the dry; and because a true mixing of the dry and the moist is when the moist is tempered by the dry and the dry by the moist, and there is made from them a sub-

## SECOND PART.

...one substance entirely uniform <note>original: "homocomera". From the Greek *homoeomeria*, a philosophical concept describing a substance where every part is identical in nature to the whole.</note> in its parts and tempered, having a middle ground between hard and soft, and extensible <note>malleable</note> under striking. This, moreover, does not happen except through the long-lasting mixing of viscous moisture and the subtlest earth with one another at the smallest scale, until the moisture becomes the same as the dry, and the dry becomes the same as the moisture. 

But of this subtle substance, there is no sudden breakdown <term>resolution</term><note>Latin: *resolutio*. In alchemy, this refers to the process of a compound being separated or dissolved back into its constituent elements.</note>, but rather a very gradual one, occurring over many thousands of years. And this is because the substance of those principles is uniform; if therefore such a breakdown happened suddenly—since the moisture does not differ from the dry because of their strong mixing—the moisture would certainly break down along with the dry, whereby the whole would vanish into smoke. Nor could the moisture be separated from the dry in the breakdown, because of the strong union they have with one another. 

We certainly see manifest proof of this in the sublimation of spirits <note>In this context, "spirits" refer to volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that can be turned into vapor by heat.</note>, because when a sudden breakdown occurs in them through sublimation, the moisture is not separated from the dry, nor the dry from the moisture—in such a way that they would be divided into the parts of their total mixture—but their whole substance ascends, or very little of their components is dissolved. 

Therefore, the successive and equal breakdown of the subtle, smoky moisture is the cause of the thickening <term>inspissation</term><note>The process of a fluid substance becoming thick or solid.</note> of metals. This thickening, however, we cannot perform in this man— <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "dum" from the original page indicates the full word is "modum," meaning "manner" or "way."</note>

### Refutation of the arguments of those who simply deny the art.

### Cap. XI.

If they say, therefore, that we are ignorant of the proportion of the elements and the manner of their mixing with one another—and even the equalization of the heat that thickens metals, and many other causes and accidents <term>accidents</term><note>Latin: *accidentia*. In philosophy, these are non-essential properties (like color or temperature) that can change without changing what the substance actually is.</note> that follow the action of nature—we certainly grant this to them. However, they do not for this reason destroy our divine science <note>original: "scientiam nostram divinam". Alchemy was often called the divine or sacred art because it was seen as a way to understand and refine the underlying principles of God's creation.</note>. We neither wish to know these things, nor can we, nor can they pertain to our work. Instead, we take up another principle for this, and another [method] of generation...

For it is well-established that certain princes (though few) and especially the ancients and the wise men found in our own time, have already investigated this science through their own industry; but they were willing to pass it on neither by word of mouth nor in writing to such people, as they are unworthy. Therefore, because the skeptics have not seen anyone possessing this science, an error has fallen upon their minds, leading them to judge that no one has ever found it.

In medieval logic, this refers to reasoning based on false mental images or speculative delusions rather than reality.</note>, asserting that our weakness makes us unable to imitate nature even in "weak" mixtures—such as the breeding <note>original: "mixtione". While it literally means "mixing," in this context it refers to the generation or biological composition of living things.</note> of a donkey or an ox—and therefore we likewise cannot succeed in "strong" mixtures [such as metals]; we respond that there is no logical necessity in their way of arguing that would force us to concede that our Art does not exist. This is because such people strengthen their fantasy and error by arguing from "similarity," or from the "lesser to the greater" <note>A logical fallacy where the opponent assumes that because a seemingly "simpler" task like creating an animal is impossible, the "greater" task of creating gold must also be impossible.</note>. These arguments do not contain logical necessity, but only contingency in most cases.

### BOOK ONE

[...animals] and other living things, in which the composition is "weak" <note>original: "debilis". In this context, "weak" refers to the delicate, complex balance required for life, which is easily disrupted compared to the "strong" or stable nature of minerals.</note>, it is not only the proportion of elements that brings them to perfection. It is not the substances used in that proportion, nor the quality of those substances, nor the blending that results from their mutual action and passion <note>original: "actione... & passione". In medieval philosophy, this refers to the way elements act upon one another and are affected by one another to form a new compound.</note>—which arises from the gathering of those primary qualities—that completes them. Rather, it is the soul <note>original: "anima"</note>, according to the opinion of many, which comes from the secrets of nature, or from the fifth essence <term>quintessence; the purest, celestial element beyond the four earthly ones</term>, or from the Prime Mover <term>the Aristotelian concept of the first cause of all motion and life in the universe</term>. We say this according to the common opinion, though we remain ignorant of the secret nature of this soul. 

Therefore, even though these living things possess a "weak" mixture, we do not know how to bring them to perfection, because we do not know how to infuse that perfecting principle which is the soul. From this it is clearly evident that our inability to "perfect" <note>In this context, "perfect" means to successfully create or fully form.</note> an ox or a goat is not due to a failure in understanding the chemical mixture, but rather from our inability to infuse a soul. For we would certainly know how to create both a weak composition and an even weaker one, or a strong and even stronger one, by following the natural course and using our own art.

In metals, however, the level of perfection is lesser than in living things, and their perfection is concerned more with proportion and composition than with anything else. Therefore, since there is a lesser "perfection" required for metals than for the living things we have described, we can more freely bring these metals to perfection, even if we cannot do so for life. Thus, God Most High and Glorious distinguishes different types of perfections in many forms. For in those things where the composition, which is

## Part Two

<meta>catchword: another</meta>

according to nature, was weak: in those [living creatures], He placed a greater and nobler perfection, which is according to the soul <note>original: "animam"</note>. But some things He founded with a stronger and firmer composition, such as stones and minerals; however, in these He placed a lesser and more ignoble perfection, which exists only by way of its mixture <note>original: "mixtionis modo". In alchemy, minerals are "perfected" simply by the correct balance of elements, whereas animals require a soul.</note>. It is clear, therefore, that their comparison is not a good one. We do not fail to form an ox or a goat because of their physical composition, but because of the "perfecting form" <term>the soul or vital principle that completes a living being</term>. The perfection found in an ox or a goat is nobler and more hidden than that which exists in metal.

If, however, they argue otherwise—claiming that one species cannot be changed into another species—we say that they are lying again, as they so often do. For a species *does* change into another species in this manner: an individual of one kind is clearly changed into the species of another. 

For we see a worm change naturally and through artifice into a fly; and we see a strangled calf change into bees <note>This refers to "bugonia," an ancient and medieval belief in spontaneous generation where bees were thought to be born from the decaying carcasses of cattle.</note>; and wheat into darnel <note>original: "lolium". It was a common medieval belief that wheat could degenerate into this weed under poor conditions.</note>; and a strangled dog into worms through the putrefaction of boiling heat <note>original: "ebullitionis putrefactionem". This refers to the bubbling or fermentation-like heat of decay.</note>. But we do not perform these acts ourselves; rather, it is nature who does them, and we are merely her servants. In the same way, we do not "transmute" metals; nature does the transmuting. Through our ingenious artifice, we serve nature by preparing the material and clearing the path, for nature always works by herself, while we are truly her administrators. And if by another...

<summary>The author defends alchemy by arguing that species can indeed change (citing examples of spontaneous generation) and explains that the alchemist does not "create" change, but rather acts as a servant to nature’s own processes.</summary>

<keywords>transmutation, spontaneous generation, nature as agent, soul vs. mixture, bugonia, alchemy vs. biology</keywords>

# OF THE FIRST BOOK

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

They argue another reason in such a way, and strengthen their misleading <note>original: "sophistice"</note> opinion, by saying that nature perfects metals over thousands of years, yet you cannot endure for thousands of years. We say that nature—acting upon its own principles according to the opinion of the ancient philosophers—achieves perfection over thousands of years; but because we are not able to follow those same principles, their persuasion does not hold, regardless of whether nature perfects these things in thousands of years, or in more, or in fewer, or even in a single moment. As for the fact that we cannot imitate nature in its starting principles <term>the raw, fundamental elements or conditions from which a process begins</term>, we have already determined this from the preceding brief negative discourse, and we shall demonstrate it with a more complete explanation in what follows.

<margin>6</margin> However, according to the opinion of certain wise and perceptive men, nature completes its purpose suddenly—that is, in a single day or even a shorter time. Even if this were true, we are still unable to imitate nature in its starting principles, as we have proven more clearly with sufficient demonstration; however, we admit the rest of this question, since we concede the whole thing to be true.

<margin>7</margin> And if they should say that the perfection of metals—and their very existence or non-existence—is granted by a specific alignment <note>original: "situ"</note> of one or more stars which we do not know, we answer that we do not care for this alignment or movement, nor is it necessarily required that we should know it. For there is no species [of metal]...

# PART ONE

B

<meta>catchword: art</meta>

...which is necessary for things capable of being generated and corrupted <note>original: "generabilium & corruptibilium"; refers to the Aristotelian idea of the physical world where things are born and die</note>, since from the individual members of such things, generation and corruption occur daily and at every instant. And therefore it is clear that every single day, such an alignment <note>original: "situs"</note> of the stars is beneficial and perfects the individuals of any species, and likewise brings about their corruption. Therefore, it is not strictly necessary for the practitioner <note>original: "artificem"; the alchemist or craftsman</note> to wait for a specific alignment of the stars, although it might be useful. It is enough merely to prepare the way for nature and assist it, so that nature herself—who is wise—may arrange the moving bodies into suitable alignments. 

For nature can complete nothing without the movement and alignment of the moving celestial bodies. Whence, if you have prepared the artifice of nature and duly considered whatever requirements of this <term>Magistery|The "Great Work" or the highest process of alchemy</term> are necessary, the work will be completed under the proper alignment by nature itself in a way suitable to it, without you having to consciously consider that alignment. 

For when we see a worm brought into being from a dog or another decaying animal, we do not immediately consider the alignment of the stars, but rather the disposition of the surrounding air and other causes that complete the <term>putrefaction|The process of decay which alchemists believed was the essential first step to new life or transformation</term>, besides the stars. And from such consideration, we sufficiently know how to bring worms into being according to nature. For nature finds for itself the suitable alignment, even if it is unknown to us. And if they should argue that perfection is granted in an instant, while our preparation does not happen in an instant, and conclude from this that the work cannot be completed by human art, therefore...

### BOOK ONE

...art is nothing. We say that their heads are empty of human reason <note>original: "capita illorum esse vacua ratione humana"</note>, and that they are more like beasts than men. For they draw conclusions from premises that have no relation to the conclusion that is inferred. Their argument holds as much weight as saying, "A donkey runs, therefore you are a goat." And this is why: even if the preparation [of a substance] does not happen in an instant, this does not prevent the <term>form|In Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" is the essential nature or identity of a thing, as opposed to its raw matter</term> or <term>perfection|The final, complete state of a substance</term> from being granted to the prepared material in an instant. For preparation is not the perfection itself, but the <margin>9</margin> enabling <note>original: "habilitatio"</note> to receive the form. 

If they also argue that it is easier to destroy natural things than to put them together—just as we can destroy a house in one day but can barely build it in a hundred—and they claim that, according to them, we cannot even destroy gold, they conclude that we would be much less able to compose it. We answer them by saying that they do not conclude this by any necessity that would force us to believe gold cannot be constructed. For while it is destroyed with difficulty, and constructed with even more difficulty, it is not impossible for it to be constructed. We assign the cause of this to the fact that gold has a strong composition, and therefore it has a more difficult <term>resolution|The process of breaking a substance back down into its component parts</term>; for this reason, it is destroyed with difficulty. And this is what makes them think its construction is impossible, because they are ignorant of the artificial destruction that follows the course of nature. They have perhaps proved that it is strong...

<summary>The author refutes skeptics' logical fallacies, explaining that the long time required for "preparation" does not mean the final transformation of matter cannot happen instantly, and addresses the unique physical durability of gold.</summary>

<keywords>logic, human reason, form, perfection, gold, composition, resolution, Aristotelian physics, alchemical theory</keywords>

### BOOK ONE

<meta>catchword: ter,</meta>

It was only through their many errors, and with great difficulty, labor, long-term guesswork, tedious experimentation, and the spending of vast sums of money, that we happened to gather the truth. Indeed, their errors often conflicted with our own mental disposition and reason, nearly driving us to despair. 

May they be cursed forever, because they left behind blasphemies and a curse to those who came after them through their errors. They persuaded those seeking wisdom <note>original: "philosophantes"; those practicing natural philosophy or alchemy</note> with these errors, and after their deaths, they left behind not the truth, but rather a diabolical prompting. And I myself should be cursed if I do not correct their errors and hand down the complete truth in this **science**, as is best suited for this most noble mastery. 

For this mastery does not require a completely hidden language, nor one that is entirely plain. We shall therefore hand it down in such a way that it will not remain hidden from the wise, yet it will seem most profound to the average person, while it will miserably exclude fools by this very method of our tradition <note>original: "traditione"; the delivery or handing down of knowledge</note>. Returning then to our purpose, we say that those who placed this Art in <term>spirits|In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances that can be vaporized by heat, such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</term> are of various opinions, and this in many different ways.

Thus, through their own insufficiency, they mistake a flaw in their reasoning for a point of strength <note>The author argues that the skeptics' failure is due to their own ignorance, not the impossibility of the science</note>.

->*Concerning those who deny the art based on the bodies assumed. And first regarding white lead, tin, or Jupiter, and their refutation.*<-

### CHAPTER XVIII.

Now, some have placed this art within the bodies themselves: however, when they came to the work, they were deceived, thinking that both types of lead—namely, the blue-gray <note>original: "lividum"; referring to common lead or Saturn</note> and the white <note>original: "album"; referring to tin or Jupiter</note>—though lacking pure whiteness, greatly resembled and approached the nature of the <term>Sun|Gold</term> and the <term>Moon|Silver</term>. They thought the blue-gray lead resembled the Sun much and the Moon little, while the white lead <note>Tin</note> resembled the Moon much and the Sun little.

*The reasons of those who deny the art as it is applied to white lead, which is commonly called tin.*

### CHAPTER XIX.

Therefore, some of these men—asserting that <term>Jupiter|The alchemical name for Tin</term> is much like the <term>Moon|The alchemical name for Silver</term>, differing from it only in its creaking sound, its softness, and the speed at which it melts—believed it to be easily liquefied and similarly soft due to an excess of its own internal humidity. They also believed it possessed its creaking <note>original: "stridorem"; this refers to the "cry of tin," a distinct crackling sound made when bending a piece of pure tin</note> because of a fugitive substance of <term>quicksilver|Mercury</term> trapped within the tin itself. 

To test this, they exposed the metal to fire, and by <term>calcining|The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder</term> it, they held it in the fire as long as it could endure, until it was turned into a white <term>calx|The powdery residue left after a metal has been burned</term>. Afterward, when they wished to <term>reduce|The process of returning a calcined powder back into its metallic state</term> it, they were unable to do so; thus, they judged that its reduction was impossible. 

And yet, some of these men, using the greatest intensity of fire, did reduce a small amount from it—though very little—and they found that same creaking and softness in it as before, as well as the same speed of melting. Consequently, they believed the work to be impossible by this path and were led into disbelief, thinking that the art of <term>hardening|The process of making a soft metal like tin more durable or "fixed," like silver</term> it could never be found. Some among them, however, persisted longer in the work; they calcined and reduced it, and then, by removing its <term>scoria|The dross or stony refuse separated from metals during smelting</term>, they calcined and reduced it again with the application of an even greater fire:

<summary>This page describes alchemical experiments with tin (Jupiter), where practitioners attempted to transform it into silver (the Moon) through calcination but failed to "harden" it or reduce it back to metal effectively, leading many to doubt the art.</summary>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Part Two.</header>

...they performed the process; and repeating this often, they saw it <note>This refers to the tin mentioned on the previous page.</note> hardened and without its creaking sound <note>original: "stridore"; the "cry of tin" caused by its crystalline structure being bent.</note>. Because, therefore, they were completely unable to remove the speed of its melting, their minds went astray, and they judged that such a goal could never be reached. Others of these men, wishing to grant the metal hardness and a delay in its melting by mixing it with other hard bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven base metals.</note>, similarly fell into delusion; for the tin broke every body added or mixed with it, and no <term>preparation|The specific chemical treatment or purification of a substance</term> helped in this regard.

And so, since they could perfect it neither through mixtures with hard substances nor by the operation of fire, they excused themselves for the long delay in discovering this <term>mastery|Latin: "magisterium"; the perfected alchemical process or the "Great Work"</term>. Because they believed it to be impossible, they claimed it was so; by arguing persistently against the Art, they asserted that it did not even exist. 

Others of them, adding many and various <term>medicines|Latin: "medicamina"; alchemical reagents or elixirs intended to "heal" or transform base metals</term>, saw that these caused no change and were not suitable for the metal, but rather corrupted it and acted against their intended purpose. For this reason, they cast away their books, turned their backs on the work, and claimed that the Art was frivolous. In opposition to these men, we respond with our first answer: that they have erred both in the <term>matter|The raw material used as the starting point of the alchemical work</term> and in the preparation.

### Arguments of those who deny the Art based on the properties of lead.

### CHAPTER XX.

The same mode of deception is found in lead <note>original: "plumbo"</note>, with the sole exception that it does not break other metallic bodies <note>Unlike tin, which was said to make other metals brittle and "break" them when alloyed, lead is more compliant.</note>, and that it returns from its ash <term>calce|The "calx" or oxide; the powdery substance remaining after a metal has been roasted or "calcined" in fire</term> more quickly than Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for tin.</note> does. However, they are unable to remove its leaden lividity <note>The dull, bluish-gray color characteristic of lead.</note> because they are ignorant. Therefore, they cannot whiten it through proper <term>dealbation|The alchemical process of whitening, purifying, or washing a substance to move it toward the "White Work" (Albedo)</term>, nor could they, by their own imagination, firmly unite and associate lead with stable bodies. Instead, it would inevitably happen that the lead would be separated from the mixture when subjected to the strong pressure of fire.

What most deceives such men in the preparation of Saturn <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note> is that after two reductions from its ash, it acquires no hardness; rather, it becomes even softer than it was before. They did not see it improved in its other qualities through similar distinctions. For this reason—because they believed this science <note>original: "scientiam"; the technical knowledge of the alchemical Art</note> could be more easily and better found in lead, yet failed to find it—they are forced to believe and argue that the Art does not exist, but is a delusion. Thus, these men err just as the previous ones did. We respond to them as we did to the others.

*Arguments of those who deny the Art concerning the mixing of hard bodies with hard, and soft with soft.*

### Chapter XXI.

Others, indeed, by combining hard bodies with hard ones and soft with soft due to their natural affinity <note>original: "convenientiam"; a philosophical concept suggesting that like substances should naturally combine or act upon one another</note>, wished for these bodies to be transmuted by one another and to effect transmutation. Yet they were unable to achieve this because of their own lack of skill. For instance, when mixing the Sun <note>Gold</note> or the Moon <note>Silver</note> with Venus <note>Copper</note> or any other of the metals, they did not see those metals transformed into the Sun or the Moon by a firm and true transmutation. Instead, it inevitably happens that through the intense action <note>original: "expressione forti"</note> of fire, each of those imperfect bodies is separated from the mixture and burned away, while the perfect body remains or returns to its former nature.

Indeed, some of these metals endure longer in a mixture, while others endure less, as shall be sufficiently determined by us in the following chapters. Therefore, all these delusions, which arise from ignorance, cause such men to completely distrust this Art and to argue that it does not exist. Others among them, however, inquiring more deeply into this matter, have applied their ingenuity and sought to find a way to unite hard bodies with soft ones, to subtly harden them, and to bring the imperfect along with the perfect to a state of <term>perfection|The final, stable state of a metal, usually represented by gold or silver, which can withstand the "trial" of fire without loss</term>, so that gene-
