generally they might be transmuted by one another and might transmute, in a firm and true transmutation <note>original: "transmutatione firma & vera"; this refers to a permanent chemical change rather than a superficial surface coloring</note>. And therefore these people wished to find the similarity and affinity of these metals, as much through medicines as through the administration of fire, by thinning the coarse and hard <note>original: "attenuando groſſa & dura"; in this context, making metals like copper and iron more refined or "soft"</note>, such as Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note>, and by thickening the subtle <note>original: "inſpiſſando ſubtilia"; making metals with lower melting points more "solid" or "hard"</note>, such as Jupiter <note>Tin</note> and its companion Saturn <note>Lead</note>. And believing they could perfect this administration, they were deceived in this mixing of bodies, either because they made the whole mixture entirely brittle, or because the soft metal was not altered by the hard one, or the overly hard metal was not altered by the soft. And thus they did not find a proper agreement <note>original: "convenientiam"; a natural harmony or "fittingness" between substances</note>: and therefore, like those who came before them, they condemned and denied the Art.

->*The reasons of those who deny the Art, based on the supposed extraction of the soul, or the governance of fire.*<-

Others, indeed, speculating still more deeply, wished to alter bodies by the extraction of their souls <note>original: "extractione animae"; in alchemy, the "soul" often refers to the essential spirit or the volatile principle (like Sulfur) believed to give a metal its specific properties</note>: and with that extracted soul, they hoped to alter all other things. However, their practical experience could not perfect this, but they were deceived in their intention, and for that reason they judged that the Art could not be found. Others, indeed, attempting to perfect bodies by fire alone, were deceived in their operation

they were according to their own reasoning: because they did not know how to reach that goal. And these people likewise thought that the Art did not exist because of this; we oppose all of them just as we did the first ones.

->*Arguments of those who deny the Art, based on its being placed in glass and gems.*<-

### Chapter XXIII.

Those, however, who placed the Art in glass and gems thought they could find alterations in metallic bodies through glass and gems; and these people were likewise deceived in their work, because that which does not penetrate <note>original: "ingreditur"; in alchemy, this refers to a substance's ability to enter into and chemically bond with another</note> does not alter. Moreover, it is clear that glass and gems do not penetrate, because they are not melted by a true fusion <note>original: "fusione recta"; referring to a melting process where substances blend perfectly rather than remaining separate</note>, and therefore bodies are not altered by them. But even if they have tried to unite glass with metals—although this can scarcely be done—they still do not achieve their purpose, since they are essentially making glass out of the metallic bodies. And because of this, they judge that this error applies to the whole Art, and thus they argue that it does not exist. To all of whom we respond that they are working with improper matter <term>materia: the base substance or "starting material" chosen for an alchemical operation</term>; and therefore, ending their work improperly, they cannot reach their intended goal, nor can they destroy the validity of this Art based on their own errors.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

There are also others who suppose this Art is found in salts and alums, nitres <note>original: "nitris"; likely referring to saltpeter or naturally occurring sodium carbonate</note> and boraxes. They can indeed experiment with these: but we do not think the true Art is to be found in them at all. And so if, after their experience, they find little use for transmutation <term>transmutatio: the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, typically base metals into gold</term>—namely through dissolving, coagulating, roasting, and working in many ways—it is nonetheless very remote and extremely laborious. Others, however, imitate the sayings of the ancient philosophers, who affirm that this Art is found in all plants <note>original: "vegetabilibus"; in this period, "vegetables" referred to the entire kingdom of plant life</note>. And those who place it in all plants suppose a possible thing indeed, but not for themselves, since they would sooner fail in their life than reach their goal. And therefore, if such people never find the Art in plants alone, it is not permitted to argue that the Art can be found by no labors at all. All those mentioned before, however, being in error, established their one single matter <term>materia: the starting substance or "prime matter" used in the work</term> and supposed no other besides it. And all of these have indeed already been refuted. Others, truly many and almost infinite in number, seek the composition of all these things, or of certain ones under a different propor- <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the word "proportions."</note>

<header>SECOND PART</header>
<meta>catchword: TER-</meta>

...acting in proportion, they proceed both knowingly and ignorantly, and their error extends even unto infinity. This is because the diversity of things that can be imagined is infinite, and similarly, the diversity of the proportions between them is infinite. Thus, from these two infinities, it is inevitable that they err infinitely—sometimes through excess, and sometimes through deficiency <note>original: "superabundantia... diminutione"; this refers to the alchemical struggle to find the "middle way" or exact balance of ingredients, avoiding too much or too little of a substance</note>. In these cases, correction is possible, provided however that they have begun their work with the true matter <term>materia: the starting substance or "prime matter" required for the alchemical process</term>.

We, however, sparing the length of speech and the wordiness of writing, do not wish to dwell upon these infinite errors. Instead, we deliver useful doctrine in brief statements, through which any wise person will clearly be able to amend the infinity of their own errors and correct others in their infinite mistakes. Now, however, we shall first discuss and define natural principles <term>naturalia principia: the fundamental laws or primary components of the natural world that govern change</term>, according to all their reasons and causes, as it may best serve this divine mastery <term>magisterio divino: a common term for the "Great Work" of alchemy, implying it is a sacred or divinely-inspired craft</term>. We have made you attentive to these from the beginning, and now we provide this recollection of them.

# THIRD PART.

### Concerning the natural principles of metallic bodies according to the opinion of the ancients.

### CHAPTER XXV.

We have therefore indicated to you, according to the opinion of certain ancient philosophers who belonged to the school of those practicing this art, that the first natural principles from which metals are produced are the **stinking spirit** <term>stinking spirit: a traditional alchemical name for sulphur, referring to the pungent odor it releases when heated</term>, that is, sulphur, and **living water** <term>living water: also known as "mercury" or "quicksilver," so called because it appears to be a liquid that is "alive" due to its constant movement</term>, which we also allow to be called **dry water** <note>original: "aquam siccam"; mercury was called "dry water" because it behaves like a fluid but does not wet the surfaces it touches</note>. 

Moreover, we have divided the stinking spirit into categories. For it is *white* in its hidden aspect, *red* in its manifest <note>original: "manifesto"; referring to the outward appearance or observable properties of a substance</note> aspect, and *black*. Both of these, however, in the mastery of this work <term>magisterio: the "Great Work" or the successful completion of the alchemical process</term>, tend toward **redness** <term>rubedinem: the final stage of the alchemical process, indicating the highest state of perfection and stability</term>. 

We shall therefore describe, in a brief, complete, and clear discourse, the nature and generation of each of these principles, as well as the manner of their generation. It is fitting, then, for us to expand and broaden our discourse, and to provide an individual chapter for each natural principle. In general, however, we say that each of these principles is of a very strong composition and uniform substance, as we have recounted. This is so because within them the smallest parts of earth are joined with the airy parts in such a way,

earthy, watery, and fiery parts are united in such a way that none of them can release the other during dissolution. On the contrary, each is dissolved with the others because of the strong natural union they have with one another. This results from the extreme subtlety <note>original: "subtilitate"; here meaning the fineness or microscopic nature of the particles</note> of the parts, which are joined atom by atom <note>original: "per minima"; literally "through the smallest parts"</note> gradually by natural heat. This heat is distributed equally and "digested" <term>digesto: in alchemy, this refers to a process of slow, constant heating that matures a substance, mirroring biological digestion</term> within the bowels of the earth according to the proper course of nature and the requirements of their essence. And this is according to the opinion of certain ancient philosophers.

### *On the natural principles according to the opinion of the moderns.*

### Chapter XXVI.

Other more recent thinkers, however, have argued differently: that those natural principles were not **living silver** <term>living silver: mercury or quicksilver</term> or **sulphur** in their raw state, but were instead altered and changed into an earthy nature. For this reason, they claimed that the principle was something other than the **stinking spirit** <note>original: "spiritus foetens"; a traditional name for sulphur due to its smell</note> and the **fugitive spirit** <term>fugitive spirit: mercury, so called because it "flees" or evaporates easily when exposed to fire</term> in their natural forms. 

The reason that chiefly moved them to this conclusion is this: they did not find anything that is naturally "living silver" or sulphur within the veins of silver or other metals. Instead, they found each of those substances separated in its own proper mine <note>original: "minera"; this term can refer to the ore, the mineral vein, or the mine itself</note>, in its own specific nature and form.

<summary>This page concludes the ancient theory that metals are formed from a perfect, microscopic union of elements in the earth and introduces a more skeptical 'modern' view. These later thinkers argued that since pure mercury and sulphur are not typically found inside metal ores, the true 'principles' of metals must be these substances in a transformed, earthy state.</summary>

form. And similarly, they affirm this through another reason: that there is no movement from one extreme to its opposite <note>original: "a contrario in contrarium"</note> except through a mediating state. Because of this, they are forced to admit—and rightly so—that there is no transition from the softness of **quicksilver** <term>argenti vivi: literally "living silver," the liquid metal mercury</term> to the hardness of any metal (without an intermediate step) unless there is a mediating state between soft and hard.

But within the mines, they do not find anything that preserves this "middle" disposition. For this reason, they are forced to believe that **quicksilver** and **sulphur** in their own natural forms are not the actual principles intended by nature. Instead, it is something else that results from the transformation of those essences at the very "root" of nature into an earthy substance. 

It happens in this way: since each of them is converted into an earthy nature, a very thin and pure **vapor** <note>original: "fumus"; literally "smoke," but in alchemical contexts, it refers to the exhalations or vapors released by heat</note> is released from both of these earthy substances by the mild heat within the bowels of the earth. This double vapor is the immediate material or "principle" of metal. This vapor—cooked and matured <term>decoctus & digestus: alchemical terms for the natural "digestion" or ripening of substances through heat</term> by the mild heat of the mine—is converted into the nature of a certain earth. Because of this, it undergoes a certain **fixation** <term>fixionem: the process by which a volatile substance becomes solid and resistant to fire</term>. 

Then, mineral water flowing through the bowels and the porous "sponginess" of the earth washes over it as it runs down, dissolving that sponginess. The water becomes uniformly entangled and united with it in a natural, firm, and true union. This is why they said that the water flowing

And this mixture, through successive and long-lasting **cooking** <term>decoctio</term> and **ripening** <term>digestio: the process of maturing a substance through gentle, steady heat</term> by the natural heat condensed within the mines of the earth, is thickened and hardened until nature completes its intention, and it becomes a metal. We say, however, that those who have held this opinion are close to the truth, yet they do not grasp the pure truth.

From there, however, we must pass on to those things which are the foundations and operations of this **mastery** <term>magisterium: the specialized skill or "great work" of the alchemist</term>, assigning causes to all of them.

->*On Sulphur.*<-

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

We say, therefore, that sulphur is the **fatness of the earth** <term>pinguedo terræ: a common alchemical term for the oily, combustible essence found in minerals</term>, thickened in the bowels of the earth by a moderate **cooking** <term>decoctio</term>, until it becomes hardened and dry; and when it has been hardened, it is called sulphur. Moreover, sulphur has a very strong composition and is of a uniform substance, homogeneous in all its parts: for this reason, its oil is not taken away from it by **distillation** <term>distillatio</term>, as oil is taken from other things containing oil. Those who truly seek to **calcine** <term>calcinatio: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or ash</term> it without losing any of its substance worth caring about, labor in vain: because it is not calcined except through great effort and by wasting much of its substance. For out of a hundred parts, you will scarcely keep three for yourself sufficiently after its calcination. Similarly, it cannot be **fixed** <term>figi: to make a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates or escapes when heated</term> unless it is first calcined. However, it can be mixed, and its **flight** <note>original: "fuga"; refers to the volatility or the tendency of the substance to evaporate or escape as vapor when heated</note> can be somewhat delayed, and its burning can be suppressed, and with the mixture more easily

<meta>catchword: more easily</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>PART THREE</header>

...is more easily **calcined** <term>calcinatio: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>. But he who seeks to draw a finished work from it by preparing it on its own will not succeed; for it is perfected only when mixed with something else. Without that companion, the **mastery** <term>magisterium: the specialized skill or "Great Work" of the alchemist</term> is prolonged until one reaches despair. However, when joined with its partner, it becomes a **tincture** <term>tinctura: a substance capable of transmuting or "coloring" base metals into noble ones</term> and grants full weight to each of the metals. It purifies them from foulness, illuminates them, and is perfected through the mastery; without this process, it performs none of these things, but rather corrupts and turns them black. Therefore, do not use it without the mastery itself.

Whoever knows how to mix it and make it "friendly" to the metallic bodies during preparation will know one of the **secrets of nature** <note>original: "secretis naturæ"</note> and one way toward perfection. While there are many paths leading to a single result, one is found to be closer and more perfect than the others. Any metallic body calcined by it undoubtedly gains weight; **Copper** <term>original: "Æs"; while usually "bronze" or "copper," in alchemy it often represents the base metal being transformed</term> truly takes on the likeness of the **Sun** <note>original: "Solis"; the alchemical symbol for Gold</note> from it.

It is also joined with **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, and through **sublimation** <term>sublimatio: heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collects as a solid</term> it becomes **Usifur** <term>an old term, derived from Arabic, for cinnabar or synthetic vermilion</term> or cinnabar. Moreover, all bodies are easily calcined by it, except for the **Sun** [Gold] and **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jovem"; the alchemical name for Tin</note>. Gold is calcined with greater difficulty, and **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: liquid mercury</term> is not **coagulated** <note>hardened or solidified</note> by it into gold or silver (which would be useful) by any weak skill, as some fools have imagined. Indeed, those bodies that possess less of the humidity of quicksilver are more easily calcined by sulphur; but those with much humidity are calcined with far greater difficulty. By God most high, it illuminates...

...and it rectifies every **body** <note>In alchemy, "body" refers to the solid metallic state of a substance</note>: because it is an **alum** <term>alumen: a specific type of mineral salt used as a mordant or astringent, here implying a purifying agent</term> and a **tincture** <term>tinctura: a substance capable of transmuting or "coloring" base metals into noble ones</term>. It is likewise mixed with **mercury** also, and from them by **decoction** <term>decoctio: a process of boiling or long heating to extract the essence of a substance</term> a delightful celestial <note>original: "cœlestinus"; a sky-blue or heavenly blue</note> color is produced. Therefore, let no one think that sulphur completes the work by itself. For to believe this would be nothing but vanity. This we prove clearly enough in what follows. Moreover, that which is thick and shining is chosen. And let these things said concerning sulphur suffice.

### *On Quicksilver*
### CHAPTER XXX.

**Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver"</note>, which is also called **Mercury**, is, according to the usage of the ancients, a **viscous** <note>thick or sticky</note> water, produced from the finest white sulfurous earth and the clearest water, digested and united in the depths of the earth by natural heat. This union occurs in the smallest parts with the strongest bond until the moisture is tempered by the dry, and the dry by the moist, created in equal measure. 

And therefore, it easily flees from a flat surface because of the liquidity and moisture of its water; yet it does not stick to what it touches, even though it has a viscous substance. This is because of the dryness enclosed within it, which tempers the moisture and does not allow it to cling. According to some of the old masters, it is—along with sulfur—the source material of all metals. It also easily adheres to three minerals: namely **Saturn**, **Jupiter**, and the **Sun** <note>original: "Saturno... Jovi & Soli"; the alchemical planetary names for Lead, Tin, and Gold</note>.

It is dissolved and **fixed** <term>figitur: made stable under heat so it does not evaporate</term>, and it is a tincture of redness, of the most exuberant restoration, and of shining splendor; it does not depart from a mixture so long as it remains in its own nature. However, it is not our **matter** or **medicine** <note>the "Philosopher's Stone" or the essential starting material</note> in its raw nature, although it can similarly be of help in certain cases.

### On the effects of the principles of nature, which are metallic bodies.

to each other greatly: yet on this account, the perfect does not perfect the imperfect simply by their mixing. For if **Gold** <note>original: "aurum"</note> is mixed with **Lead** <note>original: "plumbo"</note> in a furnace, gold does not come from this lead; rather, the lead disappears from the mixture and is burnt away. And by God, they alter and are altered by each other, and they perfect and are perfected by each other; yet each one is not perfected by itself alone without the aid of another perfect thing. We certainly judged that they had been cleansed through the benefit of the water, and finally, through the heat of the Sun in the dryness of the sand, they were evenly digested and arrived at a state of equality. Therefore, imitating nature in what ways we can, we likewise alter substances, although in this we are not able—nor should we—follow nature entirely. ### ->Of the Moon, or silver.<-

### ->CHAPTER XXXIII.<-

Directing our discourse to the **Moon**—or by its common name, **Silver**—we say that the Moon is a metallic body, white with a pure whiteness, hard, resonant, enduring in the **cupel** <note>original: "cinericio"; a vessel made of bone ash used to refine precious metals</note>, extensible under the hammer, and fusible in fire. ->*Of Saturn, or black lead.*<-

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

**Lead** is a metallic body that is very bluish-gray, earthy, heavy, and **mute** <note>original: "mutum"; meaning it does not "ring" like silver or copper when struck</note>. It possesses a small amount of whiteness along with much lividity. It shuns the **refining by ashes** <note>original: "cinereicrum"; likely referring to cupellation, where metals are melted on a bed of bone ash</note> and **cementation** <note>a process for refining gold using salt and heat</note>. It is soft, easily stretched in every dimension with little pressure, and melts easily without needing to be brought to a red heat.

Some fools, however, think and claim that lead closely approaches gold in its nature. But because they are **stiff-necked** <note>original: "duræ cervicis"; a biblical idiom for those who are stubborn or resistant to learning</note> and devoid of all reason, they are unable to grasp any truth from subtle matters—as much as is in them—but instead judge things according to their senses. And because...

### *Of Venus, or Copper.* <note>The OCR reads "Aëre" (Air), but contextually this is a typo for "Ære" (Copper or Bronze)</note>

### Chap. XXXVI.

**Mars** <term>Iron</term> is a metallic body, very dark and leaden in hue, yet slightly reddish, sharing in an impure whiteness; it is hard, subject to the fire, not meltable by direct fusion <note>Iron has a high melting point that was difficult to reach in standard alchemical furnaces</note>, malleable, and very resonant. Moreover, Mars is of the most difficult handling, and this is because of the powerlessness of its fusion. But if it is melted without a **medicine** <term>transformative substance</term> that changes its nature, it will be joined to the Sun or the Moon, and will not be easily separated from them by any...

### *Concerning the difference of imperfect metals in terms of their perfection.*

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

Among all the imperfect bodies, therefore, **Tin** <term>Stannum; associated with Jupiter</term> exists as the most splendid, clear, and of the greatest perfection. ### FOURTH PART.

->*On the artificial principles of this mastery.*<-

->The division of topics in this part, with an introduction to the perfection to be presented in the second book.<-

### CHAPTER 39.

Now that we have determined the Principles of this **mastery** <term>magisterium; the skilled practice of alchemy and its final goal</term>, let us also describe its perfection. The principles of this art are the modes of its operations by which the craftsman applies himself to this work. These modes are indeed different from one another. The first mode is **sublimation** <term>sublimatio; the process of vaporizing a solid and collecting it again as a purified solid</term>, the second is **descension** <term>descensio; a method of distillation where heat is applied from above to force a substance downward into a vessel</term>, and another is **distillation** <term>distillatio</term>, another **calcination** <term>calcinatio; heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>, another **solution** <term>solutio; dissolving a solid into a liquid</term>, another **coagulation** <term>coagulatio; solidifying a liquid</term>, and another **fixation** <term>fixio; making a volatile substance stable so it remains solid when heated</term>. The eighth and last is **ceration** <term>ceratio; the process of softening a substance until it has the consistency of wax</term>. Regarding these, we shall provide a specific account for each individually in the following sections.

Furthermore, the Perfection of this Mastery consists of the consideration of those things by which the completion of the work is reached; and the consideration of those things assisting in that perfection; and the consideration of the thing itself which ultimately completes it; and finally the consideration by which one knows whether the Mastery is in a state of perfection or not. Therefore, the consideration of those things through which one arrives at the completion of the wor... <note>The text breaks here at the end of the page; "completion" is continued from the Latin "complemen-" and catchword "plemen-" on the following page.</note>

### PART FOUR.

<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

...completion, is the consideration of the manifest substance and manifest colors, and of the weights to be changed in each of the **bodies** <term>corpora; the metals, usually referring to the seven known in antiquity</term>, and of those which are not to be changed from the root of their nature without any **artifice** <term>artificium; the skilled labor or "art" of the alchemist</term>. It is also the consideration of those same things in their root with artifice, and the consideration of the principles of bodies according to their hidden depth and their manifest appearance, according to their nature, without artifice, and similarly with artifice.

Because, if bodies and their principles were not known in the depth of their nature and in their manifest appearance—both with artifice and without—and if we did not know what was superfluous in them, what belonged to their perfection, and what was diminished in them, it would be inevitable that we should never reach the perfection of their **transmutation** <term>transmutatio; the transformation of one substance into another, specifically base metals into gold</term>.

Moreover, the consideration of those things that assist perfection is the consideration of the natures of those things which we see adhering to bodies without artifice and causing a change. These are: **Marcasite** <term>Marchesita; a term used for various metallic minerals, often iron pyrites</term>, **Magnesia** <term>Magnesia; in alchemy, this could refer to manganese or other minerals used in glassmaking and smelting</term>, **Tutty** <term>Tutia; zinc oxide collected from the flues of furnaces</term>, **Antimony** <term>Antimonium</term>, and **Lapis Lazuli**.

Also included is the consideration of those things which cleanse bodies without adhering to them, such as **Salts** <term>Salia</term>, **Alums** <term>Alumina</term>, **Nitres** <term>Nitra</term>, and **Boraxes** <term>Boracia</term>, and things of that same nature; and the consideration of **vitrification** <term>vitrificatio; the process of turning a substance into glass, often to separate pure metal from dross</term> which cleanses through a similar nature.

### Concerning the perfecting material.

### CHAPTER XL.

Truly, the consideration of the thing which finally brings completion is the consideration of selecting the pure substance of **Quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury, which alchemists believed was the root of all metals</term>. This is the material that took its origin from the substance of mercury and was created from it. However, this material is not Quicksilver in its own nature, nor in its entire substance, but is a specific part of it. It is not present now <note>original: "non est autem nunc"; the author implies it does not exist in this perfected state in nature</note>, but only when **our stone** <term>lapis noster; the Philosopher's Stone</term> has become a part of it. For the stone itself illuminates the substance and preserves it from both burning and from "fleeing" <note>original: "fuga"; a reference to volatility, or the tendency of mercury to evaporate when heated</note>, which is the true sign of perfection.

It is useful to describe all these things along with their causes and with easy experiments, through which you will be able to clearly recognize that our words have not erred. And these experiments shall be...

### Concerning sublimation in general.
### Why it was invented.
## CHAPTER XLII.

Following our purpose, we state that the reason for the invention of sublimation was this: because neither the ancients, nor we, nor those who will come after us, have found anything else that could be united with **bodies** <note>original: "corporibus"; in alchemy, "bodies" refers to the fixed metals, such as gold, silver, or lead</note> except for **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; volatile substances that evaporate or "fly" when heated, such as sulfur or mercury</note> alone, or something that contains the nature of both body and spirit within itself.

And they saw that these spirits, when cast upon bodies without preparation, either gave imperfect colors or completely corrupted and blackened them; and this occurred according to the variety of those spirits. For some of the spirits are burning and blackening, such as **sulfur**, **arsenic**, and **marcasite** <term>marchasita; iron pyrites, which alchemists often grouped with sulfurous minerals</term>. These indeed corrupt and defile the bodies entirely. Others, however, do not burn (but similarly "flee" from the fire), such as every kind of **tutty** <term>tutia; crude zinc oxide collected from the chimneys of smelting furnaces</term> and **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum; mercury</term>; and these indeed provide imperfect colors. The reason for all of them is that those which burn and blacken do so for a twofold reason.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the practical instructions for the alchemical Mastery, explaining that sublimation was invented to purify volatile "spirits" before they are joined with metal "bodies." It highlights how unrefined substances like sulfur or arsenic can corrupt metals rather than perfect them.</summary>

...they make [them black]. The first reason is because their **burning oiliness** <term>unctuositas adustiva; a sulfurous, fatty quality that alchemists believed caused substances to char and turn black</term>, the nature of which is to be easily inflamed and consequently to blacken, has not been taken away from them. The second cause is the **earthiness** <term>terreïtas; the solid, non-volatile dregs or impurities within a substance</term> which was similarly not removed from them. 

In those cases, however, where a perfect color is not achieved, the cause is earthiness alone, which creates a leaden <note>original: "lividum"; a bluish-gray or bruised color indicating impurity</note> color upon **projection** <note>original: "projectione"; the process of casting an alchemical powder or medicine onto a molten base metal to transform its properties</note>; (for burning can indeed create a leaden color). Therefore, we have devised a way to cleanse these **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that "flee" the fire</note> from the oiliness which is the cause of burning, and from the earthy dregs that bring about the leaden hue. 

And we could arrive at this through no other **mastery** <term>magisterium; the skilled alchemical operation or the "Work" itself</term> except for **sublimation** <note>original: "sublimationem"; the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools and reforms</note> alone. For when fire elevates a substance, it always lifts the finer parts and leaves behind the coarser ones. From this, it is clear that through sublimation a spirit is cleansed of the earthiness that both hindered its ability to enter the metal and gave it an imperfect color. 

Indeed, it is clear to the sight through experience that they have been freed from these impurities by sublimation. For you see that through sublimation they become more brilliant and more permeable, and more easily enter and penetrate the density of **bodies** <note>original: "corporum"; the solid, fixed metals such as lead, copper, or silver</note>; and they do not produce a foul color as they did before their sublimation. 

That the tendency to burn is removed by sublimation is likewise clear through a manifest experiment: for **arsenic** <note>original: "arsenicum"</note>, which before its sublimation was wicked <note>original: "impium"; here meaning "unclean" or "corrosive" in its raw state</note> and prone to

...combustion, [the substance] does not allow itself to be inflamed after its sublimation, but instead recedes from the fire without any burning. Because, therefore, as we have said before, we have observed the power of **adherence** and **ingression** <note>original: "adhærentia & ingressionem"; the ability of a chemical "spirit" to bond with and penetrate the internal structure of a base metal to change its nature</note> into the bodies of metals—along with their alteration—in no other substances except for the **spirits** <note>original: "spiritibus"; volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, and arsenic that "flee" the fire but are essential for transmutation</note>, we are not excused from the necessity of preparing them. This preparation must include their **cleansing** <note>original: "mundatione"</note>, which is perfected through **sublimation** <term>sublimatio; the process of heating a solid until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified crystals as they reform on a cooler surface</term>. And this was the reason for its necessary invention. It is fitting, therefore, for us to describe its entire order and method without any omission.

### *What sublimation is, and concerning the three degrees of fire to be observed in it.*

### CHAPTER XLIII.

Sublimation is the elevation of a dry substance by fire, resulting in its adherence to its vessel. Truly, the process of sublimation is varied according to the diversity of the spirits to be sublimated. For some are done with a strong ignition, some with a moderate fire, and some with a slow and gentle fire. If, therefore, sulfur and arsenic are to be sublimated, it is necessary that they be sublimated by a slow and gentle fire. This is because they possess parts that are very fine...

...[subtle substances] joined uniformly and very strongly with coarse ones <note>The text refers to the "spirits" (volatile substances like sulfur or mercury) being bound to heavier, impure matter.</note>, if they were sublimated by a great force of fire, their entire substance would surely ascend without any separation or purification—indeed, it would be blackened and scorched. 

Therefore, so that one may separate the unclean earthy substance, it is necessary to employ two types of methods: namely, the proper proportion of the fire and the purification by mixing with **dregs** <term>feces; in alchemy, this refers to the solid residue, sediment, or added "body" left behind after a process like distillation or sublimation</term>. For the mixing of the **spirits** <note>original: "spirituum"; volatile alchemical substances</note> to be sublimated with these dregs captures the coarse parts and holds them down at the bottom of the **aludel** <term>aludel; a pear-shaped ceramic pot or condenser used to collect the purified vapors in the sublimation process</term>, and does not permit them to rise.

Hence, it is also necessary for the craftsman to apply a threefold, proportional degree of fire while sublimating:

One degree should be such that only the altered, cleaner, and brighter parts ascend, until the craftsman clearly sees by this that they are being cleansed of the mixed earthy impurities. 

Indeed, let him apply another degree of fire such that whatever pure essence remained in the dregs during the first sublimation may be raised with a greater force of fire—namely, by heating the aludel and the dregs themselves until they glow, which the craftsman will be able to see clearly with his own eyes. 

The third degree, however, is that a very weak fire be applied—without any dregs—to that which has already been sublimated from the dregs and purified. This is done so that hardly any of it ascends; and so that through such heat...

Therefore, the entire intent of their **sublimation** <term>sublimation; the alchemical process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then cools back into a solid, used here to purify the substance.</term> is this: that once the unclean earthiness has been removed through the proper administration of fire, and after the very subtle and smoky part—which brings about burning and corruption—has likewise been cast away, there remains for us that part which exists in a state of balance. This part should undergo a simple **fusion** <note>melting into a liquid state</note> over the fire without any burning, and it should not flee from the fire through inflammation.

The proof that the most "subtle" <note>The author uses "subtle" to describe the lightest, most volatile particles which are highly reactive.</note> part is prone to burning is demonstrated by the most obvious arguments: for fire converts into its own nature whatever is most akin to it. But of every combustible thing, that which is subtle is more akin to fire, and that which is even subtler is more akin still. Therefore, the most subtle part is the most akin to fire and thus most easily consumed by it.

This is likewise known from experience: for **sulfur** or **arsenic** that has not been sublimated is ignited very quickly (sulfur indeed more easily), but the sublimated version is not directly ignited. Instead, it melts, evaporates, and is refined without catching fire, provided that it has first been melted. Thus, from these facts, it is clear that our account is truthful. ...in its proper proportion, is that a material should be chosen with which the **spirits** <note>original: "spiritus"; in alchemy, these are volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that can be vaporized by heat.</note> to be sublimated are more compatible, and with which they may be more deeply intermingled. For that material to which they are more closely united is more powerful in retaining the substances to be sublimated within itself than one to which they are not. Of this, there is a sufficiently reasonable and manifest proof. 

The proof that a shared administration <note>The "administration" refers to the specific way materials are combined and managed during the chemical process.</note> [of materials] is necessary is this: because if the **sulfur** or **arsenic** to be sublimated were not joined with the **dregs** <term>feces; the solid, non-volatile residue or "earthy" part of a substance that remains fixed at the bottom of the vessel during heating.</term> of some fixed thing, then it would be necessary for them to ascend with their entire substance without being "washed" <note>original: "non inundata"; here the author suggests the volatile spirits would rise without the filtering or "cleansing" effect provided by the solid base.</note>, and then no division of the pure from the impure would occur. For the expert knows that we speak the truth, as do those who are practiced in **sublimations** <term>sublimation; the alchemical process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then cools back into a solid, used to purify substances.</term>. 

Indeed, the proof that the shared administration of such things is necessary—specifically those with which these two spirits to be sublimated are compatible and with which they are united in their very depths—is this: because if the dregs were not united with them in their depths, then the same thing would happen as if they had no dregs at all: for their entire substance would ascend without any separation. 

Experience knows this to be true for whoever has seen and known it: for when one has sublimated without dregs, or with dregs with which the spirits to be sublimated were not united in their depths, he labored in vain, and in no way found a purified work after their ascent. If, however, he sublimated with the **calx** <term>calx; the fine, powdery residue left after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly burnt or roasted.</term> of some body, he was able to cleanse it perfectly...

# PART FOUR.

...could be done with ease. Therefore, the purpose of the **dregs** <term>feces; the solid, non-volatile residue that remains at the bottom of a vessel during heating.</term> is that they should be administered using the **calxes** <term>calx; a powder or oxide created by heating a metal to high temperatures until it loses its metallic form.</term> of metals. For sublimation is an easy operation when using them; but with other materials, or without dregs, it is extremely difficult. Therefore, there is nothing else that can take their place. 

We do not say, however, that sublimation is entirely impossible without the calxes of bodies <note>In alchemical texts, "bodies" (corpora) almost always refers to the seven traditional metals.</note>; rather, we say it is most difficult and of very long labor, even to the point of despair. Yet there is this much benefit in the other method: that which is sublimated without dregs, or without the calxes of bodies, results in a large quantity; whereas that which is processed with dregs results in less. Furthermore, that which is processed with the calxes of bodies results in the smallest quantity, but involves the easiest and shortest labor. 

That which provides us a better alternative to using the dregs of bodies is every kind of **prepared salt** and substances similar in nature. With these, the sublimation produces a large quantity, and there is an easy separation of the substances to be sublimated from the salts and the dregs through the dissolving of the salts—a convenience that does not occur with any other materials. 

The proportion of dregs is such that the quantity of dregs should equal the quantity of the substances to be sublimated. In this, even an unskilled **artisan** <note>original: "artifex"; the practitioner or chemist performing the work.</note> cannot go wrong. For the mediocre and practiced artisan, however, it is expedient—and usually sufficient—to use half the weight of the dregs relative to the weight of the substances to be sublimated; in this, he should not err. But for the practiced and most expert [artisan]...

...once opened, the smallest part of them suffices in respect to the substances to be sublimated. For the smaller the quantity of **dregs** <term>feces; the solid, non-volatile material used to carry the substance being heated</term> administered, the greater the abundance of the sublimation that occurs: provided, however, that as the dregs are reduced, the fire is reduced proportionally. In a small quantity, a small fire should be applied for the perfection of the sublimation; in a large quantity, a large fire; and in a larger one, the greatest. 

But because fire is not a thing that can be measured with absolute precision <note>The author is writing in an era before thermometers, where heat was judged by sight, touch, and the behavior of the materials.</note>, errors occur very frequently: partly because the practitioner <term>artifex; the "artist" or alchemical worker</term> is not experienced, and also from the variation in the proportion of the dregs, the differences in the **furnace** <term>fornax</term> and the way the wood is laid, and likewise the types of vessels and how they are fitted into the **oven** <term>furnus</term>. Regarding all these matters, it is necessary for the artisan to be cautious, attentive, and diligent. 

We will, however, provide you with a general rule. First, it is beneficial to use a very small fire to draw out only the **watery nature** <term>aqueitas; the initial moisture or volatile "water" within a substance</term> from the materials to be sublimated. Once this is removed, if anything rises by that weak fire, the intensity of the heat should be increased not suddenly, but gradually. In this way, the most subtle part is removed by the weakest fire and set aside or discarded: for this part is often the cause of **scorching** <term>adustio; a burnt or acrid quality that ruins the purity of the product</term>. When little or nothing of significance rises through that level of heat anymore (which can be tested by inserting a **wick** <term>licinium</term> or a reed with **cotton** <term>bombyx</term> or...

...or wool, and once it has been wrapped and inserted through the hole in the top of the **aludel** <term>aludel; a series of pear-shaped ceramic jars used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>, the fire should be strengthened under it. For according to the quantity, cleanliness, or impurity of the sublimated material that the **artisan** <term>artifex; the alchemical practitioner</term> sees clinging to the wick, he will be able to reason <note>original: "syllogisare"; to logically deduce the state of the reaction</note> and will necessarily find the proportional fire needed throughout the entire sublimation; through this, he will find the correct adjustment of the fire without error. Indeed, a better method of managing the process is to use **iron scales** <term>ferri squamae; the flaky oxidation produced when iron is forged</term> or **burnt copper** <term>aes ustum; copper oxidized by heat</term>. For these, because they lack much moisture, easily absorb the **sulfur** or **arsenic** that is to be sublimated and become more united with them. Only the experienced practitioner knows the truth of this.

### *On errors concerning the quantity of dregs and the arrangement of the furnace. Also on the material and quality of the vessels used in the aforementioned sublimations.*

### CHAPTER XLIV.

It is beneficial, therefore, to instruct the artisan in all those matters where it is easy to make a mistake in the **sublimation** <term>sublimatio; the process of vaporizing a solid substance and collecting it again as a solid further up the vessel</term> of these two **spirits** <note>original: "spirituum"; likely referring to mercury and sulfur, or perhaps arsenic and sulfur, the volatile "spirits" of alchemy</note>. We say, therefore, that if he should use too many **dregs** <term>feces; the solid residue or "lees" mixed with the primary substance to regulate its vaporization</term>, and does not increase the fire proportionally, nothing of the substance to be sublimated will rise for him. Now, how one may find the correct proportion of fire has already been sufficiently explained to you. 

And if he should use too small a quantity of dregs, or if he does not use dregs made from the **lime of metals** <term>calx; the powdery oxide produced by roasting a metal, used here to temper the reaction</term>, then if he does not find the correct proportion of fire, the substance to be sublimated will rise in its entirety without being **purified** <note>original: "absque mundatione"; without the separation of the pure from the impure</note>. Regarding this, I have likewise provided a sufficient explanation in the preceding chapter. 

Errors also frequently occur regarding the furnace itself. For a large furnace produces a strong and large fire, while a small one produces a small fire—provided that the wood and the openings of its **air-holes** <term>auriculae; literally "little ears," the vents or draft-holes used to control the flow of air to the fire</term> are appropriately sized. If, therefore, the artisan places a large quantity of the substance to be sublimated into a small furnace, he will not be able to produce enough heat for it to rise. Conversely, if you place a small quantity in a large furnace,

...you will ruin the **sublimation** into smoke because of the excess of fire. Similarly, a thick-walled furnace provides a concentrated and strong fire, while a thin one provides a sparse and weak fire; in both cases, it is common to make a mistake. Likewise, a furnace with wide air-holes <term>auriculis; literally "ears," referring to the vent-holes or intake apertures that regulate airflow</term> provides a bright and large fire, but one with narrow holes provides a weak fire. Furthermore, if there is a large distance from its ledges <term>spondilibus; the internal rims or supports within the furnace upon which the vessel rests</term> to where the vessel is fitted, it will produce a large fire; but if the distance is smaller, the fire will be smaller. In all these matters, errors occur very frequently.

The **correction** <note>original: "rectificatio"; here meaning the proper adjustment or rectification of the apparatus</note> for these issues, therefore, is that the furnace must be constructed according to the intended heat required: specifically, a thick-walled furnace with open vents and a significant distance between the **aludel** <term>aludel; a pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in sublimation to collect the condensed vapors</term> and the furnace ledges, if a large fire is desired. If a moderate fire is needed, let there be a moderate proportion in all these factors; if a weak fire, let the same <note>meaning a small proportion</note> be found. We shall teach you to find all these proportions through true preparation and certain experience.

If, therefore, you wish to raise a large quantity of material by sublimation, find an aludel of such capacity that it can hold the substance on its bottom to the height of one palm's breadth. Fit the furnace to it so that it holds the aludel in its center, with a distance of two fingers or more from its walls. And when the furnace is made,

### PAGE 76

Once this is done, you should make for it air-holes <term>auriculas; literally "ears," referring to the vent-holes or intake apertures</term> equally distant from each other in a uniform proportion, so that the required quantity of fire may be poured around all parts of the furnace equally. Then, with an iron rod <note>original: "stipite ferreo"</note> fixed across the middle of the furnace on its ledges <term>spondilibus; the internal rims or supports within the furnace</term>—which should be at a distance from the bottom of the furnace equal to the span of one palm including the thumb, and have a thickness of one finger—the **aludel** <term>aludel; the pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in sublimation</term> vessel is to be fixed upon it and joined to the furnace.

And let a fire be made within it. For if it then clears itself well and freely of smoke, and the flame spreads freely through the whole furnace in the circuit of the aludel, then it is well-proportioned. If, however, it does not, then widen its vents according to what seems right to you, and light the fire in it again. And if it is corrected, well and good; but if not, then it necessarily follows that the distance of the aludel from the walls is too small. 

Therefore, let the walls of the furnace be scraped <note>original: "radantur"; to thin the inner walls and increase the internal volume</note> and the distance increased, and afterward let it be tested again. Let the widening of the vents and the scraping of the walls be repeated until it frees itself of smoke, and the flame around the aludel is bright and exits freely through the vents. For this is a sufficient test for any quantity of materials to be sublimated, regarding the proportion for finding the size of the furnace and the widening of its vents, along with the distance of the aludel from its walls. D 3

<meta>catchword: not.</meta>

If you seek a great fire, its thickness <note>referring to the walls of the furnace</note> should be greater, to the measure of the span of a palm with its thumb <term>roughly 8–9 inches, used to retain higher heat</term>. If indeed a medium fire is needed, let it be to the measure of four fingers or less. If, however, a smaller fire is required, let it be formed to the thickness of two fingers.

Similarly, a proportion should be maintained in the wood used: for solid wood gives a strong fire, while spongy <note>original: "spongiosa"; likely referring to light or porous woods like poplar or willow which burn less intensely</note> wood gives a weak one; likewise, dry wood gives a large fire that is quickly finished, but green <note>freshly cut</note> wood gives a small fire that lasts a long time. 

Therefore, from the consideration of the distance of the **aludel** <term>the pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in sublimation</term>, the size or smallness of the vents <term>auricularum; literally "ears," the air-intake holes</term>, the thickness or thinness of the furnace walls, and the variety of woods mentioned before, it follows that the variations of all fires can be mastered, as their true effects have already been tested by us in various ways. 

By the closing or opening of the vents or the furnace window through which the wood is introduced, and by managing the variety of woods and their addition or subtraction, it is possible to determine the exact duration of the fire. That is to say, so that a true and certain knowledge may be obtained regarding how long each fire, at its specific grade, will be able to endure in equilibrium. And this investigation is most useful and necessary to you: because through it you will be spared from a multitude of immense labor <note>consistent heat allowed the alchemist to leave the furnace unattended for periods without the work being ruined</note>. 

Exercise yourself, therefore, in this and in everything we have now recently determined: for he who exercises himself shall find; but he who does not, shall not.

not. Now, the purpose of the **aludel** <term>a pear-shaped glass vessel used for the process of sublimation</term> is that it should be fashioned from thick glass. For it is not effective if made of any other material, unless perhaps it is of a substance similar in nature to glass. Only glass, because it lacks pores <note>microscopic holes that would allow gas to pass through</note>, is capable of containing **spirits** <term>volatile chemical substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</term> to prevent them from escaping or being destroyed by the heat of the fire. No other material will suffice; because through their pores the spirits are gradually diminished and vanish. 

Nor are metals of any use in this task; because spirits, owing to their affinity <note>original: "affinitatem"; a chemical attraction or kinship between substances</note>, unite with the metals, and so as they pass through them they would gradually disappear. This can be clearly proven by the principles we have already established. Indeed, it has been found through both necessity and experience that what we have said is true. Therefore, no other reason excuses us from the requirement of using glass in the construction of the **aludel**.

Therefore, let a round glass basin be made, the bottom of which has a small concavity. Since it is the nature of vapors to ascend and not descend, we have found through this design that the spirits have no exit to be lost; and we have found this method to excel above all others we have sought through our experimentation. By practicing this, others will see that we have judged rightly in this matter. 

One objective in every **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a solid until it becomes vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid</term> is that the **aludel** <term>the pear-shaped collection vessel</term> or lid should be emptied very frequently. This is to prevent the materials being sublimated from accumulating too much in the basin, where they might fall and be cast back down to the bottom. **OF THE FIRST BOOK**

...always separate that which has ascended upward toward the opening of the **aludel’s** <term>the pear-shaped vessel used for sublimation</term> head and appears as a powder. This should be kept apart from that which is found lower down, fused and dense in chunks near the bottom of the aludel, which appears clear and bright where it clings to the vessel’s **rims** <note>original: "spondilia," referring to the joints or the inner edges of the vessel where sections meet</note>. The reason for this is that the material at the bottom has less "scorching" <note>original: "adustione"; refers to chemical degradation or impurities caused by the fire</note> than what is found to have climbed near the top opening. This fact is proven in the earlier sections through both clear reasoning and practical experiment. 

Indeed, the test for the quality and perfection of a sublimation is that the result be found clear and translucent, and that it is neither scorched nor "inflamed" <note>likely meaning it does not catch fire or turn an angry red/burnt color during the process</note>. This, then, is the perfect goal for the sublimation of these two **spirits** <term>volatile substances that can be vaporized</term>, namely **sulfur** and **arsenic**. If the result is not found to be so, let the work be repeated on it, with careful consideration of all its intended steps, until it is finally achieved.

->*On the sublimation of Mercury.*<-

### CHAPTER 45.

Now it remains to describe the entire purpose of sublimating **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti vivi," the liquid metal mercury</note>. The complete sum of this goal is the purification of its "earthiness" <term>the solid, non-volatile impurities</term> and the removal of its "wateriness" <term>excessive or unwanted moisture and volatility</term>. In this case, we are spared the labor of removing "scorching," because mercury does not possess that quality. We say, therefore, that the technique for separating its superfluous earth is to mix it with "dregs" <note>original: "fecibus"; leftover solid matter from previous distillations or inert materials</note> or other substances with which it has no **affinity** <note>original: "affinitatem"; a lack of chemical attraction, ensuring the mercury does not bond with the "dregs" while being cleaned</note>,

...has, and to reiterate its sublimation from them many times. And of this kind of substance are **talc** <note>original: "Talek"</note>, and the **calx** <term>the powdery residue left after a substance is burnt or roasted</term> of eggshells, and of burnt white marble, and very finely ground glass, and every kind of prepared salt. 

For by these materials, the mercury is cleansed. However, when using substances with which it shares an affinity <note>original: "convenit"; substances of a similar alchemical nature or "kinship"</note>, it is not cleansed—unless they are "perfect" bodies—but is instead corrupted and blackened. This occurs because all such compatible substances possess a combustible **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitatem"; referring to the oily, flammable impurities that alchemists believed caused metals to char or discolor under heat</note>; when this sulfurousness rises along with the mercury during the sublimation process, it corrupts it. 

You may see this for yourself through experience: for if you attempt to sublimate mercury from **tin** <note>original: "Stanno"</note> or from **lead** <note>original: "plumbo"</note>, you will find that after the sublimation, the mercury is infected with a black color. Therefore, its sublimation is better performed using substances with which it does not share a nature, even though a sublimation using compatible things would be superior if only they did not contain that sulfurousness. This is because it would unite more easily with compatible substances and thus be more perfectly purified. 

And so, sublimation from **talc** is better than from all other materials, since it has little affinity with the mercury and contains no sulfurousness. 

Now, the method for removing its superfluous **wateriness** <term>original: "aqueitas"; the excess aqueous humidity or "cold" moisture believed to exist within the liquid metal</term> is as follows: when the mercury is mixed with the calxes from which it is to be sublimated, it should be ground and blended with them by **imbibition** <term>the process of soaking or saturating a solid in a liquid until it is absorbed</term> with **vinegar** <note>original: "acetum"</note> or another similar substance, until no trace of the liquid mercury is visible. PART FOUR.

D 6

...[since] such as the cleansing will be, so also will the perfection follow through it in its **projection** <term>the final stage of the alchemical process where the "philosopher's stone" or elixir is cast upon molten base metal to transmute it</term> upon each of the imperfect bodies, and even upon the **mercury** <note>original: "vivum"; referring to *argentum vivum*</note> itself which has not been prepared. ->*On the sublimation of Marcasite.*<-

### CHAPTER 46.

We have now sufficiently handed down the summary of the sublimation of mercury <note>original: "argenti vivi"</note>. Now let us pass to the sublimation of **marcasite** <term>a common alchemical term for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrite, believed to contain the "seeds" of metals</term> itself. We say, therefore, that a twofold sublimation of marcasite occurs. For the first is performed without **ignition** <note>meaning without high heat that causes the substance to glow or burn</note> in an **aludel** <term>a pear-shaped ceramic or glass vessel used to collect sublimated vapors</term>. The other is performed with ignition, and this is because it contains within itself a twofold substance: namely, one that is pure sulfur, not fixed in its nature; and another that is fixed mercury, which is moderately prepared. The first is useful in the same way as sulfur; the second is useful as **"killed" mercury** <note>original: "argentum vivum mortificatum"; mercury that has been chemically altered or "fixed" so that it no longer appears as a liquid metal</note> that has been moderately prepared.

Let us therefore take this final substance from it; since through it we are excused from the necessity of taking up **mercury** <note>original: "argenti vivi"</note> and from the labor of its **mortification** <term>the alchemical process of "killing" or changing the outward form of a metal to "fix" it or prepare it for further work</term>. The whole method of its sublimation, then, is that it should be ground, and placed in an **aludel** <term>a series of pear-shaped earthenware pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</term>, and all its sulfur should be sublimated without **ignition** <note>original: "ignitione"; meaning low heat that does not cause the vessel to glow red</note>, always removing what has been sublimated from it very frequently, for the reason already stated. Then, the vigor of the fire is to be increased successively until the ignition of the aludel and its **marcasite** <term>a mineral, likely iron pyrite, which alchemists believed contained the essential seeds of metals</term>.

The first sublimation of marcasite ought to be performed in a sublimation vessel until the sulfur has departed; and afterward, one should proceed in that vessel successively and in an orderly fashion, until everything in it that consists of sulfur has gone out. You will be able to prove this by these manifest experiments: for when all its sulfur has been raised, you will see the color of that which is subsequently sublimated change into the brightest white mixed with a very clear sky-blue color.

You may also test it in another way: because that which is of a sulfurous nature will burn and emit a flame like sulfur; however, that which is sublimated second, once all the sulfur has been raised, will neither catch fire nor show any properties of sulfur, but rather those of mercury mortified in the repetition of sublimation. Let us therefore collect that substance by its own method of sublimation: which is that a very solid and well-baked earthen vessel be made to the length of half a stature—

# PART FOUR

...of a man’s height, and to a width through which a hand may enter. Let its base be made so that it can be separated and joined, resembling a single, very deep flat dish. We have set these things down in the summary of our work, where we have sufficiently described the various types of all instruments to you. 

Surround the top of the furnace with a ring <note>original: "rota"; literally a 'wheel,' used here as a circular collar or furnace lid</note> having a hole in its center the size of the vessel, through which that vessel can pass. Seal the joints around the vessel and the furnace with lute, so that the escaping fire does not hinder you or the attachment of the **sublimation** <term>the alchemical process where a solid substance is heated into a vapor and then condenses back into a solid on a cooler surface higher up in the vessel</term>. Leave only four small windows in the ring, which can be opened and closed as needed, through which coals may also be added to the furnace at times. And in the sides of the furnace...

<header>OF THE FIRST BOOK</header>

...openings, and similarly four other intermediate ones below them, through which coals may also be placed: and let six or eight holes be left, the size of a pinky finger, which should never be closed, so that the furnace may freely rid itself of smoke through them. And let those holes be located at the junction where the furnace meets its internal grate <note>original: "rota"; literally 'wheel,' referring here to a circular iron grate</note>. The furnace is indeed one of great ignition <note>original: "magnæ ignitionis"; meaning it is capable of reaching very high temperatures</note>: its side-walls <term>spondilia; the structural supports or tiers of the furnace walls</term> should be two cubits high.

In the middle, let there be an iron wheel, perforated with very tiny and numerous holes, firmly secured with **lute**. The tops of these holes should be narrower, and successively wider toward the bottom in the manner of a rounded pyramid; this is so that ash, coals, and other waste materials may fall through them more freely: and let these holes be left continually open for the intake of air. For the free intake of a large quantity of air through the lower holes is the primary cause of great heat throughout the furnace. Practice this, and you shall find it to be true.

The reason for the length of the vessel is so that a great portion of it may extend outside the fire and be cooled: thus, the vapors raised through **sublimation** <term>the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then allowing it to collect as a solid again on a cooler surface</term> may find a place of cooling and adhere there, and not find a way to escape or be destroyed. This fact is well known to the person who, having attempted sublimation in short **aludels** <term>earthenware pots used in the sublimation process, often stacked together</term>, found nothing of the sublimated substance: because, owing to the shortness of the vessel, the heat of the fire was uniform throughout its entire length.

<summary>This page describes the precise engineering of a high-heat furnace, emphasizing the need for a conical grate to manage airflow and ash, and explains why a long vessel is necessary to create a temperature gradient for successful sublimation.</summary>
<keywords>furnace construction, sublimation, airflow, aludel, heat management, laboratory apparatus, chemical vapors</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the sublimation process, moving from the construction of the furnace to the specific properties and glazing of the aludel vessel, as well as the method for testing the progress of the work.</meta>

# PART FOUR.

<meta>catchword: Descript- <note>original: "Defcri-"; likely for "Descriptio," meaning Description</note></meta>

And so, the substance being **sublimated** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as a solid again on a cooler surface</term> turned into the form of a vapor; it stood there and did not stick to the side, but instead gradually escaped through the pores of the vessel. Therefore, in all sublimation processes, the greater part of the vessel should extend into a cool area. The reason for the **glazing** <term>vitrification; the process of coating the ceramic with a glass-like finish to seal it</term> of the vessel is to ensure that the rising vapors do not encounter a porous wall in the **aludel** <term>a pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in sublimation</term>, through which they might penetrate and escape. Thus, the area where they are meant to stick is glazed, so they find no path for escape. 

However, the bottom of the aludel and the area around it are not glazed. This is because the base sits in intense heat <note>original: "magna ignitione"</note>, which would melt the glaze itself. If that glaze were to melt, the entire substance being sublimated would melt along with it and turn into glass. For it is the nature of glass to overwhelm all things and convert them to its own substance.

Having considered all these factors and their reasons, let the fire be maintained under the aludel until you are certain, through reliable testing <note>original: "experientia veridica"</note>, that the entire substance has ascended. This test <note>original: "experientia"</note> consists of inserting a well-fired clay rod which has a small hole in its tip—about the size of a pinky finger—extending to its middle. This is placed near the material being sublimated and held there for a short time before being removed. If any of the material being sublimated sticks to the hole, it is certain that the process is not yet complete. If nothing sticks, then the sublimation is finished. You may use this same practical method to verify the completion of all such sublimation processes.

<summary>This page explains the necessity of glazing (vitrifying) the upper parts of an aludel to prevent vapors from escaping, while leaving the bottom unglazed to prevent it from melting into glass. It also describes a diagnostic test using a clay rod to determine when the sublimation process is finished.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, aludel, vitrification, glazing, laboratory apparatus, chemical testing, clay rod, vapor management</keywords>

### *On the sublimation of Magnesia, Tutia, and the imperfect bodies.*

### CHAPTER XLVII.

The purpose for the sublimation of **magnesia** <term>magnesia: in medieval alchemy, this often referred to manganese dioxide or other dark ores used in glass-making</term> and **tutia** <term>tutia: also known as "tutty," this was crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term> is the same as the sublimation of marcasite which we have previously described. Indeed, none of these substances can be sublimated without intense heating <note>original: "ignitione magna"</note>. Therefore, they all share a single purpose for their sublimation, governed by the same causes and the same methods of testing; they follow one general order. This is because whatever must be sublimated by means of intense heat ought to be sublimated without adding external **dregs** <term>feces: the sedimentary waste or impurities; here referring to additives or fluxes that might be used in other processes</term>. These substances already contain sufficient—even excessive—dregs within themselves. The proof of this is the difficulty with which they are sublimated, even when subjected to great heat.

Furthermore, all "bodies diminished from perfection" <note>original: "corpora à perfectione diminuta"; this refers to base metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed were imperfect versions of gold and silver</note> are sublimated according to this same procedure. No difference occurs, except that the fire for sublimation is more intense for these metals than it is for magnesia, marcasite, and tutia. Nor do these metals differ significantly among themselves in their sublimations, except that some require the mixing in of a substance to help "lift" them <note>original: "sublevantis ea"; likely referring to an additive that lowers the boiling point or physically carries the vapor</note>, while others do not. This is done solely to make their rising easier. There is, however, only one special

<summary>This page explains that magnesia, tutia, and base metals require intense heat for sublimation due to their inherent impurities. It notes that while the process is largely the same for all these substances, base metals require even higher temperatures and occasionally additives to facilitate the vaporizing process.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, aludel, marcasite, magnesia, tutia, imperfect bodies, base metals, alchemy, furnace, ignition</keywords>

PARS QVARTA.
<meta>catchword: illius <note>The catchword "illius" indicates the beginning of the next page's first sentence.</note></meta>

... [spe]cial <note>original: "cialis"; the completion of "specialis" from the previous page</note> sublimation in the consideration of bodies, which is a best practice discovered by experience: namely, that there should not be a large quantity of the substance to be sublimated in the bottom of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic vessel used in alchemy to collect vapors during sublimation</term>. This is because an excessive amount of material hinders the sublimation process. Furthermore, the bottom of the sublimation aludel should be flat rather than concave, so that the substance can be spread evenly and thinly across the base, allowing it to be elevated <note>original: "elevari"; in this context, to rise as vapor</note> equally and abundantly in all its parts.

Certain bodies, however, require the addition of an "elevating" agent <note>original: "rei elevantis"</note>. These are **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term>, due to the slowness with which they melt. **Venus** requires **tutty** <term>tutia: crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term>, while **Mars** requires **arsenic**. With these additives, they are easily elevated, as they are most compatible with these substances. Therefore, let the sublimation be performed after considering these factors, as is done with tutty and similar materials. Let their sublimation be arranged in the same order as we described for **marcasite** <term>marcasite: a general medieval term for various metallic sulfides, such as iron pyrite</term>, following the same principles and proven methods previously mentioned.

->### OF THE FIRST BOOK<-

[The cause] of it <note>The Latin "illius" refers back to the process of descension introduced at the end of the previous section.</note> is threefold. First, that when some matter is enclosed in that vessel called the **chemical descensory** <term>descensory: a vessel with a perforated bottom used to filter or separate molten metal from impurities by letting it drip downward</term>, it may descend through its opening after it has undergone **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting a solid substance into a liquid</term>; by its descent, we are made certain that it has undergone the correct melting. The second cause, however, is so that **weak bodies** <note>In alchemy, "weak bodies" usually refers to metals with low melting points or those easily damaged by prolonged heat, such as lead or tin.</note> may be preserved from burning by that descent, after their **reduction** <term>reduction: the alchemical process of returning a "calx" (powdered oxide) back to its solid metallic state</term> from their **calxes** <term>calx: the powdery substance, or oxide, left after a metal has been burned or "calcined" in fire</term>.

For when we attempt to reduce weak bodies from their calxes, we cannot reduce every part of them at the same time, but only a certain portion. If, therefore, that part which was first reduced into a metallic body waited for the reduction of the whole, a greater quantity of it would vanish <note>original: "evanesceret"; meaning it would vaporize or be lost to the intensity of the furnace</note> through the fire. It was necessary, therefore, to devise a way so that as soon as a part is reduced, it is removed from the fire. This happens to be achieved through the descensory. The third cause for the invention of this "descension" was the **purification** of bodies from every foreign thing. For the melted body descends clean, and leaves every foreign thing behind in the cavity of the vessel.

These three causes having been investigated, let us describe the method of descension with its instrument, as previously noted. We say, therefore, that its form is such that its bottom is sharp: and its walls terminate equally and without hesitation into the sharpness of the bottom, in the form of a **rounded pyramid** <note>original: "pyramidis rotundæ"; a geometric description of a cone</note>: so that each of the fusible materials can [descend] freely without sticking,

This is because it will stand more firmly, endure the fire longer, and provide a better melting; very often, one can test whether the matter has melted by tilting the head of the descensory.

The method of this purification is the same as the method for the descensory. Therefore, by using the pellet, we are excused from using the descensory. It holds the dregs of the metals just like the descensory, and even better; for that reason, we shall describe its method. This is done so that when the **medicine of perfection** <term>medicina: the alchemical agent or elixir used to transform "base" metals into "noble" ones</term> is applied, the bodies may be better and more perfectly transformed by it. Now, however, let us set down the description of the descensory.
