->*On distillation, its causes, and its three kinds: by the alembic, by descent, and by filter.*<-

### Chapter XLIX.

It is therefore fitting that, following our purpose, we deliver a discourse on **distillation**, along with its causes. Distillation, then, is the elevation of watery vapors in its own vessel. Distillation is varied, however, according to the diversity of the things to be distilled. For some is performed by fire, and some <margin>by cold</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of distillation, specifically detailing the methods involving heat versus those without heat, following the introduction to alchemical vessels on the previous page.</meta>

<header>PART FOUR.</header>

...[distilla]tions, indeed, are some without fire. That which is done by fire, however, is found to be of two kinds: one is by **elevation** <note>the rising of vapors</note> into an **alembic** <term>alembic: a traditional alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling volatile substances</term>; the other is by **chemical descent** <note>original: "descensum chimiæ"; a method where the substance is heated from above so the extract drips downward</note>, through which means **oil** is extracted from **vegetable** matter. And the general cause for the invention of any kind of distillation is the **purification** of liquids from the dross of their own turbulence, and the preservation of the liquid from **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrefactione"; the process of decay or decomposition</note> or corruption. For we see that a distilled thing, by whatever kind of distillation, is made purer and is better guarded against decay. 

The special reason for the invention of that distillation which is done by **ascent** into the alembic is the seeking of **pure water** from dregs, the experience of which is well known: for we see that water distilled two or three times through an alembic has no earthy dross mixed into it. The cause for the invention of this pure water was for the **imbibition** <term>imbibitio: the process of soaking or saturating a solid substance with a liquid until it can absorb no more</term> of **spirits** <note>original: "spirituum"; in alchemy, these are volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or ammonia that can be "raised" by heat</note> and medicinal powders. This was done so that, whenever we require soaking, we might have pure water that does not leave behind its own dross after it has been resolved <note>evaporated or broken down</note>; for such dross could infect or corrupt our **medicines** <term>medicina: the alchemical agents or "elixirs" used to transform and perfect base substances</term> and cleansed spirits. 

The cause, however, for the invention of that distillation which is done by **descent** was the extraction of pure oil in its own nature. This is because oil could not be obtained in its combustible nature through ascent; the inquiry was of this kind so that its **color**, which is mixed with its substance, might be retained. For this color can be helpful in certain cases.

->### OF THE FIRST BOOK<-

The reason for the invention of that distillation which is performed through a **filter** <note>original: "filtrum"; often a piece of wool or felt used to wick liquid from one vessel to another</note> without the use of fire was solely for the clarity of the water. Let us therefore now speak of the modes of all distillations, with their causes and manifest experiences.

Of that distillation which occurs by **ascent** into an **alembic** <term>alembic: a traditional alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling volatile substances</term>, there are two modes. One is performed in an earthen pot full of **ashes** <note>original: "cineribus"; a method of indirect heating similar to a modern sand bath, providing high but even temperatures</note>. The other, however, is performed in its own bronze vessel full of water, arranged in its proper order with grass or **wool** <note>original: "lanositate"; used as cushioning to prevent the glass from cracking against the metal</note>, so that the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped lower vessel of a still where the substance is heated</term> or the distilling alembic is not broken before the process is brought to completion. 

That distillation which is done with ashes is completed with a greater, stronger, and sharper fire; however, that which is done with water is done with a mild and slow heat. For water does not take on the sharpness of the fire in the same way as ashes. Therefore, through the method involving ashes, it happens that colors and coarser earthy parts are elevated more. With the method involving water, however, the results are subtler, colorless, and more closely approaching the nature of simple wateriness. 

Thus, a subtler separation is achieved through that which is performed with water than through that which is drawn out by ashes. Experience has shown this to be true for the practitioner who, when he had distilled **oil** through the ashes, received the oil in the **receiver** <note>original: "receptaculo"; the vessel that catches the distilled liquid</note> barely altered. Wishing to truly separate its parts, he came to the necessity of distilling it through water. And then, through the repetition of this process, he separated the oil into its **elemental parts** <note>the four basic components: earth, air, fire, and water</note>, and a most white...

By means of that which is done through a **filter**, the clarity of any liquid is achieved. 

All these things are manifest and well-known even to those with very little knowledge or experience. Once these things are fitted, let fire be applied until it distills. The alembic and its cucurbit should, in fact, be made of glass. Let the fire be increased according to the needs of the distillation, until the whole substance to be distilled is seen to have been processed, with a strong application of fire as required by the distillation.

Next, the distilling vessel, fitted with its alembic as we described above, should be placed upon that layer. # PART FOUR

...bit nearly to the neck of the alembic, lest the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped vessel containing the raw material</term> be broken. And over the straw itself, thin rods should be scattered, and upon these rods let heavy stones be placed, which by their weight press down the cucurbit, the **alembic** <term>alembic: the cooling cap of the still</term>, and the straw, holding them firmly and steadily against the bottom of the pot. This is done so they do not float or be tossed about by the water; such rising would cause the vessel to break and the loss of the substance being distilled. Afterward, water should be poured among the straw until the pot is full, and fire should be applied beneath until the entire substance is distilled.

The arrangement for that which is done by **descent** <note>original: "per descensum"; a method where heat is applied from above, forcing the product—usually a heavy oil—to flow downward through a hole into a receiver</note> is as follows: a glass **descensory** <note>original: "descensorium"; a specialized vessel for downward distillation</note> should be made with its own lid. Let both be **luted** <note>original: "lutetur"; sealed with airtight clay or paste</note>, and the material to be distilled be placed inside. Then, let a fire be built over the head of the vessel. Its distillate will then flow down through its opening into a vessel placed beneath it.

The arrangement for that which is done by **filter** <note>original: "per filtrum"; a process using capillary action, where a strip of cloth or wick draws liquid from one vessel to another, acting as a structural purifier</note> is that the liquid to be distilled be put into a glass or stone **basin** <note>original: "concham"; literally "shell," used here to describe a shallow vessel</note>. Let the wider end of a thoroughly washed and moistened filter be placed into the liquid, reaching the bottom of the basin. Let the narrower end of it hang outside the mouth of the basin, and beneath that end of the filter, place a vessel to receive the distillation. When the filter begins to drip, the water with which it was first moistened will distill first; once that has ceased, the liquor to be distilled follows after it.

->*On **Calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical state, typically to reduce it to a powder</term>, both of bodies and spirits, including its causes and methods.*<-

### CHAPTER 50.

Therefore, after our account of distillation, let us turn our discourse to calcination. **Calcination** is the reduction of a substance to powder by means of fire, through the removal of the moisture that binds its parts together. The reason for its invention is so that the corrupting and defiling **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitas"; in alchemy, an excess of "sulfur" was believed to cause impurities or "leprosy" in metals</note> may be destroyed by fire. This process varies according to the diversity of the materials to be calcined. Thus, bodies are calcined, and spirits <term>spirits: volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire</term> are calcined, as well as other substances foreign to their nature; however, this is done with different intentions.

Because there are two kinds of imperfect bodies <note>original: "corpora imperfecta"; metals other than gold and silver, which were considered "perfect"</note>—namely the hard ones, such as **Venus** <term>Venus: Copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: Iron</term>, and the soft ones, such as **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: Tin</term> and **Saturn** <term>Saturn: Lead</term>—all of which undergo calcination, it was necessary for them to be calcined with different aims, both general and specific. In a general sense, however, they are all calcined with one primary purpose: so that the corrupting and defiling sulfurousness may be removed and destroyed.

<vocab>calcinatio, distillatio, sulphureitas, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturnus, corpora imperfecta</vocab>

<summary>This page defines calcination as the use of fire to pulverize substances by removing their internal moisture. It explains that the primary goal of this process is to strip away "sulfurous" impurities from both hard and soft base metals.</summary>

<keywords>calcination, alchemy, sulfurousness, base metals, copper, iron, tin, lead, purification by fire</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of calcination from Chapter 50, explaining how fire purifies metals by removing combustible sulfur and earthy impurities, with a specific focus on "hardening" soft metals like tin.</meta>

# PART FOUR.

...be destroyed <note>original: "deleatur"; the word likely began on the previous page as "deleatur" (to be destroyed)</note> by fire. For in this way all combustible **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitas"; in alchemy, sulfur was the principle of combustion and impurity</note>, from whatever source, is burned away; this cannot be removed without **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>, because the body itself is solid. Due to this solidity and the way the sulfurousness is hidden within the continuity of the substance of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum)</term>, it is protected from combustion by that very substance.

Therefore, it was necessary to break the continuity of these bodies and the quicksilver within them, so that the fire, reaching more freely to every smallest part, might be able to burn the sulfurousness out of it, and so the continuity of the mixed quicksilver would no longer protect it. 

The general reason for the invention of the calcination of bodies is purification from **earthiness** <term>earthiness: "terreitate," the presence of impure, non-metallic earthy matter</term>. For we find that through the repetition of calcination and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid metal</term>, bodies are cleansed and rendered more **fixed** <term>fixed: "figuntur," made stable in fire and resistant to evaporation</term>, as we shall explain in the following sections.

There is, however, a specific calcination for soft bodies <note>original: "corpora mollia"; typically referring to lead and tin</note>. In addition to the two purposes already mentioned, the intention here is to use the process to harden them and make them withstand the fire. This is achieved through the clever repetition of calcination upon them, which we shall find it useful to define in the coming tradition. For we find that by this skill, they are clearly hardened. This hardening is seen most clearly and quickly in **Jupiter** <note>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal tin</note>. 

The reason for the invention of the calcination of **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire</term> is so that they may be better fixed and more easily dissolved into water. Because every kind of calcined material is more fixed than...

->*On **Calcination**, both of bodies and spirits, with its causes and methods.*<-

### CHAPTER 50.

After the account of **distillation** <term>distillation: the process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and condensing it back into liquid</term>, let us direct our discourse to **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to achieve a state of powder</term>. Calcination is, therefore, the reduction of a substance to powder by fire, through the removal of the moisture that holds the parts together. The reason for its invention is so that the corrupting and defiling **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitas"; in alchemy, sulfur was seen as a principle of combustion and impurity that could "stain" metals</note> may be destroyed by fire. 

This process is diversified according to the variety of things to be calcined. For "bodies" (metals) are calcined, and "spirits" (volatile substances like sulfur or arsenic) are calcined, as well as other things foreign to their nature, though with different intentions. Since there are two kinds of imperfect bodies—namely, the **hard**, such as **Venus** <note>original: "Venus"; the alchemical name for copper</note> and **Mars** <note>original: "Mars"; the alchemical name for iron</note>, and the **soft**, such as **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jupiter"; the alchemical name for tin</note> and **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturn"; the alchemical name for lead</note>—all of which are calcined, it was necessary for them to be calcined with different intentions, both general and specific. In a general sense, however, they are all calcined with one goal: so that the corrupting and defiling sulfurousness may be removed and destroyed.

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 50, defining calcination as a method of using fire to remove moisture and "sulfurous" impurities from substances. It categorizes metals into hard (copper and iron) and soft (tin and lead) types to explain why different calcination methods are required.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, distillation, sulfurousness, copper, iron, tin, lead, metallurgy, purification</keywords>

It establishes that the "solidity" of metals and their mercury content protects hidden sulfur from being burned away unless the material is first broken down.</meta>

# PART FOUR.

E 2

<meta>catchword: non</meta>

...be destroyed by fire. In this way, all **combustible sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitas adustiva"; in alchemy, this refers to the impure, flammable component believed to cause metals to "rot" or corrode when heated</note>, from whatever source it arises, is burned away. This impurity cannot be removed without **calcination** <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> because the metallic body itself is solid. Due to this solidity and the way the sulfurousness is hidden within the continuous substance of the **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum), believed by alchemists to be the fundamental "liquid" component of all metals</term>, the impurity is protected from being reached and consumed by the fire.

Therefore, it was necessary to break the continuity of these metallic bodies and the quicksilver within them. This allows the fire to reach more freely into even the smallest parts of the substance, enabling it to burn away the sulfurousness without the surrounding mercury protecting it.

The general reason for the invention of the calcination of bodies is **purification from earthiness** <note>original: "à terreitate depuratio"; the removal of "earthy" dross or non-metallic slag</note>. We have found that metallic bodies are cleansed and become more **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates or changes</term> through the repetition of calcination and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of melting a calcined powder back into its solid metallic form</term>, as we shall discuss in the following sections.

There is, however, a specific reason for the calcination of **soft bodies** <note>referring to metals with low melting points, like lead or tin</note>. In addition to the two reasons mentioned above, the intent is to use the process to **harden** them and make them better able to withstand fire. This is achieved through the skillful repetition of calcination, which we will explain in the upcoming teaching. We have clearly seen that they are hardened by this method, but **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jupiter"; the alchemical name for the metal tin</note> is hardened more obviously and more quickly than the others.

Furthermore, the reason for the calcination of **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances such as sulfur, arsenic, or mercury that easily vaporize when heated</term> is so that they may be better fixed and more easily dissolved into water. For every kind of calcined substance is more fixed than...

...not calcined, and are more easily dissolved. This is because the parts of the material that have been **calcined** <term>calcination: the reduction of a solid substance into a fine powder through the application of high heat</term> are made finer and more subtle by the fire; thus, they mix more easily with liquids and are converted into a liquid state. If you test this through experience, you will find it to be exactly so.

The calcination of other substances, however, is performed according to the requirements for preparing **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances—such as mercury, sulfur, or sal ammoniac—that can vaporize when heated</term> and **bodies** <term>bodies: the solid, non-volatile metals such as gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, and lead</term>. We shall define these more extensively in the sections that follow. However, none of these [preparations] are themselves the final **perfection** <note>original: "perfectione"; here referring to the final completion of the alchemical Great Work or the Stone</note>.

The method of calcination is therefore diverse, owing to the variety of things that must be calcined. Indeed, metallic bodies are calcined differently than spirits or other external materials; even the bodies themselves, being different from one another, are likewise calcined in different ways.

**Soft bodies** <note>original: "corpora mollia"; in alchemical practice, this usually refers to lead and tin, which melt at lower temperatures compared to "hard" metals like iron</note> follow one general method for the purpose of calcination: namely, that both can be calcined by fire alone, or by the **sharpness of salt** <note>original: "salis acuitatem"; the corrosive or reactive power of salt used as a flux or reagent</note>, whether they are already prepared or not.

The first method is performed in this way: take an **earthenware vessel** <note>original: "vas terreum"</note> that has been well-fired and shaped like a **shallow dish** <note>original: "paropsidis"; a Greek-derived term for a side-dish or plate</note>. Its structure must be firm and fitted to the **calcination furnace** <term>calcination furnace: a specialized oven, often an athanor, designed to hold a steady heat for the purpose of reducing metals to ash or powder</term> so that coals can be placed and blown with bellows beneath it. 

Place the **lead** and **tin** into the bottom of that vessel. The vessel should sit firmly upon an iron tripod or three stone pillars; it should also be secured to the walls of its furnace by three or four stones, wedging it...

If it should be **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for lead</term>, let it be placed in a greater fire until its calx is changed into a very bright yellow color. If, however, it be **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for tin</term>, let it be similarly exposed to the fire and kept there, and let it remain until its calx is changed into a complete whiteness. 

In this, however, we make the craftsman diligent, because Saturn easily returns from its calx back into a body <note>i.e., it easily reduces back into solid metallic lead</note>, but Jupiter does so with great difficulty. Therefore, lest it should happen that he errs in the exposure of the calx of Saturn—after its first reduction to powder—to a greater fire, let him take care that it does not return into a body before its calx is perfected. This is because it requires a tempering of the fire and its successive increase little by little, with caution, until it is fixed in the state of calx—that is, so that it does not easily return into a body—so that a greater fire may then be applied to it.

->*On Solution.*<-

### CHAPTER 51.

Our expanded discourse on solution <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state</term> indicates that the solution of a dry thing is its reduction into water. (Solution is the reduction of a solid and dry thing into the essence of water.) We say, therefore, that every perfection of solution is brought about with the most subtle waters—especially those that are extremely acute, sharp, and pungent <note>original: "ponticis"; an alchemical term for substances with an astringent or "biting" quality</note>, having no dregs. Examples include distilled vinegar, wild grapes, sour plums, and pears of great sharpness, as well as pomegranates similarly distilled, and other things like them. 

The reason for the invention of this process was the thinning out of those substances which have neither suitable fusion <term>fusion: the ability to be melted by heat</term> nor ingression <term>ingression: the ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate and transform a metal</term>. Without solution, much of the usefulness of spirits and substances of a similar nature would be lost. For everything that is dissolved must necessarily possess the nature of salt, or alum, or something similar. It is their nature to allow for fusion before their vitrification <term>vitrification: the transformation of a substance into glass through intense heat</term>. Therefore, even dissolved spirits will provide a similar fusion. Since, therefore, they agree much in their nature with bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven base metals</note>, and with each other, [they have...]

...once fusion has been achieved, it is necessary for the substance to penetrate bodies and, by penetrating them, to transform <note>original: "transmutare"</note> them. Indeed, one cannot arrive at this stage without the mastery <term>magistery: the expert skill or the specific chemical agent required for alchemical transformation</term>. This specifically means that after the substance has undergone solution and coagulation, it must be combined with a purified (but not "fixed" <note>a "fixed" substance is one that has been made resistant to the action of fire</note>) spirit <term>spirit: a volatile substance that can be vaporized by heat</term>. It must be sublimated <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as a solid again to purify it</term> with that spirit as many times as necessary until the spirit remains joined with it. This grants the substance a faster fusion and protects it from turning into glass <term>vitrification: the process of turning into a glass-like state, which was often seen as an undesirable "dead end" in these chemical processes</term> during the melting process.

For it is in the very nature of spirits not to be turned into glass, and to preserve any mixture they are part of from glass-making, as long as they remain within it. Therefore, a spirit that has better preserved its own essential "spiritual" <note>meaning its volatility and activity</note> nature will offer better protection against vitrification. A spirit that is merely purified preserves this nature more effectively than one that has been purified, fixed, calcined, and dissolved. For this reason, it is necessary to mix them together. This results in a successful fusion, penetration <term>ingression: the ability of an alchemical substance to enter and "dye" the pores of a metal</term>, and a permanent fixation.

Furthermore, we can prove from the works of nature that only those things possessing the nature of salts, alums <term>alum: a mineral salt used historically in tanning and dyeing; here used as a category of chemical behavior</term>, and similar substances are capable of being dissolved. In considering all of nature's operations, we find that nothing else dissolves except these. Therefore, whatever is dissolved must necessarily be dissolved through that specific nature. But because we observe that everything truly calcined <term>calcination: reducing a substance to a powder through intense heat</term> is eventually dissolved through the repetition of calcination and solution, it is clearly proven that all calcined things approach the nature of salts or alums. Consequently, it is necessary that they [approximate] each other in their properties...

# Part Four.

E 6

<meta>catchword: Regarding</meta>

...is to be accompanied <note>original: "conconitari," likely a transcription error for "concomitari"</note>. Indeed, the method of **solution** <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid</term> is twofold: one is by **warm dung**, and the other is by **boiling water**. Their purpose and effect are the same. 

The method using **dung** is as follows: place the **calcined substance** <term>calcination: the process of heating a material to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> into a **glass flask**, and pour over it twice its amount of **distilled vinegar** or something similar. The mouth of the vessel must be sealed very well so that no vapor can escape <note>original: "respirare," literally "to breathe"</note>. It should then be left for three days buried under a large quantity of warm dung so that it stays thoroughly heated. Afterward, the dissolved portion is to be removed by **filtering** <term>distillation by filter: an early chemical technique for filtration, often using a strip of felt or cloth to draw liquid out of a vessel</term>. The part that did not dissolve should be calcined again, and after that second calcination, it should be dissolved again in the same way with twice its amount of fresh distilled vinegar or another substance. This continues until, through the repetition of the work, the whole substance—or the greater part as required—is dissolved.

The method which is done by **boiling water** is faster and better. It is performed by arranging the calcined substance in a flask with vinegar in a similar way, with the opening sealed so it cannot vent. This flask is then buried in a **cauldron** full of water and straw, following the instructions we previously gave for the method of distillation by water. Afterward, a fire should be lit beneath it until the water boils for an hour. Following this, the dissolved part should be filtered and kept separately; the undissolved part is calcined again and dissolved again in the same order, until the whole is dissolved through repetition. The description of what has just been said is as follows:

| **solution** | **warm dung** | **boiling water** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **calcined material** | **glass flask** | **distilled vinegar** |
| **filtration** | **cauldron** | |

<summary>This page describes two practical laboratory methods for dissolving calcined materials: using the steady, gentle heat of a dung pit or the faster heat of a boiling water bath (bain-marie). Both processes involve sealing the substance in vinegar and repeating the cycle of heating and calcination until the material is fully dissolved.</summary>

### *On Coagulation, and its causes, and the various ways of coagulating Quicksilver, and of dissolved medicines.*

### CHAPTER 52.

**Coagulation** <term>coagulation: the alchemical process of turning a liquid substance into a solid</term> is the reduction of a liquid matter to a solid substance, through the removal of wateriness <note>original: "aqueitatis"</note>, or the deprivation of moisture. There is, therefore, a twofold reason for its discovery: one is the hardening of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury (Latin: "argentum vivum")</term>; the other is the separation of **dissolved medicines** <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" often refers to a substance or tincture intended to "heal" or transform base metals into gold</note> from the wateriness mixed into them. 

The process varies according to the diversity of the things to be coagulated. For quicksilver requires one kind of coagulation, while medicines or dissolved things require another. Furthermore, the coagulation of quicksilver is twofold: one is through the deprivation of all the **innate moisture** <term>innate moisture: the essential fluid believed to be naturally present within a substance that gives it its form</term> from it; the other is through the **thickening** <term>thickening: or "inspissation," the process of making a liquid dense or viscous</term> of that same moisture until it hardens. 

However, congealing it happens with great difficulty and labor, requiring great industry and sharp-sightedness; and this is because of the strong union and composition it possesses. But we, my son <note>The author uses "fili" (son), a traditional way for alchemical masters to address their students or readers</note>, will recount to you in this chapter the entire clever method for that coagulation. Regarding the method for its coagulation, some have thought it could be achieved through its preservation or retention in a temperate heat <note>original: "ignis temperamento," referring to a controlled, steady heat</note>: who, when they thought they had congealed it,

<summary>This page defines coagulation as the removal of moisture to solidify a substance and introduces the specific challenges of hardening mercury due to its stable internal composition. It establishes that different alchemical substances require different methods of solidification, whether through total drying or thickening.</summary>
<keywords>coagulation, mercury, quicksilver, innate moisture, thickening, alchemical medicine, temperate heat</keywords>

...congealed it, yet after removing it from the fire, they found it to flow just as it did before. For this reason they were struck with astonishment and wonder, vehemently asserting that such a goal could not be reached. Others, however, assuming by necessity from natural principles that any moisture is converted into dryness through the heat of fire, imagined that by persevering and keeping the substance in its fire continually, it would eventually coagulate. Through this continuation, some succeeded in converting the substance into a white stone <note>original: "album lapidem"; referring to the *albedo*, the second major stage of the alchemical Great Work</note>, and others into a red stone <note>original: "rubeum lapidem"; referring to the *rubedo*, the final stage of the Great Work</note>; yet these stones had no power of fusion <term>fusion: the ability of a substance to melt and blend with other metals</term>, nor any ability of ingress <term>ingress: the chemical property allowing a substance to penetrate and transform the interior of a metal</term>. Because they could not judge the cause of these differences, they cast the work aside. 

Others still attempted to coagulate it using medicines <note>In this context, "medicines" are chemical reagents or catalysts intended to "heal" or transform base metals</note>, but they were deceived: either because the substances did not coagulate at all, or because the moisture was imperceptibly thinned by the heat and vanished into smoke, or because their coagulation did not take the form of any solid body. Being ignorant of the cause of these variations, they fell into despair. 

Finally, others, by composing artificial or clever medicines, succeeded in coagulating it through projection <term>projection: the final stage of the alchemical work where the "medicine" or Philosopher's Stone is cast upon molten base metal to transform it</term>. Yet their coagulation was not useful, for they converted it only into an imperfect body; and because of their own lack of knowledge, they were similarly unable to see the reason for this failure. It is therefore necessary for us to explain the causes of these things, so that the artisan may attain the mastery <term>mastery: the "magisterium," or the completed secret of the alchemical art</term> of its coagulation.

<summary>This page describes the various failed attempts by alchemists to coagulate mercury, noting that some produced brittle "stones" that would not melt, while others saw their work vanish into smoke. The author promises to reveal the correct methods for achieving a solid, useful result.</summary>

...might come about. As has already been sufficiently explained by us, quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> is of a uniform substance. For this reason, it is not possible in a short span of time to remove its wateriness or to thicken it by keeping it in the fire. Thus, excessive haste was the cause of the first error. Since its substance is indeed subtle <note>In alchemy, "subtle" refers to a substance that is very fine, penetrating, and prone to evaporation</note>, it withdraws from the fire. Therefore, an excess of fire is the cause of error for those from whom the substance flees. 

It mixes easily with sulfur, arsenic, and marcasite <term>marcasite: a term used historically for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrite or "fool's gold," thought to contain the seeds of metals</term>, because of its natural affinity with them. For this reason, through these substances, it appears to be coagulated <term>coagulated: the process of turning a liquid or volatile substance into a solid</term>—not in the form of a metallic body, but as quicksilver mixed with lead, or in the form of antimony or something similar. For these additives, being volatile <note>original: "fugitiva"; literally "fleeing." In alchemy, this describes substances that evaporate or sublimate when heated</note> themselves, cannot hold the mercury in the struggle of the fire until it reaches the nature of a solid body; instead, they flee with it through the strike of the fire. This is the cause of error for those who attempt to coagulate it in this way. 

Likewise, quicksilver certainly has much humidity united within it, which cannot be separated from it except through the cautious violence of fire and by maintaining it in its own fire. "Its own fire" is that heat which they increase according to the demands of the substance's endurance; by this, they remove its humidity while leaving behind that part which is sufficient for metallic fusion <term>fusion: the capacity of a metal to melt and blend; if a substance is "fixed" too much, it becomes a dry powder or stone that cannot melt</term>. If this necessary part is removed, it will not melt. And this is the cause of error for those who convert it into a white stone or

### PART FOUR

...or they coagulate a red substance that cannot be melted. For quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> has parts of sulfur naturally mixed into it; some has more, while some has less. It is possible to remove this through artistic skill. Since it is the property of sulfur to create a red or lemon-yellow color when combined with quicksilver—depending on the proportion of its quantity—as we see in **cinnabar**, then by removing the sulfur, the property of quicksilver will be to produce whiteness through the action of fire. This, therefore, is the cause of the variety of colors found in it after it has been coagulated into a **stone** <note>In alchemy, the "stone" refers to a solid, stable form of the substance, often the goal of the "Great Work" to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

It likewise has an unclean and sulfurous earthiness mixed in, which necessarily taints all its coagulations. This is the cause of error for those who coagulate it into an imperfect body <note>An "imperfect body" refers to base metals like lead or tin, which alchemists believed were "diseased" or unfinished versions of gold</note>. Therefore, due to the diversity of the medicines used for its coagulation, different bodies are created during the process. For if the medicine <term>medicina: a transmuting agent or substance added to catalyze the chemical change</term> or the quicksilver contains sulfur that is not fixed <term>fixed: "fixum"; a substance that is stable in fire and does not evaporate or "fly away"</term>, it is necessary that a soft body be created from them.

If, however, the sulfur is fixed, the body must be hard. If it is white, the body will be white; if red, it will be red. If it is a faint <note>original: "remissum"; meaning a diluted or pale version of the color</note> version of white or red, it must similarly become faint. If it is earthy or leaden <note>original: "lividum"; a bluish-gray or lead-like color</note>, it will be tainted. If not, then not. For every non-fixed sulfur creates a leaden color, whereas fixed sulfur does not, insofar as it is within it. Truly, its pure substance creates a pure body, but that which is not pure...

### OF THE FIRST BOOK

...but, on the contrary. This same diversity also occurs in quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"</note> alone, without the addition of sulfur, because of the different ways it is purified and prepared within medicines <term>medicinis: in alchemy, "medicines" are the chemical agents or elixirs used to transform or "heal" base metals into gold</term>. Thus, a variation or illusion arises because of the diversity of the medicine used. Sometimes, during its solidification <note>original: "coagulatione"</note>, lead is formed; at other times Jove <note>the planetary name for Tin</note>; at others Venus <note>Copper</note>; and at others Mars <note>Iron</note>. This happens because of impurity. At other times, however, Sol <note>the "Sun," meaning Gold</note> or Luna <note>the "Moon," meaning Silver</note> is produced, which must necessarily happen when the substance is of high quality and purity.

It is therefore solidified by frequently forcing it to precipitate to the bottom through the violence of its own fire, which easily removes its watery nature. This is achieved using a vessel of great length. As the substance rises [as vapor], the length of the vessel provides a place for it to cool, adhere, and rest within the vessel's joints <term>spondilibus: the segments or ridges in the neck of the apparatus</term>. Because of the vessel's length, the vapor finds no way to escape or exit, until it is repeatedly driven back down to the bottom by the intense heat of the fire. This process is repeated many times until the substance becomes fixed <term>fixum: a "fixed" substance is one that has become stable in fire and will no longer evaporate or sublimate</term>.

It is similarly solidified by keeping it for a long time in its fire within a glass vessel. This vessel should have a very long neck and a belly shaped like a flask <note>original: "ampullæ"</note>. The neck is kept open so that its moisture can evaporate through it. Finally, it can be solidified by a medicine suitable for it, the composition of which we shall describe to you...

# PART FOUR.

...we will describe <note>original: "narrabimus"</note> in the following pages as is appropriate. Now, its medicine <term>medicina: in alchemy, a "medicine" is a chemical agent or elixir used to transform, heal, or "fix" a substance into a higher state</term> is that which adheres most strongly to it in its depths, and is mixed with it in its smallest parts <note>referring to a total union at a microscopic level</note> before its flight from the fire. Therefore, it is necessary that this medicine be created from things that are compatible with it. Such things are all metallic bodies, as well as sulfur and arsenic. 

But because we have seen none of these bodies solidify <note>original: "coagulare"</note> it with a firm and true solidification while in their own nature—but rather, it flees from them through the force of the fire, no matter how great their compatibility—we have therefore considered that no body in its own nature adheres to it in its depths. Therefore, it is necessary for that medicine to be of a more subtle substance and more liquid fusion than the bodies themselves are. 

Similarly, regarding the spirits <term>spiritus: volatile substances like quicksilver, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from fire or vaporize easily</term>, we have not seen a firm and true solidification happen while they remain in their own nature, but only one that is fleeting, and full of corruption and blackness. This happens for two reasons: one because of the flight of the spirits, and the other because of the mixture of their earthy and combustible <note>original: "aduſtibilis"</note> substance. And so, through these things it is clearly concluded that from whatever thing its medicine is drawn, it must necessarily be of the most subtle and most pure...

### On Fixation.

### CHAPTER 53.

Fixation is the suitable adaptation of a substance that flees from the fire, so that it may withstand it <note>original: "rei fugientis ab igne ad ipsum conveniens aptatio," literally the fitting adaptation of a thing fleeing from fire to the fire itself</note>. The reason for its invention was so that every <term>tincture: a substance used to change the color or quality of a metal</term> and every alteration produced by the <term>medicine: the alchemical agent of transformation</term> might be made permanent in the material being changed, and not be altered or separated from it by the fire.

This process is likewise varied according to the variety of things to be fixed. These include certain "bodies diminished from perfection" <note>In alchemical theory, base metals were seen as "sick" or "imperfect" versions of gold</note>, such as <term>Saturn: lead</term>, <term>Jupiter: tin</term>, and <term>Venus: copper</term>; and "spirits," namely sulfur and arsenic in one grade; <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> in another; and in a third, <term>marcasite: a metallic mineral, often iron pyrite</term>, <term>magnesia: a mineral substance used in alchemical recipes</term>, and <term>tutia: zinc oxide</term>, and things of a similar nature.

These bodies diminished from perfection are fixed through their <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> and reduction. This is because they are freed by these processes from the combustible and volatile sulfurous elements that cause them to corrupt. We have spoken of this sufficiently in our discourse, namely in the chapter on calcination. Sulfur and arsenic, however, are fixed in two ways. The first is through the repetition of <term>sublimation: heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</term> over them in an <term>aludel: a series of pear-shaped pottery vessels used for sublimation</term> until they remain stable <note>original: "stent," literally "they stand," meaning they no longer vaporize</note>. Therefore, the intent of hastening their fixation is that you might devise a discovery...

<vocab>Fixation, tincture, medicine, Lead, Tin, Copper, sulfur, arsenic, mercury, marcasite, magnesia, tutia, calcination, reduction, aludel</vocab>

It categorizes various metals and minerals—such as lead, copper, and sulfur—according to how they must be treated to achieve this stability.</summary>

<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...sublimation through multiple repetitions in a short time. This is performed using two <term>aludels: pear-shaped glass or ceramic vessels used for distilling and condensing vapors</term>, with their double covers, in this order or manner: namely, that you should never cease from the act of sublimation until the substances have been fixed <note>original: "fixa fuerint," meaning rendered non-volatile and resistant to heat</note>. 

Therefore, let that which ascends into one cover be immediately cast into the other aludel, and so let it be repeated in alternating turns. Never allow the aludels to remain idle or the substance to cling to the <term>rims: the "spondilia" or joints where the vessel components meet</term>; rather, let them be in a continuous sublimation over the fire until their rising ceases. For the more quickly you can complete the multiple repetitions of sublimation, the more swiftly and better will they be fixed. 

And therefore a second mode of fixation was invented: which is through the <term>precipitation: here meaning the "casting down" or forcing down of vapors</term> of the substance to be sublimated toward the heat of the fire, so that it may be continually within it until it is fixed. 

And this is done in a long glass vessel whose bottom is made of earth <note>likely earthenware or clay-tempered glass</note>, and not of glass (since glass would shatter into pieces), joined to the vessel skillfully with a good seal. By means of an iron or stone spatula, when the substance has adhered to the rims of the vessel, let it be cast down to the bottom of the heat continually, by alternating turns, until it is fixed and rises no more. 

Truly, the method for the fixation of <term>quicksilver: mercury</term> is the same as the method for the fixation of sulfur and arsenic; and they do not differ from one another except in this: that sulfur and arsenic cannot be fixed unless their flammable parts <insert>are first removed</insert>

This is done by continually returning that which ascended to the top back to that which remained at the bottom, for each substance, until they are fixed.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "ceration" (softening substances to a waxy consistency). The fragment "nis" likely completes the word "consistentionis" (consistency) from the previous page's final sentence.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...tency <note>original: "consistentionis"; this fragment completes the word from the previous page</note> did not have the power to alter the body, so that it might be softened enough to flow and have the power of **penetration** <note>original: "ingressionem," referring to the ability of an alchemical agent to enter and transform a metal</note>. However, some have thought that this **ceration** <note>the process of softening a substance to a waxy state</note> ought to be performed using liquid oils and waters. But that is an error: it is entirely removed from the natural principles of this **mastery** <note>original: "magisterii," the alchemical art</note>, and it is most strongly refuted by the manifest works of nature.

For we do not see that nature has placed a moisture in metallic bodies that is quickly exhausted for the necessity of their **melting** and **softening**. For if she had placed such a moisture in them—one that is quickly ended by the heat of fire—it would necessarily follow that very quickly (namely, once a single heating and melting were completed) those bodies would happen to be totally deprived of all moisture. Therefore, it would follow from this that after one heating, any body could be neither hammered nor melted.

For this reason, as much as we are able to imitate the works of nature, it is necessary for us to follow nature's method in **cerating**. She herself performs ceration at the very root of the softening of fusible things with a moisture which, above all other moistures, endures the heat of fire. Therefore, it is necessarily beneficial for us to cerate with a similar moisture. In no other things is this cerating moisture found more effectively or more feasibly than in these: namely, in **sulfur** and **arsenic** <note>original: "sulphure & arsenico"</note> as close substitutes. However, it is found most closely and best in **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento vivo," mercury</note>. For we do not see the moisture of these...

<summary>This page argues against using oils or water for ceration, explaining that nature uses a more durable, fire-resistant moisture found in sulfur, arsenic, and especially mercury to keep metals malleable.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on "ceration" (softening substances to a waxy state). It completes the thought from page 118 regarding the unique qualities of certain "spirits" or volatile substances compared to ordinary moistures.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>PART FOUR.</header>

...le <note>original: "feasibilius"; completing the word from the previous page</note>. For we do not see nature leave behind their earth after dissolution, because of the strong union they had in nature's work of mixing. In all other things that possess moisture, you will find by experiment that upon dissolution they are separated from their earthy substance, and after that separation, they are deprived of all moisture. However, in the case of the aforementioned spirits—and especially **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento vivo"; mercury</note>—this happens the least. Therefore, there is no other reason why we should be excused from accepting their use for **ceration** <note>the process of softening a substance to a waxy consistency</note>. 

Thus, the method of ceration is performed through them: by repeating their **sublimation** <note>a process where a substance is heated into vapor and then solidified again</note> over the material to be softened as many times as necessary, until they remain within it with their moisture and provide a good and suitable **fusion** <note>melting</note>. This, however, will not occur until they have been perfectly **cleansed** <note>original: "mundationem"</note> of every corrupting element. But it seems better to me that their oils should first be **fixed** <note>original: "figantur"; made stable and resistant to evaporation by fire</note> using **oil of tartar** <note>a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate, often used in alchemy as a powerful alkaline reagent</note>; with these, every proper and necessary ceration for this **art** <note>referring to alchemy</note> may be accomplished.

# GEBER THE ARAB
### ON
### Alchemy of the Tradition of the Highest Perfection:
## BOOK II.
## *On the consideration of perfection and those things which pertain to it.*

### CHAPTER I.

Therefore, having completed the discussion of the principles of this **mastery** <note>original: "magisterii"; referring to the overall alchemical process or Great Work</note>, it remains to fulfill the intent of our art with a discourse suitable to it. Now, the consideration of perfection itself is the consideration of the **perfecting medicine** <note>original: "perficientis medicinæ"; in alchemy, the "medicine" is the elixir or Philosopher’s Stone used to "heal" or transform base metals into gold</note>, namely that we should discuss from what substance that medicine may best or most closely be extracted for the complete perfection of every imperfect thing. And likewise, it is the consideration of the **practical arts** <note>original: "artificiorum"; the manual operations and laboratory techniques</note> through which we may recognize whether the perfection is complete. Once these things have been handed down, the entire knowledge of perfection will have been delivered, according to the requirements of our art.

## *On the knowledge of the principles of bodies.*
### CHAPTER II.

Indeed, it is not possible to know this **transmutation** <note>the change of one metal into another</note> of bodies and of **silver** <note>original: "argenti"; though Geber often uses "argentum vivum" for mercury, here the text begins a discussion on the nature of metallic bodies</note> itself...

It establishes the theoretical framework for determining how to achieve and verify the perfection of metals.</summary>

First, therefore, we shall make known the **principles of bodies** <note>original: "corporum principia"; the underlying substances (like sulfur and mercury) that make up metals</note> according to their roots and their essences; namely, what they are according to all the causes of their differences, and what of good or evil they contain within themselves. Then indeed, we shall relate the natures and essences of all these bodies themselves, with all their causes and properties; namely, which are the causes of their corruption and perfection, proving them through manifest experiences.

->*On the nature of sulfur and arsenic in particular.*<-

### CHAPTER III.

Therefore, in the first place, we affirm the nature of the **spirits** <note>original: "spirituum"; in this context, volatile substances that can evaporate or be "fixed" by fire</note>, which are the principles of the bodies themselves: which are namely **sulfur** <term>sulfur: an inflammable mineral regarded as the "fatness" or "combustible" principle in metals</term>, **arsenic** <term>arsenicum: often referring here to yellow arsenic or orpiment, used for its coloring properties</term>, and **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the metal mercury</term>. We say, then, that sulfur and arsenic are the **fatness of the earth** <note>original: "pinguedo terrae"; a medieval concept describing the oily, combustible quality of these minerals</note>, as has been related by us above. From this, you may draw proof by manifest experience through their easy inflammation and their easy liquefaction by fire. For nothing is easily inflamed unless it is oily, nor does it liquefy easily by heat unless it is of that nature. Thus sulfur and its companion <note>referring to arsenic, which was often treated as a "kind" of sulfur or its close relative in properties</note>,

alike, they contain the cause of corruption: an inflammable substance and earthy dross <note>original: "feculentiam"; the impure, sediment-like remains or dregs of a substance</note>. Yet they also possess a mediating cause of perfection, positioned between the one and the other. Therefore, the cause of their corruption is their **earthy nature** <note>original: "terreitas"</note>, because they possess neither the power of fusion <note>the ability to melt</note> nor of **penetration** <term>ingreſſio: a technical term for the way a "spirit" or tincture enters and permeates the body of a metal</term>. Likewise, the inflammable substance is a cause of corruption; for it neither remains stable nor causes other things to stay stable, and it produces blackness and a livid, bluish-gray color in every kind of substance it touches.

The cause of perfection in them, therefore, is their intermediate substance: because through its earthy nature it is not disturbed by that penetration which is perfected through good fusion, and because of its combustible subtlety, its "impression" <note>the transformative influence it has on a metal</note> is not easily removed by "flight" <note>volatilization or evaporation when heated</note>. However, this intermediate substance is not the cause of perfection for metallic bodies or for **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> unless it is **fixed** <term>figatur: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate</term>. For when it is not fixed, even though its impression is not easily removed, it is nevertheless not made to last in a stable way.

From these facts, it is concluded that it is necessary for the craftsman to divide the intermediate substance. Some, however, have thought it impossible to divide it, because of its strong mixture and natural union. These people are indeed contradicted by their own manifest works. For they have **calcined** <term>calcinaverunt: reduced a substance to a powder by high heat, often to remove impurities</term> sulfur itself (though not extensively), which resulted in a substance that offered no fusion nor any inflammation. But that must have happened through its division, since sulfur...

...sulfur <note>completing the word from the previous page</note> remaining in its natural mixture and simple substance, it must necessarily be ignited and burned. Therefore, through the division of the different substances within it, it remains for the more inflammable part in it to be separated by artifice apart from the non-inflammable parts. For if it is possible, through **calcination** <term>calcinationem: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to change its properties or remove impurities</term> to reach the removal of all inflammable parts within it, it is necessary to admit—based on their natural operations—that a complete division of parts can be achieved. But because this depends on a most subtle artifice <note>the secret or complex techniques of the alchemist</note>, they thought it would be impossible. It is therefore clearly evident from the premises that this is not the true sulfur of our Art; but perhaps only a part of it. And we have now led you to the knowledge of the artifice by which it is possible to arrive at its division. In **arsenic** <note>original: "arsenico"; in medieval alchemy, this often refers to arsenic sulfides like orpiment</note>, however, because many of its inflammable parts have been resolved by the action of nature in the root of its mixture, the artifice of its separation is easier. But arsenic is the **tincture of whiteness** <note>original: "albedinis tinctura"; a substance used to color metals white, like silver</note>, while sulfur is the **tincture of redness** <note>original: "rubedinis"; the agent used to produce the color of gold</note>. Therefore, it is necessary to use great caution in the division of sulfur.

...corruption <note>original: "ruptionis"; completing the word from the previous page, likely "corruptionis"</note>, the causes of which it is purged from during **sublimation** <term>sublimando: the process of heating a substance so it rises as vapor and collects as a purified solid elsewhere in the vessel</term>; namely, one earthy, impure substance that does not ignite, and another superfluous, fleeting watery substance. However, some have thought that it does not possess such superfluous earth or impurity. But what they thought is in vain. For we see that it has much lividity <note>a grayish, leaden, or dull discoloration</note> and is not of a pure whiteness; and likewise, through a simple artifice, we see black and drossy earth emanate from it—namely, through washing, the method of which we will now describe.

But because it is possible to draw a double perfection from it—that is, to create the **medicine** <term>medicina: in alchemy, the "elixir" or "philosopher’s stone" used to heal "sick" metals and transform them into gold</term> from it and to perfect it through **coagulation** <term>coagulatione: the process of turning a liquid or vapor into a solid, stable state</term>—it is therefore necessary to prepare it through two stages of cleansing. For this reason, two cleansings of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurii"; mercury was considered one of the two primary principles of all metals</note> are necessary. One is through sublimation for the medicine, and this is now indicated. The other is through washing for coagulation, and that will be indicated later.

For if we wish to create the medicine from it, then it is necessary to purge it thoroughly from the dross of its earthiness <note>original: "terreitatis"</note> through sublimation, so that in **projection** <term>projectione: the final stage of the Great Work, where the alchemical medicine is cast upon base metals to transform them</term> it does not produce a livid color. Likewise, one must destroy its fleeting wateriness <note>original: "aqueitatem fugitivam"; the volatile parts of the substance that evaporate too quickly under heat</note>, lest it make the whole medicine fleeting during projection. One must instead preserve its intermediate substance for the medicine; it is the property of this substance not to be burned and to protect against burning, and it likewise does not flee, but <meta>catchword: remains fixed</meta>

...the **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: mercury</term> returns to its own form. From what has been said, it is similarly clear that quicksilver is not perfecting in its own nature as produced by its **mine** <term>minera: the natural ore or source</term>, but rather it is that which is produced from it by our **art** <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, the human intervention that improves upon natural processes.</note>; and the same is to be brought about in **sulfur** <term>sulphure: one of the two primary principles of alchemy alongside mercury</term> and its companion. Therefore, in these things it is not possible to follow nature naturally, but they are perfected through our art and ingenuity.

**Tutia** <term>Tutia: tutty or zinc oxide, often collected from the flues of brass furnaces</term>, which makes a great impression <note>original: "impressionem magnam"; meaning a significant chemical influence or change</note> upon bodies. And therefore, let us explain what these substances are in their nature, according to their proofs and causes. 

We say, therefore, that **Marcasite** <term>Marchesita: a term historically used for iron pyrites or other metallic-looking minerals</term> possesses a double substance in its nature. One part is **mortified quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum mortificatum"; mercury that has been "killed" or rendered solid and inactive through chemical treatment</note> that has been moderately prepared and is approaching the nature of a **fixed** <term>fixi: a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when heated</term> material. The other part is a **combustible, non-fixed sulfur**. 

We have discovered through clear experience that it possesses this sulfurous quality. For when it is **sublimated** <term>sublimatur: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</term>, a manifest, combustible sulfurous substance is raised from it. Even without sublimation, its sulfurous nature is similarly weighed and evaluated. For if it is placed in a fire to be ignited, it does not receive the heat until the inflammation of the sulfur is ignited and burns. 

It is also clearly shown to possess the substance of quicksilver in a way that can be sensed. For it gives **whiteness** to **Venus** <note>original: "Veneri"; the alchemical name for copper</note> in the likeness of silver, just as quicksilver itself does. Likewise, we see it provide a **sky-blue color** <note>original: "colorem cœlestinum"</note> during its sublimation, and we see it possess a clear metallic luster. These things make the craftsman certain that this substance contains these two roots in its foundation. 

**Magnesia** <term>Magnesia: in this context, likely a manganese ore or a similar mineral used to "cleanse" glass or affect metal color</term>, however, possesses a more **turbid sulfur** and a more **earthy and dreggy quicksilver**; it also has a sulfur that is similarly more fixed and less flammable. ...to more closely approach the nature of **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term>. **Tutia** <term>Tutia: tutty or zinc oxide</term> is indeed the smoke of white bodies; and this is evaluated by clear proof. For when the smoke from a mixture of **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for tin</term> and **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term> is cast off, adhering to the flues of the smiths’ furnaces, it makes the same impression <note>original: "impressionem"; referring to the chemical effect or residue left behind</note> as the Tutia does; and because metallic smoke does not return <note>original: "non redit"; meaning it does not easily revert to a metallic state</note>, neither does Tutia itself return without the admixture of some other body. And therefore, because of its fineness <note>original: "subtilitatem"; referring to the small particle size of the vaporous soot</note>, it penetrates more deeply into the body of the metal: and thus it alters it more than its own body would, and it remains stable during the trial <note>original: "examine"; the testing of a metal's purity, often by fire</note> with little effort, which has already been told to you. Therefore, whatever things are altered by the power of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: mercury</term>, or of **sulfur**, or things similar to these, it is necessary for them to be altered: since these alone communicate and unite in nature with the bodies themselves, because they are more compatible with them.

...while it will seem expedient, along with its proofs, which are obtained through experiment. Therefore, **Gold** <term>Sol: "The Sun," the alchemical name for gold</term> is created from the most subtle substance of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," or mercury</term>, and the clearest and cleanest **fixation** <note>original: "fixura"; referring to the metal's ability to remain solid and stable under intense heat</note>, and from a small amount of the substance of clean **sulfur** <note>Alchemists believed all metals were made of two principles: Sulfur (the "father" or combustible principle) and Mercury (the "mother" or metallic principle)</note> of a pure redness—most fixed, clear, and changed from its own nature, tinting that [quicksilver]. But because a diversity occurs in the redness of the sulfur itself, it is necessary that the **citrinity** <term>citrinitas: the yellowing of a metal, the stage of the "Great Work" before it turns red</term> of the gold similarly possesses diversity. For one is indeed more intense in its citrinity, and another is less so. 

That [gold] is indeed made from the most subtle substance of quicksilver is clearly considered: because quicksilver receives it easily and amicably. For quicksilver does not receive anything other than what is of its own nature. That it truly possessed that clear and clean substance is manifested through its resplendent and radiating glow; manifesting itself not only in the day, but even in the night, it requires no other proof. 

That it truly possesses a fixed nature without a burning sulfurousness is clear through every operation of it in the fire. For it is neither diminished, nor is it set aflame. That it truly becomes a tinting sulfur is clear through this: that when mixed with quicksilver, it transforms it through **sublimation** <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then collects as a solid again</term> into a red color (which is **Usifur** <term>Usifur: an archaic term for cinnabar or vermilion</term> or **Cinnabar**) and that when it is **amalgamated** <term>amalgamatur: mixed with mercury to form a soft alloy</term> with bodies, and is sublimated with strong ignition

...of the bodies, such that their subtle essence rises with it, it creates an intense lemon-yellow color <note>original: "citriniffimum colorem." Citrinitas or "yellowing" was the alchemical stage following the blackening (nigredo) and whitening (albedo) of the work</note>. It is clear, therefore, that a pure substance of **sulfur** generates a pure color, while an impure substance generates an impure color. He who requires a demonstration to prove its yellowness lacks common sense, for this is something achieved through sight. 

Therefore, the most subtle substance of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," or mercury</term> brought to **fixation** <note>original: "fixio," the process by which a volatile substance is made stable and resistant to fire</note>, along with its purity and the most subtle fixed matter of sulfur—which is of a clear redness and not burning—constitutes the entire essential matter of **gold** <note>original: "auri"</note>. A greater quantity of quicksilver, however, is proven to exist within it. Therefore, whatever you wish to transform, transform it according to this model, so that you may bring those things to a state of balance. We have already provided you with the method for this. 

Because it possessed subtle and fixed parts during its creation, those parts were able to become very dense. This is the reason for its great weight. Through a long and temperate **decoction** <term>decoctio: a process of refining or "ripening" a substance through sustained, gentle heat</term> by nature, a gradual resolution and a successful thickening was achieved in the final mixture, so that it may melt when heat is applied. From the preceding points, it is clear that a large quantity of quicksilver is the cause of perfection, while a large quantity of sulfur is the cause of corruption. Likewise, uniformity in the substance, which occurs through an equal mixture, is the cause of perfection; but diversity is the cause of corruption. Similarly, the hardening and thickening, which occurs through long and temperate decoctio- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword indicates the word "decoction" continues on the next page.</note>

<header>PART ONE.</header>

...is completed by **decoction** <term>decoctio: the process of ripening or "cooking" a substance through steady heat</term>, and is the cause of perfection; its opposite, however, is the cause of corruption. Therefore, if **sulfur** that is not of the proper disposition should fall upon that **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the metal mercury</term>, it is necessary that various corruptions be introduced according to the diversity of that sulfur. 

For such sulfur which falls upon it can be entirely **fixed** <note>original: "fixum," meaning it remains stable in fire</note> and non-combustible <note>original: "non adustibile," a substance that does not burn away or char</note>, or entirely combustible and volatile <note>original: "fugiens," literally "fleeing," referring to substances that evaporate or sublimate when heated</note> in the nature of sulfur, or volatile but not of the nature of sulfur, or it may hold part volatile and part fixed; it may also hold the nature of sulfur in one part and not in another: it may be entirely pure or impure; or half-impure, or even a smaller part thereof. 

Likewise, it may be of great quantity, overpowering the quicksilver in the mixture, or of a small quantity, being overpowered by it; or it may be neither overpowering nor overpowered. Likewise, it may be white, or red, or of a middle quality. Thus, from all these variations in both sulfur and quicksilver, it was necessary for diverse **metallic bodies** to be created in nature; it is now useful for us to recount all their principles along with their manifest properties and experimental observations.

...of [quicksilver] clean, pure, **fixed** <note>original: "fixam," referring to a substance that does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat</note>, and clear—not overpowering, but rather of a small quantity and greatly overpowered in the mixture of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the metal mercury, considered the "womb" or passive principle of all metals</term>; from this, **pure gold** is created. 

If, however, the **sulfur** should be clean, fixed, and white with a pure whiteness, clear above a substance of quicksilver that is clean, fixed, and clear—not overpowering it, but slightly overpowered—**silver** is created from them. Yet it possesses a purity diminished from the purity of gold, and a density thicker <note>original: "spissitudinem grossiorem," suggesting a less refined or "coarser" internal structure</note> than that of gold. 

The evidence for this is that its parts are not condensed to such a degree that it might equal gold in weight; nor does it possess a substance as fixed as gold. The sign of this is its diminution <note>Loss of mass or volume</note> by **fire**; and its sulfur—which is neither fixed nor incombustible—is the cause of this. That the sulfur within it is of such a disposition is proven by its slight inflammation. 

Furthermore, one must not think it impossible for the same sulfur to be both fixed and not fixed, when compared to different things. For the **sulfureity** <note>original: "sulphureitas," the essential sulfurous quality or "soul" of the metal</note> of the **Moon** <note>original: "Luna," the alchemical name for silver</note>, when compared to the sulfureity of gold, is non-fixed and combustible. Yet, when compared to the sulfur of other metallic bodies, it is fixed and non-combustible.

<header>Part One.</header>

### On the nature of Mars <note>original: "Martis," the alchemical name for iron, associated with the Roman god of war</note>.

### Chapter VIII.

If, however, there should be a **fixed**, earthy **sulfur** mixed in nature with a fixed, earthy **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the metal mercury, considered the passive principle of all metals</term>, and both of these are not of a pure whiteness but rather a leaden or dull whiteness; and if the quantity of sulfur in this mixture is in great excess, **iron** is generated from them. And because the excess of **fixed sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur fixum," sulfur that has been rendered resistant to evaporation by heat</note> in the mixture prevents **fusion** <note>melting</note>, it follows from this that sulfur loses its readiness to liquefy through the process of **fixation** more quickly than quicksilver does. 

We see, however, that sulfur which is *not* fixed melts faster than quicksilver. From these things, the cause of the speed or slowness of melting in each of the metallic bodies is made clear. For that which has more fixed sulfur melts more slowly; whereas that which has more **combustible** <note>original: "adurente," burning or inflammatory</note> sulfur undergoes melting more easily and quickly. This has been demonstrated clearly enough by us. 

That fixed sulfur itself makes melting slower in bodies is shown by this: it can never be fixed unless it is **calcined** <term>calcinatio: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</term>; and when it has been calcined, it yields no melting at all. Therefore, when included in any mixture, it must hinder that melting. That it cannot be fixed unless it is calcined is evident from the experiment of anyone who tries to fix it without calcining it; for they find that it always "flees" <note>evaporates or sublimates</note>, until [it turns] into earth

For the observer has seen and discovered this just as he received it from our writings. [fixa]tion <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the "fixation" (making a substance heat-resistant) of mercury.</note>, and without being converted into earth. The second cause is the increase of an external flame upon those metals, which penetrates them and dissolves them into smoke along with itself, regardless of how **fixed** <note>fixed: the state of being "permanent" and resistant to evaporation or change when heated</note> they were. 

The third cause is the rarefaction of the bodies themselves through **calcination** <term>calcinatio: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a dry powder or "calx"</term>. For then the flame of fire can penetrate them and drive them out as smoke, no matter how perfect they may have been. If therefore all the causes of destruction...

For it is that which overcomes fire and is not overcome by fire; rather, it rests amiably within it, rejoicing in it.

Returning therefore to our purpose, we say that if the sulfur were impure, **coarse** <note>original: "groffum," a variant of "grossum," meaning thick, coarse, or unrefined</note>...

...coarse, fixed according to its greater part, but according to its lesser part not fixed, red, and livid; throughout the whole it is neither overcoming nor overcome. When this falls upon **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the liquid metal mercury</term> that is coarse and impure, it is necessary that **copper** <term>æs: the Latin term for copper or bronze; in alchemy, it is the metal associated with the planet Venus</term> be created from them. Therefore, it happens that the proof of all these things is brought forth through the data from their very nature. 

For when it is placed for **ignition** <term>ignitio: the process of heating a substance until it glows or catches fire</term>, you will be able to discern from it a manifest sulfurous flame, which is certainly a sign of non-fixed <note>original: "non fixi," referring to the volatile part of the substance that evaporates</note> sulfur, as is the loss of its quantity through **exhalation** <note>original: "exhalationem," the release of vapors or gasses during heating</note>. 

The fixed sulfur within it, however, is observed through its frequent ignition and **combustion** <note>original: "combuftionem," a burning or calcining process</note>. For from this comes the retardation of its **fusion** <note>original: "fufionis," the process of melting</note> and the hardening of its substance: which are signs of the abundance of its fixed sulfur. 

That this sulfur, however, is red, livid, and impure, mixed with impure and drossy quicksilver, is shown through the senses. Therefore, it requires no other proof. But through experiment you may draw out the secret. 

For you see that every thing changed into earth or ash by the action of heat is easily dissolved and reduced into the nature of water. This happens because of the **refining** <term>subtiliatio: the process of making a substance finer, thinner, or more "subtle" through the action of fire</term> of the parts by the fire. Therefore, a thing that is more subtle in its own nature, when reduced to this earthy nature, is refined even more. Consequently, it is more easily dissolved: and thus that which is most subtle is most dissolved. From these things therefore...
