...the cause of the corruption and infection of these two bodies—namely **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term> and **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term>—becomes clear. It arises from sulfur: a large quantity of it being **fixed** <note>original: "fixum," referring to a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when heated</note> and a smaller amount being non-fixed. There is, however, less of this [non-fixed sulfur] in Venus and more in Mars. 

Therefore, when sulfur reaches a state of **fixation** <note>original: "fixionem"</note> through the heat of fire, its parts are refined; but that part which is prone to the dissolution of its substance is dissolved. The sign of this is the exposure of these two bodies to the vapor of vinegar or another **sharp** <note>original: "acuti," referring to acidic or corrosive substances</note> substance. For through this, an **alum-like quality** <term>aluminositas: a quality resembling alum, an astringent mineral salt; here referring to the corrosive "bloom" or oxidation on a metal's surface</term> blooms on their surface, created and refined by the heat within them. 

And if you place these two bodies in a **pungent liquid** <term>liquorem ponticum: a sharp, astringent, or acidic solution</term>, many of their parts will easily dissolve by boiling in it. And if you look into the mines of these two, you will find a dissolved substance of an alum-like quality clearly dripping from them and clinging to them; this quality is proven by its pungency and easy solubility in water. For nothing else is found to be pungent and easily soluble except alum and salts, and whatever is of that nature. 

**Blackness** <note>original: "nigredo," referring here to the dark tarnish or surface impurities</note>, however, in each and especially in these two bodies, is caused by the non-fixed and **flammable** <note>original: "inflammabile"</note> sulfur that is enclosed within them. There is indeed much of it in Venus, but very little in Mars, where it approaches the nature of fixed sulfur. Therefore, it is not easy for such an impression to be removed from Mars...

...it is possible for them to be moved. Now, therefore, it has become clear that from **non-fixed sulfur** <note>original: "sulphure non fixo"; sulfur that evaporates or burns away when heated</note>, **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting or becoming liquid under heat</term> occurs and is even aided. From **fixed sulfur**, however, fusion does not occur, and in all things, fusion is hindered. 

Yet, from **fixed quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: the traditional name for mercury (argentum vivum)</term>, it is not necessary that fusion be prevented, nor is it hindered. Anyone who knows that no clever method of melting has ever been able to preserve sulfur in a molten state after it has been **fixed** <note>original: "fixionem"; the process of making a substance resistant to fire</note> knows this to be necessarily true. 

Indeed, quicksilver can be fixed through the frequent repetition of **sublimation** <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then collected again as a solid, used here to purify and "harden" the mercury</term> until it reaches a point where it can undergo a good fusion. 

From this, it is manifest that those **bodies** <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to the metals themselves</note> which contain more quicksilver are of greater **perfection** <note>Perfection refers to how close a metal is to becoming gold or silver</note>; while those that contain less are of lesser perfection. Therefore, strive, my dearest son, so that in all your works, the quicksilver exceeds the sulfur in the **mixture** <note>original: "commixtione"</note>. 

And if you learn how to achieve this perfection through quicksilver alone, you will be a seeker of the most precious perfection—one that surpasses the very work of **nature**. For you will be able to cleanse the metals inwardly; a depth of purification that nature itself cannot reach. 

The proof of this—that things containing a larger quantity of quicksilver are of greater perfection—is their easy acceptance of the quicksilver itself. For we see that "perfect" bodies <note>Refers to gold and silver</note> drink in quicksilver in a friendly manner. From the preceding discourses, it follows of necessity that there is a twofold **sulfurous nature** <note>original: "sulphureitatem"</note> within these bodies. One, indeed...

<header>BOOK TWO</header>

...indeed enclosed within the depths of the **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: the traditional name for mercury (Latin: argentum vivum)</term> at the beginning of its creation. The other [type of impurity], however, comes from outside by accident. Of these, the accidental one is removed with great effort. The innate one, however, cannot be removed by any ingenuity of the arts performed by fire. It is toward this that our operation may appropriately and usefully arrive, since it is already united with [the metal] at the root of its creation. 

This is proven by experiment, for we see **combustible sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitatem adustibilem"; sulfur that burns away or evaporates when heated</note> destroyed by fire, but **fixed sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitatem fixam"; sulfur that remains stable and does not burn off under high heat</note> not at all. If, therefore, we say that **bodies** <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to the metals themselves</note> can be cleansed and **fixed** <term>fixed: making a substance resistant to change or evaporation by fire</term> through **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to remove volatile parts</term> and **reduction** <term>reduction: the process of restoring a metal to its metallic state from an oxide or "calx"</term>, you should understand this to be from the earthy dross which is not united with them at the root of their nature or in their depths. 

Since it is not possible to cleanse those things that are united by the ingenuity of fire, unless the "medicine" of quicksilver comes to them, hiding and tempering that [impurity], or separating it from the mixture. The separation of the earthy substance from the mixture that is united to the metal at its natural root occurs either through **elevation** <term>elevation: also known as sublimation; the process where a substance is heated into a vapor and collected again</term> with things that elevate the substance of quicksilver and leave behind the earthy sulfur because of their affinity with them—such as **tutia** <term>tutia: zinc oxide, often collected from the flues of furnaces</term> and **marcasite** <term>marcasite: a common alchemical term for various metallic sulfides like iron pyrite</term>. For these are vapors in which the greater part is the quantity of quicksilver rather than sulfur. You will be able to see the evidence of this; for if you join such spirits by strong and sudden **fusion** <term>fusion: the process of melting substances together</term>...

### On the nature of Jupiter or Tin.
<note>In alchemy, the planet Jupiter is the symbolic name for the metal Tin.</note>

### CHAPTER 10.

Having proposed this investigation and pursuing our intent, we say: that if **sulfur** <note>Alchemists believed metals were composed of two primary principles: Sulfur (the "soul" or combustible part) and Mercury (the "spirit" or metallic part)</note> were at the root of the mixture, participating in little **fixation** <term>fixation: the process by which a substance is made to withstand the heat of fire without evaporating</term>, white with a non-pure whiteness, not overcoming but overcome, and it fell upon impure quicksilver—fixed according to one part, but according to the greater part not fixed, white with an impure whiteness—**tin** will result from them. And you will find the proof of these things through preparation. Since if you **calcine** <term>calcination: heating a metal until it turns into a powder or "calx"</term> the tin itself, you will perceive from it a sulfurous stench: which is a sign of non-fixed sulfur in it; and because it does not emit a sulfurous flame, do not think it is therefore fixed. Because it is not on account of fixation, but on account of the great quantity of quicksilver overcoming it, which in the mixture saves it from burning, that it does not give a flame. Thus a double **sulfurousness** <note>original: "sulphureitas"; the quality or presence of sulfur within a substance</note> is proven in tin...

That the substance of quicksilver is indeed the cause of the shrieking is proven by the washing of **lead** with quicksilver. For if lead is washed with quicksilver, and after its washing you melt it with a fire not exceeding the heat of its **fusion** <note>melting point</note>, a part of the quicksilver will remain with it; this brings a shriek to the lead and converts it into **tin** <note>original: "stannum"</note>. 

Conversely, you can observe this through the transformation of tin into lead. For through the multiple repetition of calcination upon it, and the administration of a fire more suitable for its **reduction** <note>The process of returning a metallic powder (calx) back into a solid metal</note>, it is converted into lead. It remains for us, therefore, to provide a description of **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for lead</term>; and we say that its essence and nature do not differ from **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for tin</term>, except that it has a more impure substance mixed from two coarser substances—namely, **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: also known as mercury; in alchemy, it represents the principle of fluidity and metallic luster</term>. ### CHAPTER XII.

We have proved, therefore, from my previous discourses, that **spirits** <term>spirits: volatile substances like Mercury, Sulfur, or Arsenic that can "fly" from the fire</term> are more closely assimilated and united to bodies, because we see that they are more "friendly" to bodies than any other things in nature. ...but the soft [bodies] truly such as **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for Tin</term> and **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for Lead</term>: It is necessary, therefore, that the **medicine** <term>medicine: an elixir or tincture used to "heal" or transform base metals into gold or silver</term> which completes them should be similarly manifold. For **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: argentum vivum, or Mercury</term> requires one kind of medicine, which coagulates <note>original: "coagulante"; the process of turning liquid Mercury into a solid metal</note> and perfects it; but other bodies to be transformed require another. 

Likewise, one kind is needed by the hard, fire-resistant metals <note>original: "dura ignibilia"; metals that are hard and difficult to melt or "ignite" in the furnace</note> such as **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for Iron</term>; while another is needed by the soft metals that are not so resistant, such as Saturn and Jupiter. Since both of these kinds of each genus differ from one another in their nature, it necessarily follows that they require similarly diverse medicines. 

For Mars and Venus are of one kind of hardness, yet they differ in a certain specific property. For the former [Iron] is not **fusible** <note>original: "fufibilis"; capable of being melted</note>, while the latter [Copper] is indeed fusible. Likewise, the former is entirely dark and impure, while the latter is not. Furthermore, the former possesses a dull **whiteness** <note>original: "albedine"; one of the primary colors of alchemical transformation</note>, while the latter partakes in an impure **redness** <note>original: "rubedine"</note> and even a **greenness** <note>original: "viriditate"</note>; all of these traits create a need for diversity in the medicine. 

Therefore, Mars is perfected by one medicine, and Venus by another. And the former is totally impure, while the latter is not; and the former has a certain dull whiteness, while the latter shares in redness and greenness, which similarly impose the necessity of diversity in the medicine. In the same way, the bodies of the other class are of a soft nature, namely Jupiter and Saturn; since these differ similarly, it is necessary that they require diverse medicines. For the former, namely Jupiter, is clean, while the latter [Saturn] is not.

<header>PART ONE.</header>

It is useful to bring forward in a single discourse the explanation and the sequence of the compositions of all these medicines, along with their causes and the clear evidence of their experimental proofs. First, therefore, we shall describe the series of the ten medicines; next, that of all the metallic bodies; then, that of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: Mercury, or "Argentum Vivum"</term>; and finally, we must pass to the medicine of the **perfecting mastery** <term>perfecting mastery: The highest level of alchemical work, often referring to the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone</term>. Since, however, the imperfect bodies <note>The base metals, such as Lead, Tin, Copper, and Iron</note> may in some cases require a preliminary preparation, we shall first provide an account of the preparations of these imperfect bodies. We do this so that we are not "bitten by the envious" <note>A common phrase in medieval alchemy meaning to be criticized by rivals or skeptics</note> due to any perceived insufficiency or detraction of the art itself. We will set down the reasons for all these discoveries: specifically, how these preparations are made suitable through our craftsmanship so that they may receive the medicine of the perfection of **whiteness** <term>whiteness: "Albedo," the stage of transmutation into Silver</term> and **redness** <term>redness: "Rubedo," the final stage of transmutation into Gold</term> at every stage, and be perfected by it. Following this, a sufficient and appropriate account of all the medicines shall be added.

...unclean things <note>The OCR "iununda" is likely a continuation of "immunda" from the previous page, meaning foul or impure substances</note>: all of which are things that corrupt the substance of **Saturn** <term>Saturn: Lead</term> and **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: Tin</term>. That the first kind is impossible to be removed happens because, in the principles of the specific nature of this genus of bodies, they were mixed into the true essence, and have become the true essence itself. And therefore, since it is not possible to remove the true essence of any thing in nature while the thing itself remains, it was not possible to delete these corrupting elements from them. 

Wherefore, some of the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" usually refers to the ancient authorities on alchemy and natural science, such as Aristotle, Hermes Trismegistus, or Geber</note> thought that because of this, the Art could not be achieved. But we, even in our own time, searching for this science, have arrived at this same point: that similarly, by no manner of skillful preparation, were we able to illuminate the base bodies <term>base bodies: the "ignoble" metals like lead and tin, as opposed to the "noble" metals gold and silver</term> with the completion of their clear brightness, without it happening that they were entirely corrupted, stained, and rather utterly blackened.

Returning, therefore, to our heart, and torturing ourselves with the afflictions of immense meditation and thought, we looked back upon the bodies diminished from perfection, into the depth— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "profunditatem" (depth), continuing the idea that the secrets of alchemy lie in the internal essence of the metals</note>

Likewise, these bodies of both kinds are treated by alternating turns of **calcination** <note>The process of heating a metal in air until it decomposes into a powdered ash or oxide.</note> and **reduction** <note>The chemical process of returning a calcined ash or oxide back into its metallic state.</note> using a sufficient amount of fire, until they appear cleaner. We have written about this proportion in our other volume, titled *On the Investigation of Art* <note>original: *de inquiſitione artis*; likely referring to the *De Investigatione Perfectionis*, a foundational alchemical text attributed to the author.</note>, which immediately precedes this book in sequence. In that volume, we wrote whatever we investigated according to the reasoning of our mind. In this book, however, we have fully defined what we have seen and touched with our own hands according to the order of science <note>This is a significant statement in the history of science, emphasizing empirical, hands-on laboratory experience over purely theoretical "reasoning."</note>. 

These bodies are also similarly purified through the **solution** <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid to separate it from impurities.</note> of their substance—the method of which we have already described—and likewise through the reduction of whatever remains dissolved from them. For by this same method of preparation, the substance is found to be cleaner and more perfect than by any other kind of preparation. No other method can be compared to this one, except for that which is perfected through **sublimation** <note>Heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid; used here as a high-level purification technique.</note>, which is therefore equal in power to this.

Our goal is to find a way to mix and unite within their depths the "fixed" <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating or changing.</note> substance of **quicksilver** <term>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</term>, or of fixed **sulfur**, or of its companion "fixed body," or of another hard and non-fusible substance; such as **lime** <note>original: *calx*; a powder produced by burning stone or metal.</note>, **marcasite** <note>A term used for various metallic minerals, most often iron pyrites.</note>, and **tutty** <note>original: *tutia*; zinc oxide that accumulates as a crust in the chimneys of smelting furnaces.</note>.

This is indeed perfected by maintaining them in a fire proportional to them after that first stage of **calcination**.

And because a specific sequence in the methods of preparation is necessary, let us set down a complete order for them. First, therefore, let every fugitive and combustible corrupting substance be cleansed from them. Next, the earthy superfluity <note>The "dross" or waste material that is not metallic.</note> should be removed. After this, let them be dissolved and reduced, or washed thoroughly through a bath or mixing of quicksilver.

...through this <note>Referring to the previous page's method of removing volatile parts.</note> its substance is hardened more, which does not happen to **Saturn** <term>original: *Saturno*; lead</term>. And now this is done through **alums** similarly, as we have narrated above. For these specifically harden **Jupiter** <term>original: *Jovem*; tin</term>. Another way is by keeping it in the fire of its **calcination** <term>original: *calcinationis*; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder</term>. For through this, it loses its **stridor** <note>original: *stridorem*; the "tin-cry," a distinctive crackling sound made when bending a piece of pure tin. Its removal was seen as a step toward transforming it into a more "noble" metal.</note> and similarly the brittleness of its bodies; which does not happen to **Saturn** in the same way, because it possesses no stridor, nor does it break its bodies. And through the repetition of calcination and **reduction** <note>The process of restoring a metallic powder back into a solid metal state.</note>, it similarly loses its stridor through the sharpness of salt and alums.

The special preparation of **Saturn**, however, is secondary; namely, through calcination and the sharpness of salt. For through this, it is hardened and whitened. It is likewise hardened by **marcasite** <note>Metallic minerals, often iron pyrites.</note> and **tutty** <note>Zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>. We have determined all the methods of preparation more completely in the book titled *On the Discovery of Perfection*, chapter 13, since in this work we have summarized their main points.

->### On the preparation of Venus.<-

->### CHAPTER XVI.<-

Following the order of the preceding sections, we have described the preparations of two bodies <note>Tin and lead.</note>. Next, therefore, we speak of **Venus** <term>original: *Veneris*; copper</term>, and then of **Mars** <term>original: *Martis*; iron</term>. There is a manifold method for the preparation of **Venus**: one is accomplished through **elevation** <note>Sublimation, or the process of causing a substance to rise as vapor through heat.</note>, while the other is performed without elevation.

...to join non-fixed **arsenic** to the same <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the treatment of Venus (copper).</note>, so that it may melt with it in fusion. And this is done, namely, by dissolving precipitated **quicksilver** <term>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</term>; and let the **calcined body** <note>The metal that has been roasted into a powder.</note> which is intended to be hardened be dissolved as well. Let both of these solutions be mixed, and from these, in alternating turns, let the calcined body be mixed by grinding and soaking, by calcining and reducing, until it becomes hard and yet **fusible** upon ignition. This same result is completely achieved with **calx**, **tutty**, and calcined **marcasite** <term>original: *marchesita*; iron pyrites or similar sulfurous minerals</term>, when they are dissolved and soaked. And indeed, the cleaner these substances are, the more perfectly they **transform**.

->On the medicines of the three orders.<-

### CHAPTER XIX.

->*The first presupposition.*<-

Let us first present a universal discourse regarding medicines, along with their causes and clear practical experiences. We suggest, therefore, that unless every superfluous element is removed from the imperfect bodies—either by means of a medicine or through the method of preparation—perfection will not be achieved. Specifically, all redundant sulphureity <term>original: *sulphureitas*; the combustible or "oily" principle in alchemy thought to cause impurities and prevents the metal from being stable</term> and all unclean earthiness must be stripped away. This is done so that these impurities do not separate from the mixture during fusion <note>The process of melting the metallic body.</note> after the projection <term>original: *projectio*; the final step of casting the transformative "medicine" or Philosopher's Stone onto a base metal</term> of the medicine which alters them. Once you have achieved this, you possess one of the essential markers of perfection.

->*The second presupposition.*<-

Similarly, unless the medicine also brightens and alters the substance into a white or lemon-yellow <note>original: *citrinum*; the color associated with the "citrinitas" stage, signaling the approaching transition to the final gold-producing stage</note> color according to your desired intention,

### *The third presupposition.*

Furthermore, unless [the medicine] specifically brings about a lunar or solar fusion <note>original: *fusionem lunarem aut solarem*; referring to the qualities of silver (the Moon) or gold (the Sun)</note>, it is not alterable toward its completion. This is because it will not remain stable during the trials <note>original: *judiciis*; the tests or assays, such as high heat, used to prove a metal's purity</note>, but will be entirely separated from the mixture and recede. This matter, which we have determined more extensively, is demonstrated in what follows regarding the case of cupellation <term>original: *cineritio*; a refining process where metals are heated in a "cupel" made of bone ash to separate precious metals from base impurities</term>. Moreover, unless the medicine is made permanent through a firm alteration of the impression <note>the 'imprint' or new form given to the metal</note>, the change it effects is of no value, because the impression does not last, but vanishes.

### *The fourth presupposition.*

Furthermore, unless [the medicine] brings the weights of perfection, it does not transform the substance into a firm and true completion of nature—one in which there is no deception through the error of belief <note>i.e., the resulting metal must be physically genuine, not just a superficial imitation that tricks the observer</note>. For the weight of nature <note>specific gravity</note> is one of the signs of perfection. It is clear, therefore, that since there are five defining characteristics <note>original: *assifferentiæ*; the specific traits that distinguish one substance from another</note> of perfection, it is necessary for the medicine of our mastery <term>original: *magisterium*; the alchemical craft or the "Great Work" itself</term> to bring these specific differences to bear during the projection <term>original: *projectio*; the final step of casting the transformative "medicine" onto a base metal to change its nature</term>. Through this, it becomes evident from which substances our material should be drawn. For it is drawn from those things that are most closely joined to the [metallic] bodies and are friendly to the same...

...[indeed] <note>original: *[qui]dem*; completing the word from the previous page</note> that remains deep within, performing the transformations. Therefore, when searching among all other things, we have found in our investigations no other matter more friendly to the nature of metallic bodies than **quicksilver** <term>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury, which alchemists believed was the "mother" or foundational spirit of all metals</term>. By devoting our labor to it, we have discovered that it is the true **medicine** for things capable of change, bringing them to completion through a transformation that is both genuine and quite extraordinary.

### The fifth presupposition.

It remains for us to specifically describe the substance of this mercury and the distinct properties of that substance. Since we found that it does not change without managing its natural transformation, we also found that it must necessarily be prepared; for it cannot mix into the core of a metal without a specific method of preparation. Specifically, its substance must be made such that it blends thoroughly into the very depths of the body being transformed, never to be separated again. 

This cannot be achieved unless the mercury is highly refined through a precise and determined preparation described in the chapter on **sublimation** <term>original: *sublimatio*; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used to purify and "spiritualize" it</term>. Its impression <note>the new form or quality imprinted upon the base metal</note> does not last unless it is **fixed** <term>original: *fixio*; the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to fire</term>. Nor does it provide luster unless its most brilliant substance is drawn out through skillful technique and through the operation of an appropriate **fire**. Finally, it does not allow for a perfect melting unless caution is taken during its fixation, with this hard <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page</note>

For such a fire consumes moisture and disrupts fusion. Indeed, it is necessary for a craftsman of sound mind to consider all these rules in every **medicine** <term>original: *medicina*; here referring to the "Philosopher's Stone" or the transformative elixir in its medicinal role for "sick" or base metals</term>.

We indicate, indeed, that there are necessarily three different levels of medicines. One is of the **first order**; another of the **second**; and another of the **third**. 

I call a medicine of the **first order** any preparation of minerals which, when **projected** <term>original: *projecta*; the act of "projection," or casting the alchemical medicine onto a molten base metal to transform it</term> onto bodies diminished from perfection <note>i.e., base metals like lead or iron, which alchemists believed were "sick" or "incomplete" versions of gold</note>, impresses an alteration that does not bring about a sufficient completion; instead, the altered material happens to be changed and corrupted as the impression of that medicine totally evaporates. Of this kind is every addition of the color of the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> and **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, or of mixed **Venus** [copper], placed upon the top of the **cement** <term>original: *cementi*; a "cementation" process used to refine or color the surface of metals using corrosive mineral mixtures</term>, such as **ziniar** <note>original: *ziniar*; likely verdigris or copper acetate, used as a green pigment or chemical agent</note> and similar things. For this changes the body with a change that is not lasting, but rather diminishes itself through **exhalation** <note>evaporation or venting of vapors</note>. 

Indeed, we call a medicine of the **second order** every preparation which, when [applied] onto bodies diminished from perfection...

[bodies], <note>completing the word *corpora* from the previous page</note> when it is **projected** <term>original: *projecta*; the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto molten metal</term>, it alters it toward some degree of completion, yet leaves behind some differences of corruption entirely: such as is the **calcination** <term>original: *calcinatio*; heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder and remove volatile elements</term> of bodies, through which everything **fugitive** <note>original: *fugitivum*; refers to volatile elements that "flee" or evaporate when heated</note> is destroyed. Therefore, the work of the first order is called the **lesser**, that of the second the **middle**, and that of the third the **greater** <note>original: *opus majus*; the "Great Work" is the ultimate goal of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. And this is a sufficient distinction of all medicines.

### *Concerning the differences of medicines of the first, second, and third order; and first: Concerning medicines of the first order.*

### CHAPTER XX.

[of] medicines <note>original: *medicinarum*; completing the heading from the previous page: "Concerning the differences of medicines of the first, second, and third order."</note>, first those of the **first order**; then indeed those of the second; and finally, at last, those of the third. And first, we shall deliver the account of the medicines for bodies; after that, we shall provide a narration of the medicines of **quicksilver** <term>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</term> with complete discourse and in a proper order. 

We say, therefore, that of the medicine of the **first order** for bodies, one is for hard bodies, and another for soft ones. Of the hard bodies, one is for **Venus** <note>original: *Veneris*; the alchemical name for Copper</note>, another for **Mars** <note>original: *Martis*; the alchemical name for Iron</note>, and another for the **Moon** <note>original: *Lunae*; the alchemical name for Silver</note>. 

For Venus and Mars, the medicine is a pure **whitening** <term>original: *dealbatio*; the alchemical stage of *albedo*, turning a metal white or silver-like</term> of their substance. For the Moon, however, it is a **reddening** <term>original: *rubificatio*; the alchemical stage of *rubedo*, turning a metal red or gold-like</term>, along with a yellowing of pleasant brightness. Since for Venus and Mars, reddening is not brought about by a medicine of the first order with an opening of brightness. This is because they are entirely impure and not fit to receive the brightness of redness before a preparation bringing brightness comes to them. 

Let us therefore recount first all the medicines of Venus; and afterward those of Mars, which fall into the first order. There is, therefore, a medicine whitening Venus, one by means of quicksilver, and another by means of **arsenic** <note>original: *arsenicum*</note>. Indeed, the medicine whitening it by means of quicksilver is completed in this way: 

First, precipitated quicksilver is **dissolved** <term>original: *solvitur*; the process of turning a solid into a liquid state, often using acid</term>, and similarly the **calcination** <term>original: *calcinatio*; reducing a metal to a powder through heat</term> of Venus is dissolved. Both of these solutions are mixed into one; thereafter they are **coagulated** <term>original: *coagulantur*; the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid</term>, and the medicine is **projected** <note>original: *projiciatur*; the act of casting the alchemical powder onto molten metal</note> upon the body of the metal itself. For this whitens and cleanses. Furthermore, it is dissolved in another way.

And thus, let the work be repeated so many times upon it until a great quantity of quicksilver rests within it through complete firing. For this is a good whitening of the **first order**.

Otherwise, let quicksilver in its own nature be sublimated upon precipitated quicksilver so many times until it is **fixed** <note>original: *figatur*; rendered stable so that it no longer vaporizes when heated</note> within it and allows for fusion. From these, the substance of Venus is whitened. But indeed, it is whitened even better if the quicksilver is made permanent in all medicines. It is whitened...

### Concerning the medicine that colors the Moon yellow.

## CHAPTER XXII.

Following therefore the order of the things already mentioned, let us describe with certain truth the medicine that colors the substance of the **Moon** <term>original: *Luna*; the alchemical name for silver</term> yellow <note>original: *citrinantis*; this refers to the alchemical stage of *citrinatio*, the yellowing of a substance to bring it closer to the state of gold</note>, according to the rank of the first class. This is, specifically, the medicine which adheres to the metal in its very depths, and by adhering, provides color; whether this happens through its own nature, or through the artifice of this mastery <note>original: *magisterii*; the "Great Work" or the specialized skill of the alchemist</note>. Let us first, therefore, describe the medicine that adheres to the metal from its own innate root. Then, indeed, we shall describe the artifices by which we make a substance of any kind adhere with a firm penetration. 

We extract this medicine either from **sulfur**, or from **quicksilver** <term>original: *vivo argento*; mercury</term>, or from a mixture of both: but through sulfur it is achieved more sparingly, while through quicksilver it is more perfect. It is likewise extracted through certain useless mineral things which are not of this kind, such as **vitriol** <note>a sulfate mineral, often appearing as glassy crystals</note> and **copperas** <note>original: *cuperosa*; green vitriol or ferrous sulfate</note>, which is also called the "gum of copper" or the "dripping" of the same. ...since the medicine itself does not alter the substance by a

# FIRST PART.

<page-num>181</page-num>

...namely, in **Lead** <note>original: *Saturno*; the planet Saturn was the alchemical symbol for lead</note> there exists a volatile sulfurousness and a "flight" of **quicksilver** <term>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</term>; through which corruption is necessarily brought about, as well as its earthiness. Let a medicine therefore be made, which either removes one of these entirely, or masks them with a fine appearance <note>original: *palliando decoret*; literally "decorates by cloaking," suggesting a treatment that improves the metal's outward properties</note>, leaving behind only the other causes of imperfection. Because there is something unchangeable in bodies, which is innate in the root of those bodies, it cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order. Therefore, every medicine that removes that innate quality from the mixture is called a medicine not of the second order, but of the third and greater order. 

And because we find that the superfluities of "fugitive" <note>volatile substances that evaporate easily</note> elements are removed by the method of **calcination** <term>original: *calcinationis*; heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or oxide</term>, and that non-innate earthiness is removed by the repetition of **reduction** <term>original: *reductionis*; the process of restoring a metal from its calcined or oxidized state back to a metallic form</term>, it was therefore necessary to discover a medicine of the second order, which might mask what is innate, soften what is hard, and harden what is soft. Specifically, in hard and soft substances, it should perfectly establish a fulfillment that is not deceptive <note>original: *sophisticum*; referring to "sophistry" or chemical counterfeiting intended to mimic precious metals</note>, but a true **Gold-making** <note>original: *solificum*; from Sol, the Sun/Gold</note> or **Silver-making** <note>original: *lunificum*; from Luna, the Moon/Silver</note> of imperfect bodies. 

Since it is evident, therefore, that in soft bodies alone, the haste of melting <note>refers to metals with low melting points, like lead or tin</note> cannot be removed by the clever methods of this craft, nor the impurity innate in the root of their principles, it becomes necessary to investigate a medicine which, upon being cast <note>original: *projectione*; the "projection" of the medicine or elixir onto a base metal to trigger transmutation</note> upon them, might thicken their thinness, and by thickening them to the point of their melting ignition...

<summary>This page explains the limitations of "second order" medicines, which can fix volatility or hardness but cannot change the "innate root" of a metal like a "third order" medicine can. It details how processes like calcination and reduction are used to prepare metals for transmutation into true gold or silver.</summary>

# OF THE SECOND BOOK

<page-num>182</page-num>

[...of its soft]ness, it should harden its sufficiency: in hard bodies, it should thin the density, and by thinning, it should bring them to a speed of fusion sufficient for the property of ignition <note>The author is describing how alchemical "medicines" (transmuting agents) adjust the melting point and hardness of metals so they can be worked like gold or silver</note>. And it should decorate the darkness of both kinds of bodies by masking it: transforming the former into white, and the latter into the most perfect lemon-yellow <note>original: *citrinum*; the color associated with gold in alchemy</note>. This medicine does not differ from a medicine of the third order, except by the imperfection of a lesser preparation. And it does not differ in its essence, which consists of the **projection** <term>original: *projectione*; the act of throwing the alchemical catalyst onto a molten base metal</term> upon diverse bodies and the acceptance of pigments, but rather in its method of preparation. 

Similarly, a medicine for soft bodies requires one type of industrious preparation, thickening their thinness: while another, for hard bodies, requires thinning their density; the latter indeed acts by the method of consuming fire, while the former requires the management of preserving humidity. Let us therefore put forward a complete, universal discourse on the medicines of this second order, with a certain and true determination. And first, we shall treat all **lunar** <note>original: *lunares*; medicines intended to create silver, the metal of the Moon</note> medicines for all bodies diminished from perfection, along with the differences in their preparation. Then, indeed, the **solar** <note>original: *solares*; medicines intended to create gold, the metal of the Sun</note> ones, likewise with their own differences. 

We have already proven through our discourses that **sulfur** of every kind is a corrupter of perfection: whereas **quicksilver** <term>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</term> is a perfecter in the works of nature under complete regimens. Therefore, by no means changing nature, but

...imitating [nature], in those things in which it is possible for us to follow by our works, we likewise take **quicksilver** <term>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</term> in the mastery of this work, and in the medicine of each perfection—namely the **Lunar** <note>related to silver</note> and the **Solar** <note>related to gold</note>—as well for the imperfect bodies as for the coagulable quicksilver itself <note>mercury that is capable of being hardened into a solid metal</note>. The material of this medicine is therefore unique in itself for each kind, and it is that which is already sufficiently known <note>a common alchemical trope referring to a substance that is "known to all" but understood by few, often implying sulfur or mercury</note>. Take it, therefore, and if you wish, apply yourself to the practiced lunar medicine according to the order promised to you, and prepare it with the known methods of this mastery: the intention of which is that you divide the pure substance from it, and **fix** <term>original: *figas*; "fixing" is the process of making a volatile substance stable so it does not evaporate when heated</term> one part, while leaving the other part to be **waxed** <term>original: *cerandum*; "ceration" is the process of softening a hard substance to a wax-like consistency to help it melt and mix</term>. 

And so, by following the whole mastery, continue until you complete its attempted **fusion** <note>the melting together of substances</note>. If it should suddenly melt when added to hard metals, the work is perfected; but in soft metals, the opposite is true. For this medicine, when **projected** <term>original: *projecta*; the act of casting the alchemical powder or "medicine" onto a molten base metal</term> upon any of the imperfect bodies, changes that body into a perfect lunar [silver] body, provided that the known preparations for this medicine are achieved. If they are not achieved, it leaves the metal diminished, but it perfects it in another degree of perfection only so much as...

And when projected upon **Moon** <note>original: *Lunam*; silver</note> as well, it perfects it into the Solar fulfillment, and adds much value to it.

...with the skills of this mastery's work, a **medicine** <term>original: *medicina*; in alchemy, a substance (like the Philosopher's Stone) that "heals" base metals into noble ones</term> which adheres most closely to **quicksilver** <term>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</term>, and which is melted very easily and coagulates it. But it is frequently asked from which things this most pure substance of quicksilver can best be drawn. And we, in response, declare that it is drawn from those things in which it is contained. It exists both in **bodies** <note>the solid metals</note> and in quicksilver itself according to nature, since they are found to be of the same nature. However, it is drawn from bodies with greater difficulty, but from quicksilver more easily. Therefore, whatever kind of medicine it may be—whether in bodies or in the substance of quicksilver itself—the medicine of the **precious stone** <term>original: *lapis preciosus*; the Philosopher's Stone</term> is investigated.

Yet, because it sometimes happens that the medicine mixes well, and at other times it does not, we shall therefore describe the method of mixing; namely, how each substance may acquire a most profound entry into a body, or why a medicine might not penetrate. This method is achieved through the **dissolution** <term>original: *dissolutio*; the process of liquefying a solid</term> of that which enters, and through the dissolution of that which does not enter, and through the mixing of both solutions. For any substance of any kind is made penetrative when it is joined to another by its smallest parts <note>an intimate, almost atomic level of mixing</note>. This is accomplished through solution; and through solution, **fusion** <term>original: *fusio*; the state of being melted or fluid</term> is achieved even in things that are not naturally fusible.

...therefore they are more fit to enter and alter. And this is the reason why we **calcine** <term>original: *calcinamus*; the process of heating a substance until it turns to a dry powder</term> certain things which are not naturally of this kind—namely, so that they may be better **dissolved** <term>original: *solvantur*</term>. For this reason they are dissolved: so that the **bodies** <note>the solid metals being worked upon</note> might better receive an impression from them, and similarly, so that the bodies may be prepared and cleansed by these means.

Alternatively, we grant "entry" to those things which, due to their own thickness, do not allow penetration. This is achieved through the multiple repetition of the **sublimation** <term>original: *sublimationis*; the alchemical process of heating a substance into vapor so it collects as a purified solid</term> of non-flammable spirits upon them—specifically **arsenic** <term>original: *arsenici*</term> and **quicksilver** <term>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</term> which are not **fixed** <note>meaning they are still volatile and easily evaporated by heat</note>—or by the multiple repetition of the dissolution of whatever substance lacks the power of entry.

However, a wise precaution must be taken regarding the entry of things that do not naturally mix, so that the body is dissolved; the intention being that the body is changed and altered through these means, and that those substances whose purpose is to "enter" with alteration are also dissolved. Yet, let there not be a dissolution of all parts, but only of some. After this, let that body—and no other—be **imbibed** <term>original: *imbibatur*; to soak or saturate a solid with a liquid</term> time after time. For through this beneficial process, the medicine necessarily gains entry into that specific body alone; this result does not necessarily happen with any other body.

Therefore, from these ingenious methods, it is necessary for any substance to have the power of entry (which depends on its natural properties) and to alter the body through the discovered mixture. Thus, with this discourse, the number of the **ten medicines** <note>likely referring to a specific series of alchemical preparations or stages in the author's system</note> is completed with the full authority of its tradition.

It remains, therefore, for us to pass to the medicine of the third order.

->*Concerning the medicine of the third order.*<-

### CHAPTER XXV.

The medicine of this order is twofold, namely **solar** and **lunar** <note>In alchemical symbolism, "solar" (pertaining to the Sun) refers to the transmutation of metals into gold, while "lunar" (pertaining to the Moon) refers to the transmutation into silver</note>. And yet, it is one in essence, and the manner of its action is similar. For this reason, it is called a "single medicine" by our ancestors whose writings we have read. However, there is an addition of a yellowing color <note>original: *citrinantis coloris*; this refers to the process of "citrinization," a stage in the Great Work where the substance turns yellow before reaching the final red color</note> which is perfected from the purest substance of **fixed sulfur** <term>original: *sulphuris fixi*; sulfur that has been chemically stabilized so it no longer evaporates when exposed to high heat</term>. This color marks the difference between the lunar and the solar medicines: the solar contains this yellowing substance within itself, while the lunar does not.

Nevertheless, this third order is called the "order of the Great Work" <term>original: *majoris operis*; the *Magnum Opus*, the ultimate goal of alchemy to create the Philosopher’s Stone</term>. This is because it requires the industry of greater wisdom <note>original: *sagacitatis*; a combination of intellectual keenness and practical skill</note> in its administration and in the preparation of its perfection; it also requires a longer labor to reach the completion of truth.

Therefore, the medicine of this order does not differ in essence from the medicine of the second order in any way, except through the most subtle steps of preparation during its creation, and through a more persistent duration of labor. We shall describe all these steps as we complete this discourse, providing the method of preparation in full along with its underlying causes, clear experiments, and the many stages of administration for this third order.

<summary>This page introduces the "Medicine of the Third Order," the highest stage of alchemy also known as the Great Work. It explains that while gold-making (solar) and silver-making (lunar) medicines are essentially the same, they differ in the use of fixed sulfur and the intensity of preparation required.</summary>

# PART ONE.

For the **solar medicine** requires one degree in its complete preparation of pigments, while the **lunar** requires another; the former indeed requires the administration of a sulfur that colors it, but the latter does not. Therefore, let us first describe the method of administering the lunar medicine: and it is this—that you should take its known stone <note>original: *lapidem*; the starting material or "prime matter" for the work</note>, and by the method of separation, divide its purest part and set it aside. From that part which is the purest, you must **fix** <term>original: *figas*; the process of "fixation," making a volatile substance stable so that it does not evaporate when exposed to high heat</term> a portion, and leave a portion of it unfixed.

And when it has been fixed, dissolve what is soluble from it; but that which is not soluble, you must send to **calcination** <term>original: *calcinationem*; the process of heating a substance to a high temperature in the presence of air to reduce it to a fine powder or "calx"</term>. From this point, repeat the solution upon the same material until again that which is soluble is dissolved completely. Thus, let this order be maintained until the greater quantity is dissolved.

After this, preserve it by roasting it lightly in a moderate fire, until a greater fire can be administered to it according to its requirements. After this, maintain the first order of solution until everything soluble from it is dissolved once again; and **coagulate** <note>original: *coagula*; to turn a liquid back into a solid form</note> it again, and preserve it again in a moderate fire, until once more a greater fire can be administered for its perfection. 

Therefore, repeat all these orders of preparation upon it four times, and finally calcine it in its own way; and thus you have sufficiently governed the most precious "earth" of the stone through your administration. Then indeed, the preserved...

...of the [remaining] unfixed part, combine its quantity with this processed part of "earth" <note>In alchemy, "earth" refers to the solid, stable residue left after distillation or sublimation</note> by a subtle and clever method, down to the smallest particles. In this way, apply this method for its intended "lifting"—known as the method of **sublimation** <term>original: *sublimationis*; a process where a solid is heated until it rises as vapor and collects again as a solid in the upper part of the vessel</term>—until the fixed part is completely lifted together with the unfixed. 

If this does not occur, add more of the unfixed part to it again and again until there is enough to cause its elevation. Once it has been elevated, repeat its sublimation until, through this repeated treatment, the whole becomes **fixed** <term>original: *figatur*; the process of "fixation," making a substance resistant to fire so it no longer evaporates</term>. When it has been fixed, soak it once more with a quantity of the unfixed part, using the method known to you, until the whole is lifted again. Then let it be fixed again until it achieves easy fusion <note>meaning it melts like wax without evaporating</note> when heated to a glow. For this is the **medicine** <note>the "elixir" or "philosopher's stone"</note> that transforms every metal body lacking perfection, and every kind of **mercury** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; literally "living silver" or quicksilver</note>, into the most perfect **lunar body** <note>original: *lunare corpus*; silver</note>.

The more often this cycle of completion and abundance is repeated, the more the power of this **medicine** <note>the alchemical elixir or "Philosopher's Stone" in its silver-producing form</note> is multiplied, its quality is improved, and its degree of perfection is greatly increased. 

And so that we may not be "bitten by the wicked," <note>original: *ne mordeamur ab impiis*; a common alchemical phrase expressing the desire to keep secrets away from the ignorant or those who would misuse the knowledge</note> we will describe the entire completion of this **mastery** <term>original: *magisterii*; the "Magistery" or Great Work of alchemy</term> in a brief and well-known summary. Its intent is that the stone and its addition be perfectly cleansed through the method of sublimation. From this point, through skillful means, the **volatile** <term>original: *volativum*; a substance that easily evaporates or escapes the fire</term> part is **fixed** <term>original: *figatur*; made solid and resistant to heat</term>; then the fixed is made volatile again, and once more the volatile is made fixed. 

In this sequence, the most precious **secret** <term>original: *arcanum*; a hidden or celestial mystery</term> is completed—a secret that stands above every other science in this world, and is an incomparable treasure. You must practice it with the greatest persistence of labor and with the duration of immense meditation. For through these things you will find it, and without them, you will not.

...through skillful caution, this can happen during the preparation of the **stone** <term>original: *lapidis*; the Philosopher's Stone</term>: until it transforms **mercury** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; literally "living silver," the base material for transmutation</note> into an infinite **gold-making** <term>original: *solificum*; from *Sol* (the Sun/gold); the power to transform base metals into gold</term> and true **silver-making** <term>original: *lunificum*; from *Luna* (the Moon/silver); the power to transform base metals into silver</term> substance; and this depends on nothing except the multiplication of that substance. 

Now, therefore, let the sublime, blessed, and glorious God of all natures be praised, who has revealed to us the sequence of all **medicines** <note>elixirs or tinctures used for transmutation</note> along with the practical experience of them. Through the goodness of His inspiration and the persistence of our own labor, we have sought this out; we have seen it with our eyes and touched with our hands the completion of that which was investigated through our **mastery** <term>original: *magisterio*; the specialized knowledge and practice of alchemy</term>. 

But if we have indeed veiled our meaning, let the **son of doctrine** <note>original: *doctrinæ filius*; a traditional term for a dedicated student or initiate of alchemical secrets</note> not be surprised. For we have not hidden it from him; rather, we have handed it down to the wicked and the dishonest in such a language that it must necessarily remain hidden from the foolish. Yet, by that same language, the wise are drawn to seek out its discovery. 

Therefore, you sons of doctrine, search diligently, and you will find this most excellent gift of God, reserved for you alone. But you, foolish sons of wickedness and immense, malevolent depravity, flee from this science! It is your enemy and adversary, and it will only lead you into the misery of poverty. For by the judgment of divine providence, this gift of God is entirely hidden from you and completely denied. 

Having now investigated the methods of all the medicines, and following the original path of our proposal, we must now move on to those things which signify the perfection of this mastery, along with the evidence for their proofs.

<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophy of the alchemical "Stone" to the practical, experimental methods used to verify its success. Part One.

I

### On the proofs of perfection and the trials of the perfection of the work.

### Chapter XXVII.

Having set aside, therefore, the obvious trials—of which we have made no mention since they are known to everyone and are certain even without the skill of sagacity, namely weight, color, and malleability under the hammer—let us cautiously attempt the trials of the arts. We must see whether the **projection** <term>original: *projectio*; the final stage where the "Stone" is cast upon base metal to transform it</term> of the management of this art brings about a completion with truth. These trials are: **cupellation** <term>original: *cineritium*; a process of refining gold or silver in a porous vessel made of bone ash</term>, **cementation** <term>original: *cementum*; a method of purifying gold by heating it with reactive salts</term>, **ignition** <note>original: *ignitio*; heating the metal to a glow to check for impurities that might burn off or change color</note>, **melting**, exposure to **acidic vapors** <note>original: *vapores acutorum*; testing the metal's resistance to corrosion by vinegar or mineral acid fumes</note>, the trial by mixing with **burning sulfur**, quenching, the repetition of **calcination** <term>original: *calcinatio*; reducing a metal to a powder through heat</term> and **reduction** <term>original: *reductio*; restoring a calcined powder back to its metallic form</term>, and the easy or difficult reception of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury; gold and silver readily form amalgams with mercury, while other materials do not</note>. Therefore, we must begin with the first in the sequence; thereafter, let us proceed through the others in their proper order according to our promise, explaining their known causes.

---

->*On Cupellation.*<-

### Chapter XXVIII.

Let us therefore speak concerning cupellation, with all its most manifest causes and the manner of its fixation. Only the **solar** <note>original: *solaris*; referring to gold, associated with the Sun</note> and **lunar** <note>original: *lunaris*; referring to silver, associated with the Moon</note> substance endures in the cupellation...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the "trials of perfection," specifically focusing on cupellation (the trial by ashes). It explains why gold and silver—the "perfect bodies"—survive these intense tests while base metals do not, grounded in the alchemical theory that metals are composed of varying purity and proportions of quicksilver (mercury) and "earthiness."</meta>

### OF THE SECOND BOOK

...enduring in the trial. Therefore, inquiring into the true differences in the substance of these **perfect bodies** <note>Gold and silver are termed "perfect" because they resist oxidation and do not diminish in the refiner's fire.</note>, and likewise the causes of **cupellation** <term>original: *cineritium*; a refining process where metal is melted in a cupel made of bone ash; the ash absorbs impurities, leaving pure gold or silver behind</term>; we shall investigate why some metals, which have fallen short of perfection, endure more in the trial of this **mastery** <note>original: *magisterium*; the alchemical art or the specific process of transmutation</note>, and why others endure less.

However, the secret of these two bodies has been sufficiently explained by us in the depth of their substance. Namely, it is because their primary root was a large quantity of **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; literally "living silver," the alchemical name for mercury</note> and its purest essence, which was very subtle at first, but later thickened until it could withstand melting under heat. Therefore, whichever metals have fallen short of perfection and possess more **earthiness** <term>original: *terreitas*; refers to the presence of impure, non-metallic, or stony matter within a metal</term> will endure less in this trial; whereas those with less [earthiness] endure more. 

This is because the parts of the perfect metals adhere more strongly, owing to the extreme subtlety of their components which mix and unite themselves most thoroughly. Furthermore, those bodies that are of a greater "thinness," or on the contrary, those bodies of greater "thickness" (density) than those which consist in perfection, must necessarily be separated entirely from a mixture, for they do not share the same melting point, and thus they are parted. Those metals that partake of a smaller quantity of quicksilver are more easily separated from a mixture. It is clear, then, that since **Saturn** <note>The alchemical name for lead</note> is composed of much earthiness and a small quantity of quicksilver, and is of an easy thinness of melting, which

### CAP. XXIX.

We have said, therefore, that certain bodies are burned more, and others truly less, by the method of calcination <note>original: *calcinationis modum*; the process of heating a metal to high temperatures in air to oxidize it</note>: specifically, those which contain a greater quantity of combustible sulfur are burned more, while those containing less are burned less. Because Gold <note>original: *Sol*, literally "the Sun"</note>, therefore, contains a smaller quantity of sulfur than the other bodies, it is the least burned by the inflammation of fire among all other mineral bodies. 

Silver <note>original: *Luna*, literally "the Moon"</note>, however, following Gold among all remaining bodies, partakes in a smaller quantity of sulfur [than the base metals], yet it has more than Gold. Therefore, it can withstand the ignition of inflammation for a shorter space of time than Gold and other things burning by a similar nature. Copper <note>original: *Venus*</note> withstands even less, because it possesses more sulfur and a greater "earthiness" than Gold and Silver; for this reason, it tolerates inflammation less than they do. Tin <note>original: *Jupiter*</note> truly withstands less than Copper, but possesses more [sulfur] than Gold and Silver...

If, however, combustible bodies are mixed with it, according to the nature of their combustion, it necessarily happens that **Mars** is burned and ignited. 

Since, therefore, the **cement** <term>cementum; a mixture of dry, reactive ingredients like salt and vitriol used to refine gold</term> is composed of inflammable things, the necessary cause of its invention is clear; and it was so that all combustible things might be burned away. ...[more than] the **Moon** <term>Silver</term>, but truly less [than] **Mars** <term>Iron</term>: and still less **Jupiter** <term>Tin</term>: and still less **Venus** <term>Copper</term>: and least of all **Saturn** <term>Lead</term>. <note>The text is concluding a comparison from the previous page regarding how much "inflammation" or corrosive heat each metal can withstand.</note> Let us therefore describe the method of **cementation** <term>cementum; a dry refining process where metal plates are layered with corrosive minerals and heated</term>. Since the knowledge of this is most necessary for us in the examination of perfection <note>i.e., testing the purity of gold</note>, we say that its composition consists of inflammable things. And all things of this kind are blackening, volatile <note>original: "fugientes," literally "fleeing," referring to substances that evaporate or sublime when heated</note>, penetrating, and burning: Such as **vitriol** <term>metallic sulfates</term>, **Salar** <note>Likely "Sal Armoniacum" (sal ammoniac) or a variant of "Sal Alkali"</note>, and **flower of copper** <term>æris flos; copper oxide or verdigris</term>, and crushed ancient potter’s clay <note>lapis figuli; used as a porous base to hold the chemicals against the metal</note>, and a single portion of **sulfur**, or none at all, and male urine, and similar sharp and penetrating substances. 

Therefore, all these things are mixed into a paste with male urine; and applied over thin plates of that metal which one intends to investigate by the judgment of this test. Then indeed, in a closed earthenware vessel, let the plates be spread out over an iron grate: in such a way, however, that one of them does not touch another, so that the power of the fire may run through to them freely and equally. And thus let it be kept in the earthenware vessel in a strong fire for three days. 

Caution must be taken, however, so that the plates are heated to redness, but not melted <note>original: "fundantur"</note>. After the third day, you will find the plates clean of all impurity if the metal was in a state of perfection <note>meaning it was pure gold or silver that resisted the corrosive paste</note>. If, however, it was not, you will find them entirely corrupted and consumed by **calcination** <term>the process of reducing a metal to a powder or ash through intense heat</term>. Therefore, it is necessary for these metals to be separated from the mixture without their total essence being corrupted or lost. ->On Melting.<-

### CHAPTER XXXI.

Let us therefore provide a sufficient account of **pouring** <note>original: "infusionis"; the act of casting or melting metal into a mold</note> according to the testing of all metallic bodies for their precise **ignition** <term>the process of heating a substance until it glows, but before it reaches its melting point</term>. We state, therefore: that there is a unique melting point of perfection that occurs with ignition, but not with just any kind of glowing heat. It must be an ignition where the metallic body does not turn completely white <note>original: "albescit"; referring to white heat which can indicate excessive temperature or impurity</note>, nor does it take on a dark or murky color <note>original: "fuscedo"</note> when the fire is applied, and where the body does not immediately melt and liquefy into a flow the very instant it begins to glow.

Consequently, when a weak metal melts under the slightest pressure of fire, or without glowing at all, or with a dull, dark glow, such a substance must be judged as one of the **imperfect bodies** <term>base metals like Lead or Tin, which melt at low temperatures</term> resulting from a flawed process. Furthermore, if the metal is allowed to cool after melting and its glow turns instantly to blackness—and if, for this reason, it loses its glow before it has even solidified—then that body has not reached perfection, regardless of what kind of metal it may be. Rather, it is proper to judge that this body’s softness arises from im-

...the types of the **perfection of bodies** <note>original: "perfectionis corporum"; in alchemy, "bodies" refers to metals, and "perfection" refers to the state of being a noble metal like gold or silver</note>. And if indeed, through the laborious application of a strong and violent fire, the metal glows intensely before it melts, shining with an inestimable, completely white radiance, the body has been altered not toward perfection, but toward **hardness** <note>In this context, "hardness" refers to base metals that are difficult to melt, specifically iron</note>. For this reason, if it is removed from the fire after its **fusion** <term>the process of melting a solid into a liquid state</term> and it hardens so suddenly that it ceases to flow while it is still glowing hot, then it is a body that possesses neither **lunar** nor **solar** perfection <note>original: "lunaris aut solaris"; referring to the qualities of Silver (the Moon) and Gold (the Sun)</note>, regardless of what kind of metal or preparation was used. Instead, it must be categorized under the nature and characteristics of **Mars** <term>the alchemical name for Iron</term>.

It is clear, therefore, from what has been stated, that a threefold **ignition** <term>the state of a metal when it is heated to the point of glowing</term>—or rather, the melting of these substances—can be observed through **experiment** <note>original: "experimento"; this refers to practical laboratory observation rather than just theoretical study</note>:

*   The first type of glow is **dark** or dim.
*   The second is **red and clear**.
*   The third is **bright white**, shining with radiance.

Through the test of reason, it is proven that the first belongs to **soft bodies** <note>base metals with low melting points, such as lead or tin</note>; the second belongs to **perfect bodies** <note>noble metals, gold and silver, which glow red before melting</note>; and the third belongs to **hard bodies** <note>metals like iron that require extreme heat</note>.

But he who desires to investigate the specific degree of all these various glows should melt all fusible bodies and consider the amount of fire required for a complete and perfect melting. By such consideration, he may gather the differences in the degrees of all the signs of fusion. In this way, and no other, will he find the truth. Let this be your guiding example in all the materials we have examined or shall examine. Therefore, let these words spoken regarding fusion suffice.

### *On the exposure [of metals] to the vapors of sharp substances.*

->C H A P T E R XXXII.<-

Continuing, then, to the completion of our discourse, let us speak of the exposure of bodies <note>In alchemy, "bodies" (corpora) refers to metals</note> to the vapors of sharp substances <term>acutes; these are corrosive or acidic materials such as mineral salts or vinegar</term>. We say, therefore, that we see perfected bodies exposed to the vapor of sharp things—specifically of bitter and acidic substances—either produce no "bloom" at all, or emit a most beautiful celestial bloom <note>original: "coelestinum florem"; this refers to a sky-blue or azure-colored surface patina or chemical reaction product</note>. However, the purest Sun <term>the alchemical name for Gold</term> does not bloom; but we find that the Moon <term>the alchemical name for Silver</term>, or Sun that is not pure, when exposed to the vapors of sharp things, does bloom with a most beautiful celestial color—the Sun more beautifully than the Moon. Therefore, imitating nature, let us likewise create this celestial color in our prepared bodies, which is perfected through the goodness of quicksilver <term>mercury</term>, as has been sufficiently described by us in the preceding parts of our discourse. 

Therefore, whichever prepared bodies are placed over the vapors of sharp things and do not produce this beautiful celestial color are not yet in a state of total completion in their preparation. On the other hand, there is a certain type of body that, in the trial of bitter substances <note>original: "examine ponticorum"; a test using pungent or astringent salts</note>, produces a reddish-brown or yellowish-brown bloom on its surface, mixed with a green color; of this nature is Mars <term>the alchemical name for Iron</term>. Yet another type is green...

...a brownish green blooms on the surface, mixed with a murky sky-blue; and of this nature is Venus <term>the alchemical name for Copper</term>. Yet another is a clear white; and this belongs to Jupiter <term>the alchemical name for Tin</term>. Since, therefore, the most perfect body blooms the least or not at all—or if it does bloom, it does so only after a very long span of time—and since Jupiter indeed blooms its "gumminess" <note>original: "gummositatem"; in alchemical theory, this refers to the fatty or sulfurous impurities that prevent a metal from being Gold</note> both the least and the most slowly among those bodies that fall short of the completion of perfection. Therefore, through this test of our mastery <term>magisterium; the alchemical Art or the Great Work</term>, we consider Jupiter to approach perfection most closely in the work of the greater order <note>the process of transmuting base metals into the "higher" metals, Gold and Silver</note>. 

Through this test, therefore, one may seek to understand the specific temperament <note>the balance of elemental qualities</note> of which a body consists, provided you have correctly considered the order of these things which we have described in this chapter. If you fail, however, attribute it to the foolishness of your own rashness.

->*On the quenching of heated bodies.*<-

In the test of quenching <note>original: "extinctionis"; the process of heating a metal to redness and plunging it into a liquid</note>, let us now bring forth a complete account. First, if a heated body is quenched in a liquid, and the lunar <term>Silver</term> body does not become white, and the solar <term>Gold</term> body does not become a shining citron-yellow, but instead is changed into a foreign color, <note>The text breaks off here, suggesting the next page will describe the implications of these "foreign" color changes.</note>

...there is no alteration in the completion of our mastery <term>magisterium; the alchemical Art or the Great Work</term>. For instance, if during the repetition of its ignition <note>heating the metal until it glows red</note> and its quenching in waters created with the addition of salts or alums of any kind, the metal should show a dross <note>original: "scoria"; the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting or testing</note> bordering on blackness upon its surface; or if, by quenching it in sulfurs and then repeating the ignition after that quenching, much of the metal vanishes; or if it stains itself with a foul blackness; or if it entirely breaks apart under the pressure of the hammer: then the craftsmanship of the work is deceptive. 

Or, if after a "cementation" <note>a process of heating a metal surrounded by a powdered chemical mixture to purify it or change its color</note> made of a mixture of sal ammoniac <term>ammonium chloride</term>, verdigris <term>green copper acetate</term>, and child's urine—or other things of a similar nature—the metal is placed in the fire, and after that ignition and a quenching in sulfur, it should lose its proper lunar <term>Silver</term> or solar <term>Gold</term> color entirely when it is drawn out; or if it should create dross, it is certain that the body still remains in a state of sophistical <note>original: "sophistica"; in alchemy, this refers to a metal that has been made to look like gold or silver through surface treatments but is not the true substance</note> corruption. 

However, we hand down to you one certain general rule: because in both the examinations already mentioned and those yet to be mentioned by us, if the body undergoes a change in any of the distinct markers of perfection—namely in its weight or its color—the craftsman has pursued the work not rightly, but fantastically <note>illusorily or based on appearances rather than reality</note>. Such a result is not a gainful thing, but is rather the way of ruin.

### *On the mixture of burning sulfur.*

### C H A P. XXXIV.

Similarly, the mastery <term>magisterium; the successful completion of the alchemical Art</term> is proven by the mixture of sulfur, to see whether it stands in a state of perfection. Since by our experience we have found that when sulfur is mixed with the metallic bodies, it burns some more and others less; and by our artifice, we have found that some return from this combustion <note>the process of heating a substance in the presence of sulfur, often resulting in ores or sulfides</note> to their original state, while others do not. 

And from this, a difference can be noted between those bodies that fall short of perfection and those prepared with a deceptive <note>original: "sophistico"; refers to a metal treated to look like gold or silver but lacking the true internal properties</note> finish. Therefore, among the various bodies of every kind, we find that the Sun <term>Gold</term> is burned the least by sulfur. After this comes Jupiter <term>Tin</term>; then the Moon <term>Silver</term>; and after that Saturn <term>Lead</term>. Indeed, Venus <term>Copper</term> is burned more easily than all of these, and Mars <term>Iron</term> is burned most easily of all by the oiliness <note>original: "oleaginitatem"; alchemists believed sulfur contained a flammable, oily principle that caused metals to corrode or "burn"</note> of the sulfur. 

By this test, it is noted which metal approaches more or less to the nature of the perfect. Furthermore, from the diversity of colors appearing after the combustion of the bodies, it can be investigated in which category the altered body consists, based on the root of its nature. For the Sun indeed produces an intense lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinum"</note> or a clear red; the Moon truly a black mixed with sky-blue; Jupiter a black mixed with a moderate tint of redness; and Saturn a dark brown-black with much redness:

->*Concerning calcination and reduction.*<-

### CHAPTER XXXV.

Next, we must investigate the examination of repeating calcination <term>the process of heating a metal in air to turn it into a powder</term> and reduction <term>the process of restoring a metallic powder back into solid metal</term>. We suggest, therefore, that because the "bodies of perfection" <note>referring to Gold and Silver, which alchemists believed were chemically "perfect" and indestructible</note> have been discovered, they lose nothing of their color, weight, or quantity—about which one must be very careful—nor of their luster, no matter how many times these operations are repeated or multiplied upon them, due to the excellence of their nature. And therefore, if through the repetition of these methods of calcination and reduction from the ash <note>original: "calce"; literally 'lime' or powder</note>, any kind of altered metal loses anything of its quality, one must judge that the artisan has pursued his investigation by deceptive means <note>original: "sophistice"; referring to "sophistical" alchemy, which produced results that looked like gold or silver but were actually base metal alloys</note>. For this reason, you should practice these operations yourself, so that you may know them.
