### *On the easy reception of Quicksilver.*

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

Now, therefore, it has been truly made clear to you that bodies existing in perfection contain a very large quantity of silver <note>original: "argenti"; here referring to the "mercurial" or "silvery" essence alchemists believed was the core of all metals</note>. For this reason, they are most friendly to <term>Quicksilver [Mercury]</term>. Consequently, it must be concluded that those bodies which more "amicably" drink in Quicksilver approach closer to perfection. The evidence of this is the easy reception of Quicksilver by a <term>Solar [Gold]</term> or <term>Lunar [Silver]</term> body of perfection. Therefore, for this reason, if a transformed body <note>a base metal that has been chemically treated</note> does not easily receive Quicksilver into its own substance, it must necessarily be very far from the highest degree of perfection.

Here, we shall draw together into a summary the mastery that has been dispersed throughout these brief chapters. We say, therefore: that the sum of the intention of the whole work is nothing else, but that

the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "lapis"; the Philosopher's Stone or the primary material of the work</note>—already known from the previous chapters—is taken. Then indeed, with the constant application of labor, the work of the **first degree of sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then condenses back into a solid, used here for purification</note> should be persistently applied to it. Through this, it is cleansed from corrupting impurity; this is the perfection of <term>sublimation</term>, namely, that the Stone is refined by it until it reaches the final purity of subtlety and at last becomes volatile. After this, let it be fixed by methods of <term>fixation</term> <note>the process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated</note> until it remains at rest even in the harshness of the fire. And here lies the goal of the **second degree of preparation**.

But the Stone is similarly administered in a **third degree**, which consists in the final completion of the preparation. This involves making the already fixed Stone volatile once more through methods of sublimation, then making the volatile fixed again, and the fixed dissolved; then the dissolved is made volatile once more, and the volatile again fixed. This continues until it flows and transforms in a certain <term>solar-making</term> <note>original: "folifico"; the power to turn base metals into Gold</note> and <term>lunar-making</term> <note>original: "lunifico"; the power to turn base metals into Silver</note> completion. 

From the repetition of this third degree of preparation in the <term>Medicine</term> <note>the final alchemical elixir or powder</note>, there results a diversity of multiplication, quality, and transformation. Thus, from these medicines, some may transmute a body into solar or true lunar perfection sevenfold, some tenfold, some a hundredfold, some a thousandfold, and some even to an infinite degree. From this point, therefore, and finally, let it be tested whether the <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the completed Great Work of alchemy</note> stands in perfection.

### What method the author followed in teaching the art.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

But so that we ourselves may not be bitten by the envious <note>original: "invidis"; in alchemical literature, the "envious" are those who would misuse the secret for greed or harmful purposes, often depicted as those who begrudge others their success</note>, we declare that we have not handed down our <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "scientiam"; referring here specifically to the "science" or systematic study of alchemy</note> in a continuous narrative, but have scattered it across various chapters. This was done for this reason: because if it had been handed down continuously, both the honest and the wicked would have seized upon it indiscriminately. We have likewise hidden it even where we spoke most openly: not, however, through riddles, but by addressing the craftsman through a plain sequence of words.

And we have written it in this manner of speech just as it occurred to our mind—we who wrote it—through the grant of only God most high and blessed, sublime and glorious, who gives to and takes from whom He wills. Therefore, let the **son of doctrine** <note>original: "doctrinæ filius"; a traditional term for a dedicated student of alchemy who follows the "father" or teacher into the mysteries</note> not despair: for if he seeks it, he shall find it, not through doctrine alone, but through the investigation of Nature's own movement. 

For he who seeks knowledge through the goodness of his own **diligence** <note>original: "industria"; implies hard work, ingenuity, and careful experimentation rather than passive reading</note> will find it. But he who seeks it only by the inspection of books will arrive most slowly at this most precious art. For we have published for ourselves alone this art investigated by ourselves alone, and not for others; yet it is most true and entirely certain. We have therefore attracted only the prudent to the art,

# GEBER THE ARABIAN
## PHILOSOPHER
### AND
## ALCHEMIST
### *The Book of the most acute investigation of the Mastery.*

We hand down to you the investigation of this **noble science** <note>original: "nobilis scientiæ"; alchemy was traditionally called the "Noble Science" because it dealt with the transmutation of metals and the perfection of nature</note>, derived from continuous and frequent diligence in the work, and from an intense zeal for the task, as well as from the most profound thoughts and various related matters. We do this so that the following volumes may be better and more suitably understood by you, and so that once these things are understood and certain, other things devised and examined concerning them may more easily and promptly be brought to effect through practice. And what else is it to investigate the perfection of the art, than to investigate through **reason** <note>original: "rationem"; emphasizes that alchemy is a rational, logical pursuit, not merely superstition</note>, to attempt, and to test the subtleties and **methods** <note>original: "ingenia"; refers to the clever techniques or mental frameworks used by the alchemist</note> of these things, until through working, searching, and experiencing, they have reached the intended completion? For in this book of ours, whatever we have previously investigated, we have written according to the reasoning of our mind, pertaining, I say, to the art—

# OF INVESTIGATION

[matters] pertaining <note>original: "pertinentia"; this completes the sentence fragment from the previous page</note> to the art. Let no one think, however, that we composed this investigation before our other book, which is entitled ***The Summary of the Perfection of the Mastery*** <note>This refers to the *Summa Perfectionis Magisterii*, arguably the most influential alchemical text of the Middle Ages, attributed to Geber</note>. In that work, we have fully determined those things which we have seen and touched, according to the order of our science; namely, through experience and certain knowledge. These are things which we have noted and devised through our scrutiny concerning the effects of natural and mineral things, and the various **transmutations** <note>original: "transmutationibus"; the transformation of base metals into noble ones like gold or silver</note> appearing in the work. 

We have proposed our previously composed *Summary* alongside this investigation as a contents, as it were, to be declared upon it. Therefore, by right, this book precedes that one: since through this book of investigations, the science seeks the knowledge of the perfecting thing. Since, therefore, this science treats the **imperfect bodies** of minerals insofar as it is fitting to perfect them, we first consider these two things: namely, **imperfection** and **perfection**. 

Around these two things, we found our intention. Concerning the things that perfect and those that corrupt, according to what we have investigated through our experience, we have composed this book—because opposites placed next to each other shine more clearly <note>This is a common medieval philosophical maxim: *contraria iuxta se posita magis elucescunt*</note>. 

The thing which perfects in minerals is the substance of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum; mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" or passive principle of all metals</term> and **sulfur** <term>the "father" or active, combustible principle of metals</term>, mixed proportionally and, through long and tempered **boiling** <term>decoctio; a process of heating a substance to mature or purify it</term> in—

# OF THE MASTERY

...thickened and fixed within the bowels of the clean earth; produced with the preservation of its **radical humidity** <note>term: "humiditatis radicalis"; the innate, essential moisture thought to hold a substance together and provide flexibility</note> which is non-corrupting, and brought to a solid substance that is fusible with proper ignition and capable of being extended under a hammer <note>i.e., malleable</note>. Indeed, through the definition of this perfecting nature, we can more easily arrive at the knowledge of the corrupting thing. 

This is to be understood from the opposite sense: namely, an impure substance of **mercury** <note>original: "argenti vivi"; literally "living silver"</note> and **sulfur**, mixed without due proportion, either too little or too much **decocted** <note>term: "decocta"; a process of "cooking" or maturing through heat within the earth</note> in the bowels of the (perhaps) unclean earth; neither rightly thickened nor fixed; having humidities that are combustible and corrupting, and of a thin and porous substance. Or, having a melting point without the due heat of ignition (or none at all), and likewise not enduring the hammer. 

You will find the first definition <note>that of perfection</note> in these two bodies: namely, in the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> and the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, according to the perfection of each. But you will find the second <note>that of imperfection</note> in these four: namely, in **Tin**, **Lead**, **Iron**, and **Copper**, according to the imperfection of each. 

And because these imperfect bodies are not reducible to health and perfection unless the opposite is operated within them—that is, that what is manifest be hidden, and what is hidden be made manifest <note>A core alchemical principle: changing the outward "accidental" properties of a metal to reveal its inner, noble essence</note>—which operation and opposition is completed through **preparation**, therefore preparation must be applied to them. To prepare, therefore, is to take away the superfluous and to supply what is absent, 

<summary>This page defines the alchemical difference between perfect metals (gold and silver) and imperfect metals (tin, lead, iron, copper) based on their internal balance of sulfur, mercury, and moisture. It introduces "preparation" as the necessary process to remove impurities and "heal" base metals into noble ones.</summary>

# OF INVESTIGATION

[To sup]ply, and thus to instill true perfection in them. However, **perfect bodies** <note>i.e., Gold and Silver, which were already considered "mature" by nature</note> do not need this preparation, since they are not polluted. But they require such a preparation by which their parts are more refined, and reduced from their **corporality** to a fixed **spirituality** <note>term: "spiritualitatem"; in alchemy, this refers to a state where a substance is purified and made volatile or subtle, yet here it is "fixed" so it remains stable under heat</note>. The intention of this preparation is to make from them a **fixed spiritual body**, that is, to attenuate and refine them much more than they were before. 

Regarding the preparations of all these, according to our investigation, we will treat them sufficiently in their proper place in this book. After these have been sufficiently prepared, they will be fit for the **white or red elixir** <note>term: "elixir album vel rubeum"; the white elixir transmutes metals into silver, while the red elixir transmutes them into gold</note> to be made from them through the **mastery** <note>term: "magisterium"; the successful completion of the alchemical process or the "Great Work"</note>. 

However, we find that the moderns write for us of only a single **stone** <note>original: "lapidem"; the Philosopher's Stone</note> for the complete white and red work; which we concede to be true. For from whatever thing the white or red elixir is prepared, there is nothing there but **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> and **sulfur**, of which one does nothing without the other, nor can it exist. 

And therefore it is called "one stone" by the philosophers: although it may be extracted from many bodies or things. For to extract it from a thing in which it is not, this would be foolish and vain to think—as certain fools do—which was never the intention of the philosophers; although they say many things, and this through **similitude** <note>original: "similitudinem"; the use of metaphors, allegories, and analogies to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note>. And because all metallic bodies are composed of quicksilver and sulfur...

<summary>This page explains that while imperfect metals need purifying, perfect metals (Gold and Silver) must be "refined" into a "fixed spiritual" state to create the Elixir. It also defends the theory that the Philosopher's Stone is ultimately composed of only two principles: Sulfur and Mercury.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded with the statement that the Philosopher's Stone is composed of mercury and sulfur, ending with the unfinished word "call..." (likely "called"). # OF THE MASTERY

[...ed] <note>completing "called" from the previous page</note> are placed, whether pure or impure by accident and not by their primary innate nature; therefore, it is possible for these impurities to be removed through appropriate preparation. For the stripping away of accidental qualities is not impossible. Preparation, therefore, consists of removing the superfluous and supplying what is lacking in **imperfect bodies** <note>metals like lead, tin, copper, or iron which were thought to be "sick" or "incomplete" versions of gold</note>. This cannot be accomplished without the aid of perfection or skillful operation, and of purifying substances. 

For preparation varies according to the diversity of the things in need. **Experience** has given us these modes of action: namely, **calcination** <note>term: "calcinationem"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder</note>, **sublimation** <note>term: "sublimationem"; heating a substance until it turns to vapor, then collecting the solidified deposit</note>, **descension** <note>term: "descensionem"; a method of melting a substance so it flows downward through a filter or into a lower vessel</note>, **solution** <note>dissolving a solid into a liquid</note>, **distillation**, **coagulation** <note>turning a liquid into a solid</note>, **fixation** <note>term: "fixationem"; making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand fire without evaporating</note>, and **creation** <note>original: "creationem"; likely a variant of "ceration," the process of softening a substance until it has a wax-like consistency</note>. We have provided a sufficient account of each of these in the *Sum of the Perfection of the Mastery* <note>original: "summa perfectionis magisterii"; a reference to one of the most famous alchemical manuals of the Middle Ages, traditionally attributed to the author Geber</note>. These are the works that assist in preparation. 

The materials that assist in these preparations are all kinds of **salts**, **alums**, **vitriols** <note>original: "atramentorum"; literally "blackings," referring to metallic sulfates used in industrial and alchemical processes</note>, and also **glass**, **borax**, and other things of this nature, along with **very sharp vinegar** and **fire**. And because we propose to prepare—that is, to cleanse—imperfect bodies with these substances, if any impurity exists within these reagents according to our experience (of which we are certain), then the aforesaid reagents must first be cleansed themselves.

### *The Cleansing of Common Salt.*

Common salt is cleansed in the following manner.

<meta>The previous page concluded with a list of alchemical operations (calcination, sublimation, etc.) and began describing the specific preparation of common salt, ending with "We have prov..." -># OF THE INVESTIGATION<-

[...to be clean]sed. <note>completing "mundandum" from the previous page</note> First let it be burned, and once burned let it be dissolved in hot common water: once dissolved let it be passed through a filter <note>distilled here refers to the process of filtration or dripping</note>, and let the filtered liquid be congealed over a slow fire in a glazed dish: once congealed, let it be calcined <note>term: "calcinetur"; reduced to a powder through heat</note> for a day and a night in a moderate fire; and keep it, for thus it is sufficiently clean.

### Purification of Alkali salt.

Alkali salt <note>term: "Sal Alkali"; likely soda or potash, used as a flux</note> is cleansed in the same way as was stated for common salt; it is the "blood of glass" <note>original: "sanguis vitri"; a traditional name for this salt because of its essential role in glassmaking</note>. First let it be ground; then let the ground powder be dissolved in hot common water; afterwards let it be passed through a filter; let it be coagulated <note>turned back into a solid</note> and calcined in a slow fire.

### Purification of Rock salt.

In order that it may be cleansed, let it be treated in all ways just as was done with common salt <note>original: "salis Gemmæ"; literally "gem salt," referring to clear, crystalline halite</note>.

### Purification of Sal Ammoniac.

First let it be ground with an equal proportion of cleansed common salt: Afterwards let it be sublimated <note>term: "sublimetur"; heated until it rises as vapor and collects as a solid in the upper part of the vessel</note> in a tall aludel <note>term: "aludel"; a stack of pear-shaped pots used to collect vapors</note> until the pure substance has been totally extracted. Afterwards let it be dissolved upon a porphyry stone <note>original: "porritem"; likely a misspelling of 'porphyritem', a hard stone slab</note> in the open air, if you wish to make an oil <note>original: "aquam"; literally 'water', but refers to a liquefied salt</note> from it, or else keep it in its sublimated and pure state.

### Purification of other salts.

For various other types of salts are still to be found; these are prepared and cleansed by the method already described.

<margin>K 3</margin>

->*The Cleansing of Glacial Alum.*<-

Many things can be prepared from it without its cleansing. Some, however, cleanse it in this manner: Let it be placed in an alembic <note>term: "alembic"; a distilling apparatus consisting of a boiling vessel and a cap with a tube to collect vapors</note> and all the moisture extracted from it, which is of great value in this art. The dregs <note>original: "feces"</note> remaining in the bottom of the vessel should either be dissolved upon a stone, or in the water extracted from it, or they should be reserved.

->*The Cleansing of Jamen Alum.*<-

Jamen Alum <note>original: "alumen Jameni"; likely a reference to alum sourced from Yemen, highly valued for its purity in medieval chemistry</note> is prepared in the same way as glacial alum: except that in this art it is of greater power.

->*The Cleansing of other Alums.*<-

Several other alums are still found, which are prepared and cleansed as has already been stated.

->*The Cleansing of Inks, and first concerning the Black.*<-

Black ink <note>term: "atramentum nigrum"; in this context, it refers to black vitriol or ferrous sulfate, used both as a pigment and a chemical reagent</note> is cleansed thus. First, let it be dissolved with cleansed vinegar: Afterward, let it be distilled through a filter <note>in early chemistry, "distilling through a filter" often meant allowing the liquid to wick through a cloth or pass through a porous material to clarify it</note>, and let it be congealed: And let it be dissolved again with that same liquid which was distilled from it: And then let the dregs from this distillation be distilled through an alembic: Let them be calcined <note>reduced to a dry powder through high heat</note> and afterward ground, and again dissolved in their own water and congealed. Or let the water itself be reserved according to the will of the craftsman.

# OF INVESTIGATION

### The Cleansing of Copperas.

Copperas <note>original: "cuperosa"; a historical name for metallic sulfates, specifically green vitriol or ferrous sulfate</note>, otherwise known as vitriol, is cleansed in the same way as black ink. However, black ink possesses a greater earthiness <note>original: "terrestritatem"; referring to the solid, non-volatile mineral impurities</note> than green ink.

### *The Cleansing of other Inks.*

Various other inks <note>term: "atramenta" (inks); in alchemical texts, this refers to a class of vitriols or mineral salts used for dyes and chemical reactions</note> are still found; these are cleansed just like those mentioned previously.

### *Concerning Glass and Boraxes.*

Glass and Boraxes <note>term: "boracia"; a broad term for various fluxing salts used to help metals melt or to purify them</note>, if they have been treated in the proper manner, do not require preparation.

### *The Cleansing of Very Sharp Vinegar.*

Vinegars <note>original: "aceta"</note> of whatever kind they may be, even if they are bitter, are refined <note>original: "subtiliantur"; literally "made subtle" or broken down into finer parts</note> and purified: and their power or effect is improved through distillation. We have sufficiently treated the cleansing and purification of these substances: with which imperfect bodies <note>term: "corpora imperfecta"; in alchemy, these are the base metals such as lead, tin, copper, or iron, which were believed to be "sick" or "immature" versions of gold and silver</note> can be prepared, improved, and refined, always with the mediation of the proper fire. Indeed, the imperfect bodies are prepared and purified with the help of these vinegars and fire in this manner. For these imperfect bodies possess superfluous moistures and a combustible sulfurousness <note>original: "sulphureitatem adustibilem"; the alchemical principle of sulfur was thought to cause metals to tarnish or "burn" in the fire</note> which generates blackness within them and corrupts them. They also have an unclean, foul-smelling, and combustible earthiness that is excessively coarse, hindering the entry <meta>catchword: impe- (likely 'impedientem' or 'hindering')</meta>

### The Special Preparation of Jupiter [Tin]

Jupiter is prepared in many ways, but most effectively through this method: Place it in a **calcination furnace** <note>a furnace designed for roasting materials at high heat to reduce them to powder</note> in a vessel suited for this purpose, and build a fire underneath until the body itself melts. While the body is liquefied, stir it with a **perforated iron spatula** <note>original: "fpatula ferrea perforata"</note> and extract the **dross** <note>original: "fcoriam"; the impurities or oxidation that rise to the surface of molten metal</note> that forms upon it. Continue stirring in a steady heat until a significant quantity of powder has gathered on the surface. Remove that powder, and then stir again until, through frequent motion and the removal of the dross, another good quantity has gathered as before. Set this aside as well, and continue this process turn after turn, until the entire metallic body is reduced to a **very fine powder**.

Next, let that powder be sifted and returned to the furnace. Subject it once more to a strong fire—though not so hot as to melt the powder—and stir it frequently. Let it remain in the fire of its **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve oxidation or dehydration</term> for a **natural day** <note>a period of 24 hours</note>, or until nearly all the **accidental moisture** and excess has been destroyed, along with the **combustible sulfur** <note>original: "fulphure combuftibili"; alchemists believed metals contained a "dirty" sulfur that caused them to tarnish or burn, which had to be removed to reach the pure metallic core</note> corrupt-
<meta>catchword: -ing</meta>

<summary>This page provides a detailed technical guide for the calcination of tin (Jupiter), describing how to reduce the solid metal into a fine, purified powder through constant stirring and skimming of dross. This process aims to strip away "combustible sulfur" and "accidental moisture" to isolate the metal's pure essence.</summary>

### *Of the preparation of Saturn.*

The preparation of **Saturn** <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for the metal Lead</term> is as follows: Let it be placed likewise in the calcining furnace, moving it just as you did with the Tin, until it is converted into a very fine powder: let it be sifted and placed back into the furnace. Let it stay there in the fire of its calcination in the manner aforesaid, until all the fugitive <note>original: "fugitiva"; volatile elements that would otherwise evaporate</note> and inflammable substance has been destroyed. 

Afterward, let the **red calx** <note>original: "calx rubea"; the reddish powder or oxide remaining after lead is roasted at high heat</note> and clean material be extracted: which should be soaked and ground very frequently and very finely with cleansed common salt, and with **vitriol** <note>original: "atramento"; literally "blacking" or ink, usually referring to green vitriol or iron sulfate in this context</note> and with purified and very sharp vinegar. Use these for the **red work** <note>original: "ad rubeum"; referring to the Rubedo, the final stage of the Great Work signifying completion and perfection</note>, just as you did for the white work with common salt...

...[cleansed with salt] and **Alum of Yemen** <note>original: "alumine Jameni"; a high-quality mineral salt used as a mordant and astringent</note>: and by soaking many times with vinegar, just as was done with **Jupiter** <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for Tin</term>. ### ->Of the preparation of Venus.<-

**Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term> is best prepared in this manner. Let a **layer upon layer** <note>original: "stratum super stratum"; a technique of alternating layers of material in a crucible</note> of very well cleansed common salt be placed in a **crucible**, and upon it a plate of the metal [Venus], and above that a layer of salt, and above that a plate: and so alternately, until the vessel is filled. Let it be covered and **sealed with lute** <note>original: "lutetur"; to seal a vessel airtight using "lute," a paste of clay, dung, or sand</note>, and placed in the furnace of calcination for a natural day <note>a "natural day" usually refers to a full 24-hour period</note>. Then let it be taken down and let whatever has been calcined be scraped away: and let the plates be replaced with new salt: and so let them be calcined in turn, until all the plates have been consumed or corroded by the benefit of the salt

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>OF MASTERY I.</header>
<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

...ansed salt and fire, because salt corrodes the superfluous humidity and combustible sulfurousness: and because fire raises the fugitive and inflammable substance with due proportion. Then let this powder be ground very finely, and washed with vinegar, until it is free from blackness. Then, let the water emanating from it be treated another time with new cleansed salt; and let it be soaked with vinegar and ground. Having repeated and cleansed this, and after the grinding, let it be placed in the **furnace of calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve decomposition or oxidation</term> in a suitable vessel, and let it stand there for two **natural days** <note>original: "dies naturales"; a period of 24 hours including both day and night</note>. Then let it be extracted and ground, and made into a paste well and finely: and let it be washed well with water for so long until it has been cleansed and purged of all impurity. Then let it be dried well in the sun: and then let half its weight of **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "sale armoniaco"; ammonium chloride, used as a flux and cleansing agent</note> be added, and let them be ground very well, until the powder becomes an **impalpable substance** <note>original: "substantia impalpabilis"; a powder so fine that its grains cannot be felt between the fingers</note>. Then, by **sublimating** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then solidifies again, used for purification</term> over the fire and repeating the sublimations, until the sal ammoniac remains with the **lime** <note>original: "calce"; the powdery residue left after calcination</note> at the bottom of the **cucurbit** <note>original: "cucurbitæ"; the lower, gourd-shaped part of a distillation apparatus</note>: And afterward let it be placed **under the open sky** <note>original: "sub divo"; exposure to night air and dew to encourage liquefaction</note>, or in a furnace of solution, or in **dung for resolution** <note>original: "resolutionis fimo"; the use of fermenting manure to provide a gentle, constant heat for dissolving substances</note>; until whatever is soluble has been dissolved, renewing the sal ammoniac if it should be necessary, until the whole becomes water. Honor this water, which we name the **water of fixed sulfur**: with which the **elixir** <note>original: "elixir"; the transformative substance of the Great Work</note> is **tinged** <term>tinge: in alchemy, to fundamentally change the color and nature of a metal through transmutation</term> even unto infinity. And let these things suffice concerning the preparation of **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper</term>.

### *On the preparation of Mars.*

**Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term> is also better prepared by this method. Let it be **calcined** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder</term>, just as **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term> was, with cleansed common salt: Once calcined, let it be washed with pure vinegar: Once washed, let it be dried in the sun: and once dried, let it be ground and soaked again with new salt and vinegar, and let it be placed back in the same furnace for two days, as was done with Venus. The dissolved substance is highly esteemed <note>original: "Solutum honoratur"</note> because it is the **water of fixed sulfur** <note>a solution thought to contain the non-volatile essence of sulfur, used to stabilize colors</note>, wonderfully increasing the color of the **elixir** <term>elixir: the transformative substance sought by alchemists to achieve the Great Work</term>. And let these things said regarding the preparation of the **imperfect bodies** <note>the base metals, such as iron, lead, and copper, which alchemists sought to "perfect" into gold</note> suffice.

### *On the refinement of the perfect bodies, and first concerning Sol in a specific manner.*

Truly, the **perfect bodies** <note>the noble metals, gold (Sol) and silver (Luna)</note> do not need preparation for the sake of perfection, since they are already perfect: But they are prepared so that they may be further **refined** <note>original: "subtilientur"; literally to be made subtle or thin</note> and attenuated. Therefore, such a preparation should be applied to them. Let **Sol** <term>Sol: the alchemical name for gold</term>, beaten into thin plates, be taken and placed skillfully with very well-prepared and cleansed common salt in a calcination vessel, and let it be placed in the furnace to be well calcined for three days, until the whole has been finely calcined. Then let it be extracted and ground very well, and washed with vinegar, and dried in the sun. Afterward, let it be ground well with half its weight of cleansed **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "sale armoniaco"; ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used as a flux</note>, and let it be placed

### *On the refinement of Luna in a particular way.*

**Luna** <term>Luna: the alchemical name for silver</term> is likewise refined, thinned, and reduced into a spiritual body by the method already described. Therefore, perform each and every step in its refinement just as you did with **Sol** <term>Sol: the alchemical name for gold</term>. And the water of dissolved Luna is the ferment made for the **white spiritual elixir** <note>the "White Work," the stage of transmutation that results in silver</note>.

We have now sufficiently explained the preparation of the **imperfect bodies** <note>the base metals like lead, tin, or copper</note> and the **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance to purify it, often by turning it into vapor and then back to solid</term> of the perfect ones; so that from these instructions, any discerning person may be able to achieve their goal. Therefore, let everyone observe the properties and methods of operation or composition of the **greater elixir**. For we seek to create a single substance: yet one gathered from many things, united and **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of making a substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate or change</term>, which fire cannot drive away when it is placed upon the flames. Let it be mixed with melted materials and liquefy with them, by means of the **ingressive substance** <note>the "penetrating" quality that allows the elixir to enter the "pores" of a metal</note> within it; and let it be blended by its mixable substance; and let it be made firm by its consolidating substance; and let it be fixed by its fixative substance; and let it not be burned—

...is [com]busted <note>The text continues from the previous page's catchword "combura-", meaning to be burned or consumed by fire.</note> by those things which do not burn gold and silver; and it should bring about consolidation and weight with a due and perfect ignition. However, do not understand "a short time" in such a way that the work can be constructed in a few days or hours the first time. But rather, compared to other modern truth-tellers, and even compared to the operations of nature, the truth is reached more quickly in this manner. Hence the philosophers say: **Medicine** is that which has anticipated a long span of time. <note>original: "Medicina eſt, cujus longum tempus ſpacium anticipavit." This implies that the alchemical Elixir accomplishes in a short time what nature requires centuries to perform.</note>

Wherefore I tell you to endure patiently, lest by chance you shorten the delay; for a certain haste is from the devil. <note>A common alchemical warning: attempting to rush the "cooking" of the Stone usually results in the vessel shattering or the contents being scorched.</note> Therefore, he who does not have patience should stay his hand from the work: for a belief in haste will only hinder him. Every natural action has its own movement and a determined time, in which it is finished in a greater or lesser space.

For this, three things are necessary: **patience**, **delay**, and the **adaptation of instruments**. Concerning these and their mode of action, we have spoken sufficiently to the artisan in various chapters in the *Summa* <note>original: "ſumma noſtri magiſterii"; likely referring to the *Summa Perfectionis* (The Height of Perfection), the most famous work attributed to Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan).</note> of our perfect mastery. In those chapters, the artisan can experiment, provided he has sufficiently investigated our sayings; in those, we conclude with manifest and open proof: That our **Stone** is nothing other than a **stinking spirit** <term>stinking spirit: *spiritum foetentem*, a common alchemical term for sulfur, known for its pungent smell when heated</term> and **living water** <term>living water: *aquam vivam*, often referring to mercury (quicksilver) or a potent mineral acid</term>, (which we have also named **dry water** <note>original: "ſiccam aquam"; a classic alchemical paradox for mercury, which is a liquid that does not wet the hands.</note>) and through natural **decoction** <term>decoction: a process of heating or "digesting" a substance in a closed vessel for a long period to mature it</term>, and through true proportion...

...[compo]sition <note>The text begins with a fragment of a word, likely "composition" or "perfection," referring to the alchemical substance described on the previous page.</note> cleansed and united in such a union that nothing can be missing from it, nor can anything be added to it. To these should be added a third thing to shorten the work: that is, a **perfect, attenuated body**. <note>original: "corpus perfectum attenuatum." In alchemy, a "perfect body" usually refers to gold or silver, and to "attenuate" it means to refine it or reduce it to a more subtle, reactive state.</note> From the foregoing, therefore, the things are evident in which the truth is near, and which bring the body itself to perfection.

Indeed, through our investigation—of which we are certain, and through clear experience—we plainly consider all the truths which have already been written by us for you alone, according to what we have seen by the reasoning of our mind in these volumes. Indeed, those things which we have extracted by experience with our fingers, seen with our eyes, and touched with our hands, we have written in our **Summa of the Perfection of the Mastery**. <note>original: "summa nostra perfectionis magisterii." This is the title of the most famous medieval alchemical text, attributed to Geber. It emphasized that alchemy was a physical, experimental science.</note>

Therefore, let the wise <term>artisan</term> <note>original: "artifex." The alchemist was not merely a theorist but a "maker" or "craftsman" who worked alongside nature.</note> study in our volumes, gathering our true intention which we have scattered and set forth in different places, so that it might not be made public to the wicked or the ignorant. Once gathered, let him test it by studying and experimenting with the persistence of ingenious labor until he reaches total knowledge. Let the artisan therefore exert himself, and he will find it. 

We are moved with deep affection <note>original: "commotis amore visceribus," literally "with bowels moved by love," a common Latin idiom for profound compassion or a sincere desire to help the reader.</note> to provide the method of investigation from which we, through our own consideration, have extracted the certain knowledge of the truth. For we consider that this <term>science</term> <note>original: "scientia." Alchemists viewed their work as a rigorous branch of natural philosophy based on observation.</note> deals with perfect and imperfect bodies. It also gives full knowledge of perfecting them, and of the corruption of matter... <meta>catchword: [ma]teriæ</meta>

<summary>The author emphasizes that his alchemical secrets are scattered throughout his writings to hide them from the unworthy, requiring the student to use both intellect and physical "hands-on" experience to find the truth. He identifies the "science" of alchemy as the study of the perfection and corruption of physical bodies (metals).</summary>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header># ON THE INVESTIGATION OF THE MASTERY</header>

...ficer <note>This completes the word "artificer" from the previous page.</note> of **matter and form**. <note>original: "materiae & formae." Following Aristotelian philosophy, alchemists believed "matter" was the raw substance and "form" was the set of characteristics (like color or weight) that made it a specific metal.</note> We consider, through our investigation, that the matter of the **perfect bodies** <note>The "perfect bodies" are gold and silver, which were considered "perfect" because they do not corrode in fire or air.</note> along with the form and root of their mixture, remains pure and without any occurring corruption until its completion.

We have also considered, by looking at the opposite, that the substance of the **imperfect bodies** <note>Base metals like lead, tin, copper, and iron.</note> and the perfect bodies is one and the same everywhere; namely, **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note> and **sulfur**. <note>original: "argentum vivum & sulphur." This is the core "Mercury-Sulfur theory": the belief that all metals are composed of these two principles. Differences between metals were thought to be caused by different levels of purity and proportions of these two ingredients.</note> We found that before their mixture, the corruption of the imperfect bodies occurred accidentally, which gave a new and corrupt form to the matter. 

For when we saw the imperfect bodies prepared by our skill and cleansed of all superfluous corruption—whether a **fugitive impurity** <note>Substances that evaporate or vanish when heated.</note> or a deliberate **earthy** one—we found them to have greater clarity, brilliance, and purity than the naturally perfect bodies themselves. Through this consideration, we reached the perfect and complete end of this science, which we have written about perfectly and fully in our volumes. Therefore, study those volumes, and you will find our whole science, which we have summarized from the books of the ancients.

---

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page summarizes the Geberian theory that all metals consist of mercury and sulfur. It claims that by removing "accidental" impurities from base metals, the alchemist can produce a substance even purer than naturally occurring gold or silver.</summary>
<keywords>matter and form, perfect bodies, imperfect bodies, quicksilver, mercury-sulfur theory, purification, Geber, ancients</keywords>

# DEDICATION.

Whenever I return to those <unclear>past</unclear> times, the memory of your virtues and your <unclear>kindnesses</unclear> toward me is most delightful. From there, having been joined <unclear>once again</unclear> at Leiden <note>original: "Leidæ," the city where Hornius was a professor.</note>, until you sought to travel through France <note>original: "Galliam"</note> and <unclear>Italy</unclear> with the intent of surveying them, you did <unclear>not</unclear> cease to ease the painful burden of <unclear>my</unclear> absence with frequent letters. <unclear>Afterward</unclear>, having been called to the most distinguished public duties—which were owed both to your family name <unclear>and</unclear> to your own capable mind—you steadily <unclear>continued</unclear> your former goodwill.

Considering <unclear>how</unclear> pleasing all this is to me and how honorable it is to you, I wished to bear witness to it for both present and <unclear>future</unclear> generations with this <unclear>small</unclear> gift: humbly asking <unclear>Almighty</unclear> God that He may long preserve <unclear>you</unclear> for the Country, your family, and even for <unclear>us</unclear>, His own. # PREFACE.

Alchemy <note>original: "Chhymia," an archaic spelling of alchemy, then considered the highest "art" of nature.</note>, the most famous of the arts, recognizes no older propagator—after its first parent or restorer, Hermes the Egyptian <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure from late antiquity to whom many foundational alchemical and philosophical works were attributed.</note>—than Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 813), an influential polymath whose Latinized name "Geber" became synonymous with alchemical authority in Europe.</note>, who was of the Moorish people. For he, on account of the keenness and weight of judgment with which he was endowed, and his absolute experience in metallic matters <term>metallurgy: the science and study of the properties and purification of metals</term>, was the first of all, so far as is known, to have embraced this art with clearly admirable skill. He collected and discovered it from the many errors of others—albeit with difficulty and labor, through long conjecture and tedious experience, and with the investment of great expense (as he himself complains)—organizing it through writing and a method suited to nature. From this man, as if from a fountain, all philosophers of later ages have drawn and diverted most abundant waters to irrigate their own small gardens; and having plucked little flowers from this Arabic garden, they have adorned the monuments of their own genius.

<summary>This preface introduces the history of alchemy, identifying Hermes Trismegistus as its founder and the Arabic scholar Geber as its primary systematic propagator. The author emphasizes that Geber's rigorous, though costly, experimental approach laid the foundation for all subsequent European alchemical study.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus, Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), History of Science, Metallurgy, Arabic scholarship, natural philosophy</keywords>

-># PREFACE.<-

[have expanded] and enriched them. Let there be witnesses among many: Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and philosophy were foundational to both Islamic and European science.</note>, Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist.</note>, Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316); though a philosopher and missionary, many influential alchemical texts were attributed to him in later centuries.</note>, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and "Universal Doctor" of the Church who wrote extensively on the natural sciences.</note>, various *Rosaria* <note>original: "Rosaria diverſa," referring to "The Rosary of the Philosophers," a famous series of alchemical texts.</note>, Count Bernard <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who allegedly spent 60 years seeking the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, and whoever else is of more upright value, in all of whose writings the clear footprints of Geber are seen to be impressed not lightly.

And indeed, this is not without merit. Since no one can teach any art unless he knows it exactly; this man truly wrote those things which he had extracted with his own fingers, seen with his eyes, and touched with his hands. Furthermore, he declared himself worthy of a curse unless he handed down the total truth. For this reason, there is no cause why (in the words and judgment of Johannes Bracesco in *Demogorgon* <note>Johannes Bracesco was a 16th-century author whose work "The Dialogue of Demogorgon" defended Geber's alchemy as the only true method.</note>) he should not be preferred by all students of true philosophy over all others who have approached this most subtle part of philosophy, whether among the ancients or the moderns. All study and time should be spent in unfolding and knowing his writings.

And although Dr. Mejus <note>Likely Michael Maier (1568–1622), a famous alchemist and physician known for his complex allegories, whose name was often Latinized as "Majus" or "Mejus."</note> may seem the most obscure of all despite his "open clarity"—a quality which deters almost everyone from reading him—nevertheless, he himself [suggests] <meta>The catchword "sug" on the original page indicates the next page begins with "suggerit" (suggests).</meta>

-># PREFACE.<-

...[others, he] suggests and shows the way, because whatever difficulty is presented can be overcome by an attentive and not entirely foolish reader, when he says: "Therefore, let the wise craftsman <term>artifex: a skilled practitioner or alchemist</term> study in our volumes, by gathering our true, scattered intention <note>original: "intentionem," referring to the hidden meaning or the core objective of the alchemical work</note>, which we have set forth in various places, so that it might not be published to the malicious or the ignorant; and let him test what has been collected, until he arrives at total knowledge <note>original: "cognitionem"</note> through study and experimentation with the persistence of ingenious labor."

And so, when by chance I had taken up the impulse to unroll his writings—celebrated and praised by the voices and pens of everyone—it seemed difficult at first, though not entirely impossible, to give light from this smoke, especially by lighting a torch from the hearth of other philosophers, and to drag into the light the truth hidden here and there from the thickets of obscurities. Whether or not I have achieved this, these aphorisms <term>aphorisms: concise statements of scientific principles or truths</term> will testify, into which, as if into certain small cells and treasure-chambers <note>original: "favissas," referring to storage pits for sacred objects, suggesting the depth and value of the content</note>, I have organized whatever better juice could be found in this stony Arabia <note>original: "Arabia petrea." This is a scholarly pun: Arabia Petraea was a Roman province, but the author uses it here to describe the "stony" or difficult and dense nature of the writings of Geber, the "Arab" philosopher.</note>. Indeed, I have taken care that everything of Geber's—or rather his interpreter's...

# GEBERIAN
# APHORISMS.

### I.

The subject, or the Material treated within the books of Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in both Islamic and European alchemy.</note>, is *Alchemy* <note>original: "Al hymia"</note>, or the science of transmuting metals. This is an excellent, noble, and glorious branch of Natural Philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe that was the precursor to modern science.</note>; indeed, it is most precious and most hidden.

### II.

For this science ingeniously follows Nature—insofar as it is possible through her principles—in all her works, and in all things governed by natural actions below the lunar circle <note>The "sublunary sphere" refers to the Earthly realm, which in ancient physics was the region of change, decay, and the four elements, as opposed to the unchanging heavens.</note>.

### III.

Therefore, the student <note>literally "lover" (amatorem)</note> of this science must know and understand all the principles of Nature and the causes of minerals, as well as the way they are generated, and the sicknesses and imperfections found in their ores. Otherwise, he will be far removed from this art, as he will have no true root upon which to ground his intentions.

### IV.

For if bodies and their principles were not understood in the depths of their nature

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental principles of Geber’s alchemy, defining it as a noble branch of natural philosophy dedicated to metal transmutation. It asserts that a practitioner must possess a deep understanding of natural laws and the generation of minerals to succeed.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Alchemy, Natural Philosophy, transmutation, minerals, sublunary sphere, principles of nature, Jabir ibn Hayyan</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Geberian Aphorisms" from page 143. The text concludes the fourth aphorism regarding the necessity of understanding the internal nature of metals and moves into the specific principles of their generation through the interaction of mercury and sulfur.</meta>

->APHORISMS<-

<meta>catchword from previous page: & ma-</meta>

...and clearly; for if we did not know what was superfluous or what was lacking in them, it would be impossible for us ever to achieve the perfection of their transmutation <term>transmutation: the alchemical process of changing one substance, typically a base metal like lead, into another, usually gold</term>.

### V.

Now, the principles of this science are the same as those of Nature regarding the generation of metals. For since the goal of the art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy, the human skill used to replicate or perfect natural processes.</note> is to produce the same gold as nature, whatever and of what quality the material was in nature, it must also be the same in the art. But if the material of the art is not the same but merely similar, then not the same natural gold, but only something similar to it, will result.

> *Natural things are produced naturally,*
> *Things outside of nature are cast aside as useless,* <note>original: "neutralia projiciuntur"</note>
> *Let art not be attempted, unless it accompanies nature,*
> *What nature denies, no one successfully dares.*

### VI.

In creating and forming metals, Nature possesses a certain substance or earthy matter fused from a mixture of quicksilver <term>quicksilver: also known as mercury or "argentum vivum"</term> and sulfur. From this mixture, a certain very thin and pure twofold vapor <note>original: "fumus." In alchemical theory, these two vapors—one moist (mercurial) and one dry (sulfurous)—are the parents of all metals.</note> issues forth (white and red), which is afterwards coagulated by tempered heat. Once mixed with a viscous mineral water, it is thickened and hardened into a metal by long-lasting decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating a substance to ripen it or extract its essence</term>. In no different a manner than these metals, the Philosophical Elixir <note>The "Elyxir philosophicum" or Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> is composed from Mercurial water and a sulfurous, fatty, or viscous earth; this is the foundation of the entire Hermetic work <note>The "opificium Hermeticum," referring to the "Great Work" of alchemy attributed to the mythical founder Hermes Trismegistus.</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental alchemical theory that "Art" must mimic "Nature" by using the same raw materials—mercury and sulfur—to create gold. It explains that the Philosophical Elixir is formed through the same process of heating and refinement that Nature uses to create metals in the earth.</summary>

<keywords>transmutation, gold, mercury, sulfur, Philosophical Elixir, Hermetic work, Geber, decoction, natural principles</keywords>

### VII.

And in this operation, Nature (as Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy. This passage alludes to the "Emerald Tablet," a foundational alchemical text.</note> intended) makes that which is of the earth ascend into heaven—that is, it makes a corporeal thing spiritual—and descend from heaven to the earth—that is, it makes a spiritual thing corporeal, as an uncertain author writes regarding alchemy in chapter 3. <note>original: "facit de terra ascendere in coelum... & descendere de coelo in terram"</note>

### VIII.

Just as the principles are the same, so too the resulting products are the same in both cases. For nothing else is required for this work than the metallic body. For as Count Bernard <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist who emphasized that the "Stone" must be made from metallic materials alone.</note> rightly advises, we also take this very body just as nature created it.

### IX.

It is indeed certain that all metals, as far as their root is concerned, are of the same substance or matter. Because of this, they have a great affinity for one another; they change and are changed by one another, and they perfect and are perfected by one another. However, they are not of the same form. This [difference in form] is due to their weakness or health, their purity or impurity, and the scarcity or quantity of the quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury; considered the "mother" or "womb" of all metals in alchemical theory</term> and sulfur in their natural union and mixture, as well as the long or short decoction <term>decoction: a process of "ripening" or "cooking" by the earth’s internal heat that alchemists believed transformed base metals into gold over centuries</term> by nature itself.

### X.

Hence, those metals which are purer, more thoroughly "cooked," and more fertile in quicksilver are called "perfect," such as gold and silver:

<meta>catchword: Quicksilver-</meta>

...but the others, which are of a contrary nature, are imperfect; they are partly soft, like Saturn <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for Lead.</term> and Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for Tin.</term>, and partly hard, like Mars <term>Mars: the alchemical name for Iron.</term> and Venus <term>Venus: the alchemical name for Copper.</term>: *To which is added mineral quicksilver.* <note>original: "argentum vivum minerale." Mercury was often treated as a metal that was "not yet a metal" due to its liquid state.</note>

### XI.
The proper subject of metallic transmutation, therefore, is partly an imperfect, alterable body, and partly a coagulable quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum coagulabile." That is, mercury that has been made solid or thickened through alchemical processes.</note>.

### XII.
And indeed, imperfect bodies are, in the depths of their nature, unclean and foul; nothing shining is found in them, since according to nature it is not in them. For that which is not in a thing cannot be found within it.

### XIII.
Since, therefore, nothing of perfection is found in them, there is necessarily nothing in abundance; rather, something diminished is detected in them, which must necessarily be completed.

### XIV.
Indeed, that which is diminished in them is a scarcity of quicksilver and its improper thickening <term>thickening: "inspissatio," the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid or dense state.</term>.

### XV.
The completion in them, therefore, will be the multiplication and good thickening of the quicksilver, along with a permanent fixation <term>fixation: "fixio," the process of making a volatile substance (like mercury) stable under heat so it does not evaporate.</term>. The whole art, therefore, consists in the fixation of quicksilver, whether it is considered within bodies or outside of them.

### XVI.

Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivum."</note> is truly "fugitive" by its very nature, meaning it evaporates easily without even catching fire. It therefore requires a certain medicine <term>medicine: in alchemy, this refers to the "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone," viewed as a substance that "heals" base metals into gold.</term> which, suddenly and before the quicksilver's flight, adheres to it in its depths. This medicine must join with the metal's smallest particles, thickening it and preserving it through "fixation" <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable in fire so that it does not evaporate.</term> in the heat of the fire. This continues until the metal can tolerate a greater fire that consumes its excess moisture, and by this benefit, transforms it in an instant into true gold or silver <note>original: "solificum & lunificum," literally "sun-making and moon-making."</note>, depending on what the medicine was prepared to achieve.

### XVII.

The ultimate goal of this art, therefore, is to repair the defects of nature. By removing weakness and impurity, it transforms each of the imperfect bodies—and even the "coagulable" quicksilver itself—into a perfect Lunar or Solar body <note>Lunar and Solar bodies are the alchemical terms for Silver and Gold.</note> through a firm and true mutation. For where nature falls short, art assists and brings aid. Nature always intends perfection, but when external support is lacking, it stops and proceeds no further; thus, it produces the imperfect. Art observes this weakness and removes it through its own ingenuity. *As written in the book attributed to Solomon.* <note>Solomon: The biblical King Solomon was often cited as an ancient authority in alchemical and hermetic traditions.</note>

### XVIII.

Truly, since this goal cannot be reached without an intermediary, there is a need for a certain medicine to supply what is lacking, and to veil and temper that darkness and foulness...

...preceding, bringing forth brightness, and completing it perfectly.

### XIX.

Although the craftsman <term>artifex: the alchemist or practitioner of the "Great Work."</term> must never fail to be like an ape <note>original: "velut simia." The "Ape of Nature" was a common medieval metaphor for the artist or scientist who strives to mimic natural processes as closely as possible.</note> and a perpetual imitator of Nature, the great mother of all things, in his work; nevertheless, he cannot imitate her in every distinction of properties, and especially not in her first principles. Nor is that the intention of the philosophers, since certain things are impossible for them and remain utterly unknown.

### XX.

Accordingly, even if the matter or "root" of minerals is quicksilver <note>original: "arg. vivum" (argentum vivum).</note> combined with sulfur, we should not take the raw materials from which mineral bodies are first made. Instead, we must take that which is derived from the natural bodies themselves. A clear example of this is found in plants. For we know that the generation of a plant is composed of water and fine earth (just as minerals are), and yet if we were to take only water and earth, we would never generate a plant. Therefore, we do not take the raw ingredients from which a plant grows, but that which comes *from* the plants—namely, its seed. This is excellently discussed by King Alphonso <note>Alfonso X of Castile, known as "The Wise," to whom the alchemical text *Clavis Sapientiae* (The Key of Wisdom) was frequently attributed.</note> in the *Key of Wisdom*, chapter 2.

### XXI.

Therefore, following the example of Nature, from quicksilver—which possesses within itself a mixture of red sulfur—a certain most subtle substance and...

->GEBERIANS<-

L 3

...the most brilliant <note>original: "fulgidissima."</note> substance is to be generated by our art, in which the spirit of the **quintessence** <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth essence," a celestial and incorruptible element distinct from earth, air, fire, and water.</term> dwells and lies hidden. That smoky and volatile substance is fixed with its own proper substance of **quicksilver** <note>original: "arg. vivi" (argentum vivum).</note>, which is fixed and firm; this mercury is its "earth" that endures the fire. By persisting in that fire, it is fixed, and afterward, through a tempered and continuous **decoction** <term>decoctio: a process of boiling or heating a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or mature it.</term>, according to the **mastery** <note>original: "magisterium," referring to the specific technical procedure of the alchemical art.</note> of this science, it is congealed into a stone that is flowing, fusible, staining, and enduring in the fire.

### XXII.

And in this way, by the imitation of Nature, the craftsman has caused a certain corporeal substance to ascend from the earth into heaven, becoming a spiritual substance. Once that substance or matter has been made spiritual, and is afterward congealed again and fixed, being converted into a stone, he then causes it to descend from heaven to earth. Thus, he has once again made the spiritual matter corporeal, as an uncertain author says in *On Alchemy*, chapter 4.

### XXIII.

And so it is clear that just as nature makes a spirit out of a body, and a body out of a spirit in the generation of minerals and metals, so we too, in the artificial generation of the **mineral stone** <note>original: "lapis mineralis," another name for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> through our wonderful art, make bodies into spirits and spirits into bodies. And this is what **Aros** <note>Aros is a name frequently cited in medieval alchemical texts, often associated with the "Dialogues of Aros and Maria," where he acts as a student of Maria the Jewess.</note> says: "Make the bodies into spirits, and you will find what you seek." The same author says this in the same place.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of "fixation" and "volatilization," where substances are transformed from solid to spiritual and back again to create the Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes that the alchemist mimics Nature's own cycle of creation by refining the "spirit" of metals into a permanent, fire-resistant form.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 149 concerning the artificial generation of the "mineral stone." This page covers the first and second "orders" of the work.</meta>

->APHORISMS<-

### XXIV.

The philosophers reached the summit of this Medicine <note>In alchemy, "Medicine" often refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, capable of "healing" base metals into gold or curing human illness.</note> not by a single leap, as it were, but through certain stages blocked by many difficulties, finally and with great effort opening the way for their ascent.

### XXV.

For **Our Author** <note>original: "Noster." This likely refers to Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), as indicated by the header "Geberians" on the previous page.</note> testifies that he gathered the truth and discovered that **One and true medicine** only through a multitude of errors, by difficult and laborious conjecture, by long invention, by tedious experience, and by the contribution of great expense.

### XXVI.

Thus, medicines of the **Three Orders** <term>tria ordina: a traditional classification of alchemical success, moving from superficial change to total transmutation.</term> were established.

### XXVII.

By the first investigation, nothing was found except a medicine that lightly alters and masks <note>original: "pallians," meaning it cloaks or disguises the metal rather than fundamentally changing it.</note>; this was varied and tenfold, according to the diversity of the **imperfect metals**. This was called the **first order** and the **lesser work** <note>original: "opus minus."</note>.

### XXVIII.

Afterward, through further careful efforts, a more advanced refinement was reached, by which a greater power and piercing quality was added to the medicine. It no longer merely masked the outer skin, but colored more deeply by penetrating further, though not yet quite completely. This is called the **second order** and the **middle work** <note>original: "opus medium."</note>.

<meta>catchword: Tandem</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the historical progression of alchemical discovery, categorizing the first two stages of the "Medicine": the superficial First Order (Lesser Work) and the more penetrative Second Order (Middle Work).</summary>
<keywords>Three Orders, Lesser Work, Middle Work, Medicine, imperfect metals, Geber, transmutation, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the numbered aphorisms regarding the classification of alchemical medicines. While the previous page (149-150) discussed the "lesser work" and the first two orders of medicinal preparation—which only mask or lightly alter metals—this page introduces the definitive "Third Order," also known as the Great Work or the Philosopher’s Stone.</meta>

->GEBERIANS<-

L 6

### X X I X.

Finally, indeed, through the persistence of long labor and the diligence of great investigation, the **one true medicine** was discovered. By this medicine, the hard body is softened and the soft body is hardened; the volatile <note>original: "fugitivum," referring to substances like mercury that vaporize or "flee" when heated.</note> is fixed, and every foul thing is enlightened with an unspeakable splendor, even that which stands above nature. This is called the medicine of the **third order** and the **Great Work** <note>original: "majus opus."</note>. It does not differ in its essence from the medicine of the second order in any way, except through the most subtle degrees of preparation in its creation and through the persistence of long labor.

### X X X.

Know, however, that the medicines of the first and second order are **Sophistic** <term>Sophisticus: in alchemy, this refers to "imitation" or "deceptive" gold that looks real but lacks the true physical properties of the metal.</term>, and they produce Sophistic gold. But the medicine of the **Third Order** is perfect and true, because this alone removes impurity from the mixture and the imperfection of metals, and restores them to the highest health and perfection. This is the **Great and Precious Philosopher's Stone**, and that supreme and **Universal Medicine** <note>original: "Catholica illa medicina." Here, "Catholic" is used in its original sense of "universal" or "all-encompassing."</note> about which alone the other philosophers have written in their books, leaving aside all other medicines and stones. **Our Author** <note>Referring back to Geber.</note>, and after him **Isaac Hollandus** <note>A 15th-century Dutch alchemist whose works were often bundled with Geber’s in early modern chemical compendiums.</note>, have explained them all together with sufficient diligence.

### X X X I.

Therefore, having understood the subject, the goal, and the means of Alchemy, it would be next to look at the source from which these means are to be drawn.

<meta>catchword: Hither (Huc)</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Third Order" of alchemy, identifying it as the true Philosopher's Stone and the "Great Work" that fundamentally perfects metals. It distinguishes this true medicine from "sophistic" imitations and credits Geber and Isaac Hollandus for providing the most diligent explanations of the process.</summary>
<keywords>Third Order, Great Work, Philosopher's Stone, Universal Medicine, Sophistic Gold, Isaac Hollandus, Geber, transmutation, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the numbered aphorisms concerning the nature of the "Third Order" of medicine, also known as the Philosopher’s Stone. While the previous page established that this medicine is the only true way to achieve the "Great Work," this section explains the physical principles of why "spirits" (volatile substances) are the necessary ingredients for such a transformation. catchword: Spiri-</meta>

# APHORISMS

### XXXII.

Indeed, you will be led to this conclusion by considering those things which are most closely joined to bodies and which adhere to them in a friendly way, altering them to their very depths. For it is from these things that the medicine is drawn. <note>The author suggests that the best medicine for a substance comes from materials that already have a natural "affinity" or attraction to it.</note>

### XXXIII.

Experience has indeed taught us that **spirits** <term>Spiritus: In early chemistry and alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances—such as mercury, sulfur, or ammonia—that can vaporize or "flee" when heated, and have the power to penetrate solid materials.</term> are more easily assimilated and united with bodies <note>In this context, "bodies" almost always refers to solid metals.</note>, because we see that these spirits are more "friendly" to bodies than anything else in nature.

### XXXIV.

For this reason, neither the ancients, nor we, nor those who will come after us, have found anything else that could be united with bodies except for spirits alone, or something that contains the nature of both body and spirit within itself. Nor have we seen in anything other than spirits such a close adherence and penetration into bodies, resulting in a total transformation.

### XXXV.

From this it is concluded that spirits are the true medicine for the alteration of bodies; or, what amounts to the same thing, the medicine must be made from spirits.

### XXXVI.

Therefore, if you wish to convert or transmute bodies, O **children of doctrine** <note>original: "filii doctrinæ," a traditional term used by alchemical authors to address their students or fellow initiates.</note>, then if it is possible for this to be achieved by any medicine, it must necessarily occur through spirits alone.

<note>Key terms from the original: Aphorism, spirits, bodies, medicine, transmutation, children of doctrine.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the true alchemical medicine must be composed of "spirits" because only these volatile substances possess the natural affinity required to penetrate and permanently change the nature of solid metals.</summary>
<keywords>spirits, volatile substances, metallic bodies, affinity, transmutation, children of doctrine, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the numbered aphorisms regarding the chemical principles of alchemy, specifically focusing on "spirits"—volatile substances that act as the bridge between solid metals. This section moves from the theoretical "affinity" of substances discussed on the previous page to the specific identification of mercury, sulfur, and arsenic as the primary agents of change. catchword: Spiritus</meta>

->GEBERIAN PRINCIPLES<-
<note>original: "GEBERIANI" — Referring to the teachings of Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), the legendary 8th-century Islamic alchemist whose works laid the foundation for European chemistry.</note>

### XXXVII.

Moreover, those minerals are called **spirits** <term>Spiritus: In early chemistry, "spirits" were substances that would vaporize or "flee" when heated, such as mercury or sulfur. They were thought to represent the active, life-giving principle of matter.</term> which have a volatile and spirit-like nature when placed in the fire. There are three principal spirits: **Quick-silver** <note>original: "Arg. vivum" (Argentum vivum), commonly known as Mercury.</note>, **sulfur**, and **arsenic**. Others are less principal, such as **sal ammoniac** <note>A salt found near volcanoes, used to clean metals.</note>, **marcasite** <note>A brittle mineral with a metallic luster, often iron pyrites or "fool's gold."</note>, **magnesia**, and **tutty** <note>original: "Tutia" — Zinc oxide collected from the flues of furnaces.</note>.

### XXXVIII.

Therefore, whatever things are altered, they must necessarily be altered either by the power of quick-silver, or sulfur, or similar spirits; for these alone communicate and unite in their very nature with the solid bodies <note>Metals.</note> themselves, because they are more "friendly" to them than any other substances.

### XXXIX.

Among these, **Mercury** holds the prize of excellence, as it is more friendly to the metals than the others, and is therefore the **medium** for joining tinctures. <term>Tincture: An alchemical substance (often the Philosopher's Stone in liquid form) capable of infusing its properties and color into a base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</term> **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and chemical texts were standard in European universities.</note> says: "Mercury possesses more of the spirit than the others."

### XL.

For there is no passage from one contrary to another except through a distributive **medium** <note>A "medium" here acts as a bridge or middle-ground between two opposite states.</note>; and so, there is no passage from the softness of quick-silver to the hardness of any metal, except through a medium that exists between the soft and the hard. This is supported by **Richard the Englishman** <note>Richardus Anglicus, a 13th-century alchemist.</note> in his *Correctorium*, chapter 15: "When you wish to join imperfect bodies with perfect ones, you should create a medium through Mercury, which dissolves and opens their natures, so that simply..."

<meta>catchword: one (original Latin: unum)</meta>

...one thing may pass into another, and the perfect <note>In alchemy, "perfect" usually refers to gold or a completed substance.</note> can project its power into the imperfect, so that it may be perfected along with it.

### X L I.

**Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"</note> therefore plays both roles <note>original: "utramque facit paginam" — literally "makes both pages," an idiom meaning it is essential on both sides of the operation.</note> in this art. For the **Medicine** <term>Medicina: Also called the Elixir or the Philosopher's Stone; the substance capable of curing "sick" (imperfect) metals and turning them into gold.</term> is created from it, and Mercury itself is brought to perfection through **coagulation** <note>The process of turning a liquid or vaporous substance into a solid.</note> by that same Medicine.

### X L I I.

That the Medicine is fashioned from it is gathered from this: 1. when searching among all other things, we have not found by our discovery any other substance that is more "friendly" <note>original: "amicari" — referring to chemical affinity or the ease with which substances bond.</note> to the natures of metallic bodies than **quick-silver** <note>original: "arg. viv." (argentum vivum).</note>. Therefore, bestowing our labor upon it, we find it to be the true medicine for things capable of change, bringing them to completion with a true and remarkably unique transformation.

### X L I I I.

2. Because, just as a large quantity of **sulfur** is the cause of corruption <note>Alchemists believed sulfur caused metals to "burn" or tarnish, leading to impurities.</note>; so a large quantity of quick-silver is the cause of perfection. For all quick-silver is a perfecting agent in the works of nature. It is, in fact, the preserver against **adustion** <term>Adustion: From the Latin "adustio," meaning burning or scorching. In alchemy, it refers to the process where heat destroys or oxidizes a substance; mercury was thought to resist this.</term>, which is the ultimate goal of perfection. It is this which overcomes the fire, and is not overcome by the fire, but rests peacefully within it, even rejoicing in it. Thus, whatever things we see are best preserved from burning, those possess more of that Mercury-like nature. And for this reason, those bodies which are richer in quick-silver, are of greater...

It argues that the more mercury a metal contains, the closer it is to perfection and the better it resists "burning."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the numbered aphorisms concerning the nature of Mercury (quicksilver) and its role as the primary "Medicine" in alchemical transformation.
