...are of perfection; those which are indeed more sterile, are of less. This is evident from the easy reception of **quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum: Literally "living silver," the common alchemical name for mercury.</term>. For we see that the bodies of perfection <note>Referring to the "noble" metals, gold and silver, which absorb mercury to form amalgams more readily than "base" metals.</note> drink it in in a friendly manner; whereas we do not see other bodies having such a conformity to it, and therefore they participate less in its nature. And if any do participate (such as **tin** <note>original: "stannum"</note>), they approach closer to perfection.

### XLIV.

Therefore, we also—hardly changing nature but rather imitating it in whatever works are possible—similarly take up quicksilver in this mastery <note>The "magisterium" or the Great Work of alchemy.</note> for the medicine of each perfection, both **Solar** <note>Gold-reaching; related to the Sun.</note> and **Lunar** <note>Silver-reaching; related to the Moon.</note>, as much for the imperfect bodies as for the **coagulable** <term>Coagulabilis: Capable of being turned from a liquid or vaporous state into a solid mass.</term> quicksilver itself.

### XLV.

For when quicksilver has been cleansed and prepared through our artifice, and reduced into a most pure and shining substance, and thus worked into a medicine, and **projected** <note>The "Projection" is the final stage of the alchemical process, where the Philosopher's Stone or "Medicine" is cast upon base metals to transmute them.</note> upon bodies diminished from perfection, it will not only hide and cover their duskiness by cloaking it with its bright splendor and convert it into radiance, but it will also perfect them by its **fixation** <term>Fixio: The process by which a volatile substance is made stable and resistant to the heat of fire.</term>, and by its purity convert and strengthen them with complete perfection.

### XLVI.

Therefore, let the sublime

of all natures be praised.

### APHORISMS

**GOD**, blessed, glorious, and most high, who created it <note>Referring to Mercury.</note>, and gave it such a substance and properties of substance, which no other thing in nature happens to possess, so that in it this perfection might be found through some artifice, which we find in it as a **near potentiality** <term>Potentia propinqua: A philosophical term meaning a state where a substance is ready to transform into its final form with only a slight intervention of "Art" or alchemy.</term>.

->XLVII.<-

**Mercury** not only provides the **Medicine** <note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" is another name for the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone that "heals" imperfect metals into gold.</note> from itself for removing the imperfection of other metals, but it also desires to be cured and perfected itself, and thus has need of medicine just as they do, suitable to its nature.

->XLVIII.<-

Indeed, such a **Medicine** is suitable for it which adheres most deeply within it, and is mixed with it through its smallest parts, before its flight from the fire, as was said above in aphorism 16.

->XLIX.<-

It is therefore necessary that this medicine be created from things suitable to it.

->L.<-

->LI.<-

But indeed, because we have seen none of the bodies <note>Referring to the "solid" metals like iron or copper.</note> solidify it in its own nature with a firm and true **coagulation** <note>original: "coagulatione"; the process of turning liquid mercury into a solid metal.</note>; but rather that it flees from them through the fire of expression, however much

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Mercury’s purification. This page concludes that thought and proceeds to examine why "spirits" (volatile substances) are also insufficient on their own, ultimately defining the necessary qualities of the alchemical "Medicine." catchword: matter:</meta>

**FOLLOWERS OF GEBER**

...however much it might be of its own affinity; and therefore we have considered that no body, in its natural state, adheres to it <note>Referring to Mercury, the subject of the preceding page.</note> in its depths. Therefore, it is necessary for that medicine to be of a more subtle substance and of a more liquid melting point than those bodies themselves possess.

### LII.

Similarly, among the **spirits** <term>Spiritus: In alchemy, these are volatile substances that vaporize when heated, such as sulfur, arsenic, or mercury itself.</term>, we have not seen a firm and true **solidification** <note>original: *coagulatio*</note> of it occur while they remain in their natural state. Instead, it is fleeting and full of corruption and blackness; this occurs partly because the spirits flee the fire, and partly because of the mixture of their earthy and combustible substance.

### LIII.

Therefore, it clearly follows from this that from whatever source its **medicine** <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which was thought to "heal" or perfect base metals.</note> is drawn, it must necessarily be of the most subtle and pure substance, adhering to Mercury by its nature and affinity in its depths, and of the easiest melting or liquefaction—as thin as water, wax, or oil—and fixed against the assault of the fire. For this medicine will solidify it and convert it into a **Solar** <note>original: *Solarem*; referring to Gold</note> or **Lunar** <note>original: *Lunarem*; referring to Silver</note> substance. The reasoning behind these aphorisms is elegantly discussed by the *Rosary of Toledo* <note>original: *Rosario Toletano*; a significant medieval alchemical treatise.</note> in these words:

> Body does not act upon body, nor spirit upon spirit, because form does not receive an impression from form, nor matter from matter.

<summary>This page defines the requirements for the alchemical "Medicine," arguing that neither raw metals nor raw volatile spirits can transform Mercury; instead, a highly refined, fire-resistant substance is required. It concludes with a philosophical principle from the Rosary of Toledo regarding the interaction of matter and form.</summary>

**matter:** For like does not act upon its like, since neither of them is more worthy than the other. Therefore, none of them acts upon the other, because an equal has no authority over an equal <note>original: *par in parem non habet imperium*; a legal maxim used here to explain that substances of identical nature cannot transform one another.</note>. Nevertheless, the body receives an impression from the **spirit**, just as **matter** does from **form**, because they are naturally suited to act and be acted upon by each other. Thus the body **colors** <term>tingit: to "tinge" or dye; in alchemy, this refers to the "Tincture" that changes the color and internal quality of a metal.</term>, but the spirit penetrates. However, the body does not color unless it is first colored, because a thick, earthy thing cannot enter another due to its coarseness; rather, it is the thin, airy thing that enters and colors.

->LIV.<-

However, it is often asked from which things this most subtle and pure substance can best be drawn. And in answering, we say that it is drawn from those things in which it resides. For to think of extracting it from a thing in which it does not exist would be foolish and vain, as some simpletons do.

->LV.<-

Now, it exists both in bodies <note>In this context, "bodies" usually refers to solid metals.</note> and in **mercury** <term>arg. vivo: *argentum vivum*, literally "living silver" or quicksilver.</term> according to nature, since they are found to be of one nature. Therefore, the medicine of the **precious stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> (which is to be made from mercury) is sought both in bodies and in the substance of mercury itself. But it is sought in bodies with greater difficulty, and in mercury more easily and directly.

->LVI.<-

For we do not find anything more [similar] to it...

### LVII.

Indeed, we see that **quicksilver** <term>arg. vivum: literally "living silver," the common name for liquid mercury.</term> adheres more to quicksilver and is more friendly to it; after that, it joins truly to gold and silver, from which it is gathered that it is of their nature more so than others. For quicksilver does not receive anything other than what is of its own nature.

### LVIII.

And there is likewise in it an easy extraction of that most subtle substance, since it already possesses a material that is subtle in its actual state.

### LIX.

If, therefore, you know how to perfect your work through **Mercury** <term>Mercurium: here referring to the elemental principle or the purified substance used in the "Great Work."</term> alone, you will be a seeker of the most precious perfection, and of that which overcomes the work of nature.

### LX.

For you will be able to cleanse it intimately, to a point which nature could not reach. And thus, consequently, you will create from it a work that will surpass all nature, says the <term>Rosar. abbreviat. ...to itself, for the completion of both the **four imperfect bodies** <note>original: "4 imperfectorum". In alchemy, these are lead, tin, iron, and copper, which were considered "unripe" or "diseased" compared to the "perfect" gold and silver.</note> as well as of the coagulable **quicksilver** <term>arg. vivi: an abbreviation of "argenti vivi," meaning mercury.</term> itself, no other substance can be thought of or found, except for the substance of quicksilver alone, which contains within itself an **analogous red sulfur** <note>Alchemists believed metals were composed of two principles: Sulfur (the active, masculine, coloring principle) and Mercury (the passive, feminine, material principle). ### LXII.

For from whatever thing the **Elixir** <term>Elyxir: a medicinal or chemical substance capable of changing base metals into gold (red elixir) or silver (white elixir).</term> is made, whether white or red, there is nothing there but quicksilver and sulfur, of which one does nothing without the other, nor can they even exist without each other.

### LXIII.

That quicksilver truly has its own sulfur hidden within it is evident from the fact that in quicksilver alone, without the addition of any external sulfur, the same diversity occurs in the **making of the medicine** <note>the "Medicine" refers to the Alchemical Stone or Elixir.</note> as occurs in the **generation of metals** <term>metallorum generatione: the natural process by which metals were thought to grow like plants within the earth's crust.</term>. For just as in mines, if sulfur of an improper disposition falls upon the quicksilver, it must necessarily lead to various corruptions <note>The author is explaining why "base" or "imperfect" metals like lead or iron exist; they are the result of "impure" sulfur mixing with mercury in the earth.</note>: so too in the Art, whatever the **cleansing** <term>mundatio</term> and **preparation** <term>præparatio</term> of the quicksilver may be, such will be the perfection that follows through it in its **projection** <note>The final stage of the Great Work, where a tiny amount of the Elixir is "projected" or cast upon a molten base metal to transform it.</note> upon each of the imperfect bodies.

### LXIV.

Therefore, in quicksilver alone the entire metallic nature rests, because a metallic body is nothing other than quicksilver changed and coagulated from its own nature, and according to the [nature] of sulfur...

### LXV.

However, whatever has been said so far concerning **quicksilver** <term>argento vivo: literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</term>, and its wonderful power in performing the miracles of the **one Hermetic thing** <note>original: "rei unius Hermeticæ." This refers to the Emerald Tablet's description of the "miracles of one thing," which is the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, take care that you do not understand it to mean that common stuff, in that foul form and condition in which it comes out of the **apothecary's drawers** <note>original: "officinarum loculis." In the early modern period, an *officina* was a pharmacy or workshop where raw minerals were sold.</note>; rather, understand it as that which has been refined by the industrious hand of an ingenious philosopher.

### LXVI.

For **quicksilver** is not *perfecting in its own nature*—that is, the state to which its own mines brought it—but rather that which is produced from it through our artifice. It is not, I say, the material of our **medicine** <note>the "Medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher’s Stone.</note> *in its entire substance, but it is a Part of it*, or rather, a material which took its origin from that substance and was created out of it. For quicksilver contains within itself an **internal spirit** <note>original: "spiritum internum." Alchemists believed that within the material body of mercury lay a spiritual, energetic force that performed the actual transformation.</note>, living and converting; if this spirit is moved from potentiality into action, the philosophers recognize it as their own **Mercury** <term>Mercurio: Alchemists distinguished between "vulgar mercury" (the liquid metal) and "Philosophical Mercury" (the secret essence extracted from it).</term>. **Anonymous**. See the notes in **Bernhardus**. <note>Likely referring to Bernhardus Trevisanus, a famous 15th-century alchemist.</note>

### LXVII.

For quicksilver has in its nature two causes of corruption: one, an **earthy, foul, sulfurous substance** <note>Alchemists believed raw mercury was "dirty" because it was mixed with "combustible sulfur" and "earth," which prevented it from being pure enough for the Work.</note>, yet without flammability; the other, a **superfluous ingrained wateriness** <note>original: "superfluam insitivam aqueitatis substantiam." Mercury was viewed as "water that does not wet the hands," but the excess of this cold, wet quality had to be removed.</note>. The former prevents the **ingression** <term>ingressionẽ: the ability of the alchemical medicine to penetrate and "enter" a base metal to transform it.</term>, and likewise makes the color imperfe-

<header>APHORISMS</header>

...defective and leaden <note>original: "lividum." In alchemical color theory, a leaden or dull gray color indicated impurity or "death" in the metal, often associated with lead or slag.</note> color during **projection** <term>projection: the final step of the alchemical process where the "Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone is cast onto molten base metals to transform them into gold or silver.</term>, which inevitably infects all its coagulations. This makes the entire **medicine** fugitive <note>original: "fugitivam." In alchemy, a "fugitive" substance is volatile; it evaporates or escapes when heated. The goal of the Great Work was to make the substance "fixed" or stable under fire.</note> during projection.

### LXVIII.

It is therefore not possible in this work to follow nature naturally (in the sense that we would simply take that **quicksilver** <term>arg. viv. (argentum vivum): literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</term> just as it has been coagulated by nature); rather, it must be perfected through our own artifice and ingenuity. For in external things there is corruption, but in the internal parts there is perpetuity.

### LXIX.

For since these superfluous qualities have come upon it accidentally, rather than being part of its essential root <note>original: "radicaliter." Alchemists distinguished between "accidental" impurities (external dirt or mixed minerals) and the "radical" or "root" essence of the substance which remains after purification.</note>, the stripping away of these accidents is possible. The substance of the quicksilver and the **radical sulfur** remains, and this is found to be of greater clarity, purity, and brilliance than those very bodies naturally perfected in the **mine**.

### LXX.

If, therefore, we wish to create the **medicine** from it, it is necessary: 1. To cut away the superfluous things—that is, to purge it thoroughly from the dregs of its earthiness—so that it does not produce a leaden color during projection. Likewise, one must destroy its fugitive wateriness, so that it does not cause the entire medicine to be fugitive during projection.

### LXXI.

2. Afterward, one must divide or separate its middle substance, which is the cause of perfection in both bodies and spirits, and...

<summary>This page argues that natural mercury cannot be used in alchemy without refinement because its "accidental" impurities—earthiness and wateriness—render the final medicine unstable. The text claims that through philosophical artifice, a "radical" substance can be extracted that is purer than anything found in nature.</summary>

# GEBERIAN.

...whose property it is not to be burned, but to defend against burning, and which similarly does not flee, but makes things fixed <note>original: "fixum." In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand the intense heat of a furnace without evaporating or changing state.</note>.

->LXXII.<-

For it is a metallic, viscous, and **radical humidity** <term>radical humidity: the "root moisture" or essential liquid life-force of a metal, believed by alchemists to be the source of its growth and its ability to be melted without being destroyed.</term> which is above all other humidities. It awaits the heat of the fire, overcoming it, resting amicably within it, and even rejoicing in it. For this reason, it is everywhere called the **Salamander** <note>In medieval folklore, the Salamander was a mythical lizard-like creature believed to be able to live inside fire and even extinguish flames with its cold body.</note> by the philosophers.

->LXXIV.<-

This artifice is most studiously hidden by all philosophers. For it is the secret of the art, and the primary key. Concerning this, therefore, you should hear all of them thundering with one voice: "We have hidden nothing from you regarding this **mastery** <note>original: "magisterio." The "Magistery" or "Great Work" refers to the entire process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> except the secret of the art, which it is not permitted to reveal, for one would be cursed for the revelation of the secret."

...these things; namely, in sulfur and arsenic closely: but more closely and more perfectly in quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum." Common mercury.</note>. For we do not see the humidity of these things leave their earth after dissolution <note>original: "resolutionem." The process of breaking a substance down into its component parts.</note>, because of the strong union they possessed in the natural work of mixing. [From this, learn that Mercury is not to be dissolved into water and despoiled of its virgin earth. For without this earth, no coagulation <term>coagulation: the thickening or hardening of a substance; in alchemy, it refers to turning a "spirit" or liquid into a solid "body."</term> of the body and spirit would occur. ### LXXVI.

That quicksilver has a viscous and dense substance is signaled by its grinding <note>original: "contritio." The physical process of rubbing or pounding materials together to mix them.</note>, along with its absorption <note>original: "imbibitione." The alchemical process where a solid material "drinks" or soaks up a liquid.</note> and mixing with other things. For its viscosity is clearly felt within it through its great adherence.

### LXXVII.

Furthermore, some have thought it impossible to divide this middle substance because of its strong mixing and natural union. For quicksilver (and some say sulfur) is of a uniform substance and a most powerful composition. Therefore, for no causes of corruption does it allow itself to be divided into its composite parts; instead, it either remains standing in the fire with its entire substance, or it withdraws from the fire. Since, therefore, this depends upon the most subtle artifice <term>artifice: the secret, skilled chemical manipulation or "work" performed by the alchemist.</term>, most have thought that it would be impossible. See Aphorism 74.

### LXXVIII.

Nevertheless, since we have found that it does not change its nature without the application of some alteration, we have likewise found that it must necessarily be prepared, since it cannot be mixed thoroughly <note>original: "in profundo," literally "in the depths," meaning a total molecular or internal union.</note> with other metallic bodies without its proper preparation.

### LXXIX.

For it does not illuminate <note>In alchemy, "to illuminate" often refers to giving a base metal the "light" or color of gold.</note> unless the brightest substance is drawn out from it, by the skill of its own specific method and by the manner of its operation through a suitable fire; nor does its impression <note>The lasting change or "stain" the substance leaves on another metal.</note> remain unless it is fixed <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance, like mercury, stable and solid so it does not evaporate when heated.</term>.

### LXXX.

Therefore, it is clear from this that such a preparation should be applied and administered to it, so that the brightest and purest substance may be created from it. Then, indeed, it should be fixed with caution, so that <term>arg. viv.</term> <note>original: "argentum vivum," or quicksilver (mercury).</note> may be worked with skill—both in the administration of fire and in the manner of its fixation—by which the humidity may be removed from it to such an extent that it suffices to complete a perfect fusion <term>fusion: the state of being melted or ready to flow and mix.</term>. This most subtle and pure essence is seen most clearly in gold, which is fashioned from it.

...is done through **sublimation**, if one is negligent, for the perfection that follows will only be as good as the cleansing itself. Hardly anyone has explained this entire matter more openly and clearly than **Ewaldus Vogelius** <note>A pseudonym for the 16th-century alchemist Theobald van Hoghelande, who wrote extensively on the pitfalls of the art.</note>, who in his *Golden Book on the Conditions of the Physical Stone* writes as follows: "The preparation of this spirit is its refinement <note>original: "subtiliatio"</note>, which is perfected through multiple **distillations**, until it has achieved brilliance and crystalline serenity." When the philosophers require "crystalline transparency," they do not want it in *actuality* <term>actuality: "actu," referring to a physical state you can see and touch.</term>, but in *potential* <term>potential: "potentia," referring to an inner capacity or a state the matter is capable of reaching.</term>. For while Mercury can be turned into such a liquid, that is not what is sought in this art. Therefore, it ought not be transparent in any other way than as a **pure celestial mirror**. Yet such a mirror is not so transparent that it allows images to pass through it; if it did, it would not be a mirror at all, since a mirror’s job is to stop images and reflect them back. Therefore, such "transparency" is nothing other than a certain supremely pure brilliance.

Hence the book called *The Golden Halo* <note>original: "Aureola," likely the "Aureoli de duplicis ortu," a common alchemical text.</note> warns: "One must not work toward the transparency of Mercury"—that is, trying to reduce it into a clear, see-through water, as many **cooks** <note>original: "coqui," a derogatory term used by philosophers for alchemists who worked blindly with heat without understanding the theory.</note> do. Because if Mercury were reduced into such water, it would be "unextinguishable" for our work; it would neither be **fixed** <term>fixed: The process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</term> nor receive any **color**. Lully <note>Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval alchemy.</note> says in his *Codicil*: "The water is distilled until the sign of perfect washing is reached, which is a transparent and shining brilliance, like the serenity of crystal." He who...

...[if] it reaches the sign, one possesses the **Mercury of the Philosophers**, which dissolves all bodies, especially those of the **Sun** <note>In alchemy, the Sun (Sol) represents Gold.</note> and the **Moon** <note>The Moon (Luna) represents Silver.</note>, because of their proximity of nature. For this reason, the subtlety of this spirit more easily penetrates their density, and entering the innermost parts of the bodies, softens them, and finally converts them into running Mercury, provided a suitable **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: "putrefactio," a process of breaking down and softening a substance through gentle heat, often the first step in a transformation.</term> has preceded. The second is done with rest, and without any labor (of which **Bernhard** <note>Likely Bernhard of Trevisan, a legendary 15th-century alchemist famous for his allegorical parables.</note> speaks in his parable when he says he did not have much labor), and it is the second stage of the work, which is achieved through **fixation** <term>fixation: "fixio," the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat.</term> and the **permanence** of that which has been sublimated and cleansed. For then the agent, when it introduces the form, puts an end to the work, and rests and takes delight."

Mercury, both Solar <note>original: "Solarem"; referring to Gold.</note> and Lunar <note>original: "Lunarem"; referring to Silver.</note>. **Arnold** <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> says in his *Mirror* <note>original: "in speculo"; likely referring to the *Speculum Alchimiae* (The Mirror of Alchemy).</note>: The entire mastery consists of one single thing, and that single thing is perfect for both the **white** and the **red** <note>The *Albedo* (whiteness) and *Rubedo* (redness) are the final stages of the Great Work, representing the creation of silver and gold respectively.</note>.

### LXXXVII.

For if you clean and perfect it through direct **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the "spirit" of the metal.</term>, it will be a true and firm tincture of whiteness, to which none is equal. Note therefore, as **Arnold** well advises, that the **Lunar tincture** is made from Mercury alone by itself. And, according to **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and influential philosopher-scientist.</note>, the ferment is taken from the same thing; which indeed is nothing else (in this operation at least) than Mercury cooked through **drinking** and **feeding** <term>drinking and feeding: "potatio & cibatione," metaphorical terms for the rhythmic addition of liquid and solid materials to the alchemical vessel to "nourish" the growing Stone.</term>, as the **Anonymous** author says.

### LXXXVIII.

There is also a tincture of redness, of most exuberant perfection and shining brilliance, and it does not depart from the mixture as long as it exists.

### LXXXIX.

But so that it may create a **lemon-yellow** <note>original: "citrinum"; the *Citrinitas* or yellowing was often seen as a transitional stage between the white and red works.</note> color, it is not completed without the addition or mixing of a tinting thing, which is of its own nature. For since its **sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle, while Mercury represents the passive, fluid, and feminine principle.</note> is too tender and weak, it does not suffice unless another sulfur is added. See **Bonus** and **Libavius** <note>Andreas Libavius (1555–1616), a German doctor and chemist known for writing the first systematic chemistry textbook.</note>.

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>OF GEBER <note>original: "GEBRIANI"; likely referring to the school of thought or followers of Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the father of chemistry.</note></header>
<sig>M 4</sig>

then from dry water or Mercury; but it is pure fire hidden in Mercury, as Bernhard <note>original: "Bernh."; referring to Bernard of Treviso (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist.</note> says. For this substance has within itself its own sulfur, by which it is coagulated into gold. Since, however, that sulfur is imperfect and is worked upon by nature over a very long time, the philosophers—in order to shorten the time—add perfect sulfur to it, like a masculine seed, so that the sulfur of the quicksilver <note>original: "arg. vivi"; abbreviation for "argentum vivum," or mercury.</note> may be perfected by it. And thus, what nature could scarcely provide in 1000 years, art, working with nature, completes in a year. For the generation of the Stone, therefore, two seeds or sulfurs are required: the feminine and the masculine. The former is implanted by nature in raw and imperfect Mercury; the latter in cooked and perfect sulfur. From the former, when rightly prepared, is drawn forth the Little Fountain <note>original: "Fontina"; a symbolic bath used for the purification of the matter.</note> in which the metallic King washes; or the Azoth <term>Azoth: the "universal medicine" or "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, often symbolized as the mercury of the philosophers.</term>, which washes the latten <term>latonem: an alchemical term for an unrefined metallic body, often compared to brass, which must be cleansed during the "whitening" stage.</term> with the help of fire. Hermes expressed and represented this whole matter, as if in living colors, in his tablet, which is read in these words according to Bernhard, book 1:

> The matter is true, most certain, and without fallacy: that which is sublime is from the nature of the lowest, and that which ascends is of the nature of that which descends. Join them in one way and by one disposition. The red Sun is the father, and the white Moon is the mother, and fire is the ruler <note>original: "regimen"; referring to the governance or controlled application of heat.</note>. Make the thick subtle, and the subtle thick, and so you shall have the glory of the world, and everything you have desired.

...lius, <note>likely referring to Heinrich Nolle (c. 1582–1626), a German physician and hermetic philosopher.</note> in these words: Mercury and gold differ only accidentally <note>In the philosophy of the time, an 'accident' is a property (like color or state) that does not change the essential nature of the substance.</note>: For among metallic bodies gold is supremely pure and supremely digested; while quicksilver <note>original: "arg. vero vivum"; an abbreviation of *argentum vivum*.</note> is impure and supremely undigested among the same. Hence it is called the lower body; the former, the upper. But that the upper is like that which is lower, and the lower is like that which is upper: Hermes, the father of philosophers, taught this clearly enough in his Emerald Tablet. <note>The *Tabula Smaragdina*, a foundational text of alchemy attributed to the mythical Hermes Trismegistus, containing the famous maxim "As above, so below."</note>

### XCIV.

The sum, therefore, of the intention of the entire work, scattered through various chapters, is this: 1. That the stone known in the chapters be taken—that is, Mercury, according to the interpretation of Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and polymath who was one of the first medieval scholars to apply Aristotelian philosophy to the study of nature.</note>. 2. That upon it (enclosed in a glass vessel with a blind alembic <term>blind alembic: a distilling apparatus without an outlet, used to circulate vapors back into the liquid rather than collecting them elsewhere.</term>) the labor of the first degree of sublimation be applied with persistence, so that the stone (Mercury) may be most perfectly cleansed, whether alone for the lunar tincture <note>The 'lunar tincture' refers to the preparation used to transform base metals into silver.</note>, or with its additive for the solar tincture <note>The 'solar tincture' refers to the preparation used to transform metals into gold.</note>, until it arrives at the ultimate purity of subtlety and finally becomes volatile. 3. That the volatile be fixed by methods of fixation, until it rests in the harshness of the fire. 4. That the fixed be made volatile again by methods of sublimation, and the volatile be made fixed once more, until it flows and transforms in a certain sun-making and moon-making completion. For from the repetition of the preparation results a variety of multiplication, goodness, and transformation, etc. Hence a certain author says: O, how much hope is in the repetition of the operation—

<meta>catchword: tera- (referring to "reiteratione" on the next page)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the essential unity of mercury and gold based on the Emerald Tablet and outlines a four-step alchemical process: cleansing mercury, fixing it, and repeatedly volatilizing and fixing it again to achieve the final tincture.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Gold, Emerald Tablet, Hermes Trismegistus, Albertus Magnus, Sublimation, Fixation, Great Work, Tincture</keywords>

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>OF GEBER</header>
<sig>M 5</sig>

...reiterations! For every reiteration makes the operation more sublime <note>original: *sublimiorem*; in alchemy, this suggests both the physical act of rising as vapor and an increase in spiritual or material purity.</note>.

**Note.** All the philosophers write that the entire preparation and compounding of the stone consists in **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it becomes vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid; it was seen as the primary way to "spiritualize" matter</term> alone. Truly, it is twofold: **Common**, which he described above in its proper place; and **philosophical**, which is specific to this section and serves both for cleansing and perfecting Mercury. 1. For when Mercury is prepared through the fire of its own endurance <note>original: *ignem suæ tolerantiæ*; this refers to the specific degree of heat a substance can withstand without being "burnt" or destroyed.</note>, then from the continuity of the fire, the natures are transformed within it, and the more subtle part is separated into the upper vessel, so that the entire purest substance of Mercury is raised up, leaving behind the earthiness, which must be cast away as dregs <note>original: *fex*</note> at the bottom. And following such a method of working, we add nothing to our stone, nor do we take anything away, but only remove the **superfluities**. 2. Mercury, having been purified in this way and placed in a new glass [vessel], further experiences the powers of sublimation, which then is nothing other than **refinement** <note>original: *subtiliatio*; making a substance "subtle" or rare.</note>, and it encompasses all other operations under itself, as **Arnold** <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> testifies in his *Mirror* <note>original: *Speculum Alchimiae*; a highly influential alchemical treatise attributed to Arnold.</note>: *Know that all operations, namely putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the breaking down or "death" of the material, often turning it black</term>, solution <term>solution: dissolving a solid into a liquid</term>, calcination <term>calcination: reducing a metal to a powder through heat</term>, coagulation <term>coagulation: the thickening of a liquid into a solid</term>, and fixation <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates</term>, are contained in sublimation alone, and are performed in one vessel, and not in multiple vessels, just as there are seven operations within sublimation alone, which we have set down in our book, etc.* For although Mercury, whether natural or derived from perfected bodies <note>In alchemy, "perfected bodies" usually refers to gold or silver.</note>, is of the strongest composition, 

<meta>catchword: nevertheless</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the "Great Work" of alchemy is achieved through the process of sublimation, which cleanses Mercury of its earthy impurities. Citing Arnold of Villanova, the author argues that all other alchemical stages (like rotting and dissolving) are actually contained within this single process if performed correctly in one vessel.</summary>

-> # A P H O R I S M S <-

...yet through the constant continuation of the fire, one part is always separated from another through refinement <note>original: *subtiliationem*; the process of making a substance "subtle" or less dense</note>, in the manner of a vapor. And because this vapor cannot escape from the vessel, it thickens <note>original: *inspissatur*</note> and descends again. By means of the proper governance of the fire—namely, a slow heat, as the intention of the operation requires—the vapor then carries up another, more refined part with it, until the whole work is completed. 

And all these things are performed with one stone, one vessel, and one fire; this includes the very reiteration of the sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it</term> which our Master urges. For it should be a doubt to no one that when a part has been thus fixed <term>fixed: rendered solid and resistant to fire</term> and calcined <term>calcined: reduced to a powder through heat</term> by the "aerial fire" through sublimation alone, that same part (on account of the many parts not yet fixed) is refined, fixed again, and sublimated again. 

Regarding the precise intention of this book, Albertus Magnus <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and bishop; though a theologian, many alchemical texts were attributed to him in the middle ages.</note> discusses this excellently in *The Concordance of Philosophers*. To which Thomas Aquinas <note>The most famous student of Albertus Magnus; like his teacher, several alchemical works were falsely or truly attributed to him.</note> bears witness: "Our Stone (that is, Mercury), when it has been once dissolved into its first nature and its first water—also called 'virgin's milk' <note>original: *lac virginis*; a symbolic term for a white mercurial solvent</note> or 'Dragon's blood' <note>original: *sanguinem Draconis*; likely referring to a deep red stage of the material or a specific mineral acid</note>—then the stone itself calcines, sublimates, distills, leads, washes, and coagulates itself. By the power of a proportional fire, it perfects itself in a single vessel without any further manual operation by the chemist." 

And this is the primary intention of the philosophers who write the truth in this art, according to Albertus. However, this way is truly long and tedious, and is scarcely completed in two or three years.

<summary>This page describes the "one-vessel" method of alchemy, where the material undergoes a self-refining cycle of vaporization and condensation. It cites Albertus Magnus and Thomas Aquinas to emphasize that once the process begins, the "Stone" perfects itself through internal "manual" operations without human interference.</summary>

### OF GEBER <note>original: *GEBRIANI*; refers to the teachings of Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy.</note>

Hence the Phoenix <note>A mythical bird often used in alchemy to represent the "Red Work" or the final stage of transformation and rebirth.</note> says: Let the fire be continued always without separation, until the quicksilver <note>original: *arg. v.* or *argentum vivum*; common mercury</note> is dry, which will happen in two years. And Lullius <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and writer to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note> states: The entire course of nature is two years. Therefore, patience and delay are necessary in our work. Yet there are also other ways tending toward the same effect; however, one is nearer and more perfect than the other, as our Master says.

->X C V.<-

And so, by this order, the most precious secret <term>arcanum: a deep mystery or "inner secret" of nature known only to initiates</term> is completed—a secret which is above every other secret of the sciences of this world, and is an incomparable treasure. And indeed, you should apply yourself to it with the greatest persistence of labor and with a vast duration of immense meditation. For with these things you will find it, and without them, you will not.

->X C V I.<-

But this also must be added, as our Master warns: in the composition of the stone, the craftsman ought to set before himself the structure of natural gold as a kind of model, so that whatever he wishes to alter, he alters in imitation of this.

->X C V I I.<-

For just as (1) gold is a yellow <note>original: *citrinum*; a lemon-yellow color associated with the "citrinitas" stage of the Great Work</note> body, mixed equally by nature, united, and digested in the belly of the earth: so whoever thoroughly turns any metal yellow <note>original: *citrinat*; to turn a substance the color of citron or gold</note> and brings it to equality, makes gold from every kind of metal.

(2) Just as gold, from the most subtle and purest—

[the] substance consists of Mercury and sulfur; so in the making of the stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note>, there is nothing except quicksilver <note>original: *arg. viv.* or *argentum vivum*; liquid mercury</note> and sulfur sufficiently and legitimately purified.

3. Indeed, in gold there is a greater quantity of quicksilver than of sulfur; thus you should strive, dearest son, in all your works, that the quicksilver exceeds the sulfur in the mixture.

4. Just as gold in its first creation had subtle and fixed parts, from the condensation of which it received weight: In the same way, the stone is composed of the fixed <note>Substances that resist the action of fire and do not evaporate.</note> and the volatile <note>Substances that easily vaporize or pass away in the heat.</note>.

5. Just as through much and tempered decoction <term>decoctio: the process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or mature it</term> in the mine, there occurred a gradual resolution and a good thickening in the final mixture, so that it might melt when ignited: By a similar reasoning, Mercury is fixed through successive and repeated sublimation <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify and "fix" the mercury</term> with a tempered fire, and it gives a good metallic fusion.

## XCVIII.

Furthermore, it is necessary that the Medicine or the perfect physical tincture <note>Also known as the Elixir, the substance used to "tinge" or transform base metals.</note> be marked by seven properties, the first of which is mineral oiliness, giving a sudden and universal fusion during projection <term>projection: the final stage of the alchemical process where the "Stone" is cast upon a molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver</term>.

2. The thinness of the matter or its spiritual subtlety <note>In alchemy, "spiritual" refers to the refined, gas-like, or volatile state of a substance rather than a religious quality.</note>, which allows for entry and penetrates into the depths of the thing to be transformed.

3. The affinity between the Elixir and the thing being trans-

### X C I X.

Finally, the perfect medicine or <term>Lapis</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> must be examined to see whether it is truly complete and whether it performs a legitimate **projection** <note>The final stage where the Stone is cast upon base metals to transform them</note> or not. This is done through the **docimastic** <term>docimastica: the art of testing or assaying the purity of metals</term> arts, of which nine are listed: **Cupellation** <note>original: *Cineritium*; the process of refining gold or silver in a cupel or hearth made of bone-ash</note>, **cementation** <term>cementum: a technique for purifying gold by heating it with "cements" such as salt or brick dust</term>, **ignition**, **fusion**, **exposure over sharp vapors** <note>such as vinegar or mineral acids</note>, the **mixing of burning sulfur**, **quenching**, the **repetition of calcination and reduction**, and the **easy or difficult reception of quicksilver** <note>original: *arg. vivi*; liquid mercury</note>.

# APHORISMI
<note>Aphorisms: concise statements of scientific or philosophical principles.</note>

...[condemned] and outlawed, attributed by the most learned **Libavius** <note>Andreas Libavius (c. 1550–1616), a German physician and chemist who wrote the first systematic textbook on chemistry.</note>, in the *Syntagma*, book 7, chapter 27, concerning **sublimated quicksilver** <note>original: *arg. vivo sublimato*; mercury that has been heated into vapor and then cooled back into a solid state</note>, which begins in this manner:

Many things in the **chemical art** <term>ars chymica: the study of the composition of substances and their transformations</term> are worthy of admiration, from which the wisdom, power, and glory of God shine forth remarkably, and the internal face of nature around us and its hidden power can be observed. But among all things, there is scarcely a work of art more wonderful than that which we now undertake to treat (namely, **sublimation**) on account of its forces, effects, and singular composition. 

In this process, that **living, heavy water** <note>original: *aqua illa viva*; a poetic name for mercury or quicksilver</note>, which is friendly to metals but impure by itself—indeed, that marvelous wind which has nothing comparable to it in the mineral realm—is transmuted into a stone, as if it had looked upon a **Gorgon** <note>A reference to the mythical creature whose gaze turned men to stone; here used to describe the sudden solidification of mercury vapor</note>. 

Yet, it retains its original weight while becoming a stone which, in its purity and transparency, rivals any crystal, but in its power finds nothing comparable to itself. It is the supreme poison of life after it has been **slain** <note>original: *interfectus*; in alchemy, to 'slain' or 'kill' a substance means to change its state, such as turning liquid mercury into a solid salt</note>, but it also carries within itself no less a supreme remedy against poisons; and so it can even become the sole remedy for all curable diseases. A fire is hidden within this stone, a marvelous administrator of marvelous works. Though all things might seem to have been said regarding its power, yet far more still remains; and I am persuaded that in this life all its properties can neither be explored nor described...
